It is not endorsed by the podcast, its host or its guest. The sources discussed look directly, or as closely as possible, at statements made in the episode. Reporting on each topic in full would need a far wider search and review than a report of this scope allows. The literature searches were completed in November 2026, and later findings may change some of the conclusions. Each statement was checked against peer-reviewed studies, systematic reviews and meta-analyses found through searches of the medical and scientific literature, with priority given to human studies and to the most recent reviews. Official statistics and statements from health agencies, government bodies and research organisations were used for figures such as case numbers, trial details and the status of medicines. These are listed in the references alongside the studies. Studies that have not yet been peer reviewed are identified as such in the text. NOT MEDICAL ADVICE The views quoted in this report are those of the speakers, taken from the episode. The discussion of research that follows each quote is for general information only. Nothing here should be used in place of professional medical advice, diagnosis or treatment. If you have a health concern, or are considering any medicine, supplement or change in diet mentioned in these pages, please speak to a qualified healthcare professional. FOUND AN ERROR? References 01 The State of Alzheimer’s Dr Perlmutter opens with a hopeful message: Alzheimer’s is largely preventable, current treatments do little, and the field has been looking in the wrong place. Much of the dementia risk in a population is linked to factors people can change, and the drugs now available offer modest benefits. Several of the figures he gives, including the number of Americans affected and where Alzheimer’s ranks as a cause of death, differ from the official estimates. Can Alzheimer’s Be Prevented? ““What we really understand now, in fact the greatest development in neuroscience, is that our lifestyle choices are really the architects of our brain’s destiny… And it is significantly preventable. That’s the message that I’ve got to get out.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 7 IN SHORT A large share of dementia is linked to risk factors people can change, and some randomised trials show small benefits. Risk can be lowered, but no trial has yet shown that Alzheimer’s itself can be prevented on a large scale. A sizeable share of dementia cases can be linked to risk factors that are, at least in principle, modifiable. This evidence comes mainly from observational studies, which follow large groups of people and record who develops dementia. Studies of this kind can show that a risk factor is linked to dementia, but not that removing it would prevent the disease, because people with and without a risk factor often differ in other ways too.1 How large that share is depends on which factors are counted and how the overlap between them is handled. A 2024 meta-analysis of these estimates found that seven classic factors together accounted for about 32% of cases once overlap was taken into account. Without that adjustment the figure rose to 55%, because the same person can have several risk factors at once. The largest single contributors were low education (9.3%), physical inactivity (7.3%), hearing loss (7.2%), high blood pressure (7.1%) and obesity (5.3%).1 The 2024 Lancet Commission went further, estimating that 45% of dementia cases could in theory be delayed or prevented by addressing 14 risk factors.2 Not all of these are lifestyle choices. A 2024 commentary grouped them as educational (low level of education), cardiovascular (high blood pressure, obesity, high LDL cholesterol and diabetes), sensory (hearing loss and vision loss), lifestyle (smoking, physical inactivity and excessive alcohol consumption), psychological (depression and social isolation) and environmental (traumatic brain injury and air pollution).3 These are population models built on observational data. They describe how much dementia is statistically linked to each factor, and they cannot say that 45% of individual cases THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 8 would be avoided. Estimates also differ between populations. In a 2026 US study of 83,370 adults aged over 50, the share of dementia linked to 12 of the 14 factors, with each factor adjusted for the others, was 11.1%, compared with the Commission’s 45.2%.4 The stronger test is a randomised controlled trial, in which people are assigned by chance to an intervention or a comparison group, so that the groups differ only in the intervention. Several such trials have tested lifestyle programmes, and their results are more modest:5 FINGER (Finland): 1,260 adults aged 60 to 77 at higher risk of dementia were randomised to two years of diet advice, exercise, brain training and management of heart risk factors, or to general health advice. The programme group did slightly better on tests of thinking.6 The trial was not designed to measure new cases of dementia. Maintain Your Brain (Australia): 6,104 adults aged 55 to 77 were randomised to online coaching covering similar areas, or to information only, for three years. The programme produced a small benefit on tests of thinking.7 It also did not measure new cases of dementia. US POINTER: 2,111 adults aged 60 to 79 at higher risk were randomised to a structured lifestyle programme or a self-guided version for two years. Both groups improved, and the structured group improved slightly more on tests of thinking.8 There was no group that received no programme, so the trial shows that more support helps a little, not how much either programme helps compared with doing nothing. It did not measure new cases of dementia either. LatAm-FINGERS (Latin America): 1,065 adults aged 60 to 77 at higher risk, in 11 countries, were randomised to a structured lifestyle programme or to health advice for two years. Thinking scores rose in both groups, and rose more in the structured group, • • • • THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 9 by 0.11 standard deviations a year.9 It did not measure new cases of dementia. MAPT (France and Monaco): 1,680 adults aged 70 or over with memory complaints or early frailty were randomised to a group programme of exercise, brain training and nutrition advice, omega-3 supplements, both, or a placebo, for three years. None of these slowed decline in thinking compared with placebo.10 preDIVA (Netherlands): whole GP practices were randomised, so that 3,526 adults aged 70 to 78 received either six years of nurse- led care for heart risk factors or usual care. After a median of 6.7 years, dementia developed in 7% of both groups.11 The clearest result so far comes from treating high blood pressure with medicines. In a trial in rural China, 326 villages were randomised so that 33,995 adults aged 40 or over with uncontrolled high blood pressure received either blood pressure medication, started and adjusted by trained village health workers to reach a target below 130/80 mmHg, or usual care. Over four years, the upper blood pressure reading fell by 22 mmHg more in the treated group, and dementia was less common, with a risk ratio of 0.85 compared with usual care. 12 This was a medical treatment, not a lifestyle programme. Because whole villages were assigned, and participants knew which group they were in, the trial was open label, although the people judging who had dementia did not know which group each person was in.12 In the US SPRINT trial, 9,361 adults aged 50 and over with high blood pressure and raised heart risk, but without diabetes or a previous stroke, were randomised to a target for the upper blood pressure reading of below 120 mmHg or below 140 mmHg, and treated for 3.3 years. Over a median follow-up of 7 years, the lower target did not significantly reduce probable dementia (hazard ratio 0.86, range 0.72 to 1.02), but mild cognitive impairment (0.87, range 0.76 to 1.00) and mild cognitive impairment or dementia combined (0.89, range 0.79 to • • THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 10 0.99) were less common. By the end of follow-up, cognitive status was known for 59% of those who could be contacted again.13 Treating a single risk factor has not always changed the outcome. Hearing loss is one of the largest contributors in the estimates above, 1 so the ACHIEVE trial tested whether treating it would slow decline. In this US trial, 977 adults aged 70 to 84 with untreated hearing loss were randomised to hearing aids with counselling or to health education. After three years, decline in thinking did not differ between the groups. A planned analysis found that the effect differed between the two populations recruited, older adults with more risk factors taken from an established study of heart health, and healthy volunteers, and the authors suggested that hearing aids might slow decline in people at higher risk.14 The trial measured thinking, not new cases of dementia. A 2022 review of 112 completed prevention trials found that 21 (19%) showed a clear benefit on their main outcome. Only two of these were large: Syst-Eur, which tested blood pressure lowering, and FINGER.15 These trials also have limits. They were relatively short for a disease that develops over decades. Most measured changes in thinking, not new cases of dementia. And those that did count dementia counted dementia of any cause, not Alzheimer’s specifically. The evidence does not yet show that Alzheimer’s disease itself is “significantly preventable”, and the largest trial benefits so far relate to dementia of any cause. How Many Americans Have Alzheimer’s? ““For, in my country, the 7.6 million Alzheimer’s patients, it’s not so rough for them as it is for their families, truth be known. And it is significantly preventable.” THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 11 Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The latest official figure is 7.4 million Americans aged 65 and over with Alzheimer’s dementia. The quoted 7.6 million does not match any official estimate. Each year the Alzheimer’s Association publishes an estimate of how many Americans aged 65 and over are living with Alzheimer’s dementia. The 2026 figure is 7.4 million. 16 It was 7.2 million in 2025, 17 6.9 million in 202418 and 6.7 million in 2023,19 and in 2019 the Association reported 5.8 million Americans of all ages.20 No official report gives a figure of 7.6 million. The estimates count people with the symptoms of Alzheimer’s dementia. The Association noted that if diagnosis required biomarker confirmation, the number could be up to 30% lower, because between 15% and 30% of people diagnosed on symptoms turn out not to have the brain changes of Alzheimer’s.17 Is There Really No Treatment? ““It is, I think, far easier to prevent a disease, at least as it relates to things like Alzheimer’s, than it is to treat it. We don’t have any meaningful treatment… We don’t have a treatment. Would I welcome a treatment? You bet.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT There is no cure, and today’s drugs offer modest benefits at best. But it is not accurate to say there is no treatment at all. THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 12 Several drugs are approved for Alzheimer’s, and trials show they help to a measurable degree. A 2019 meta-analysis of 36 trials found that donepezil, rivastigmine and galantamine improved thinking, daily functioning and doctors’ overall impressions compared with placebo. 21 Memantine slowed decline on some measures but not all. 21 Another meta-analysis of 54 trials concluded that memantine combined with donepezil gave better results than placebo. The clearest differences were on one measure of thinking in more severe disease and on behavioural symptoms, while the ranges for daily activities, overall impression and the other measure of thinking included no difference.22 The 2019 meta-analysis concluded that the drugs benefit thinking, but described their effect on daily functioning, behaviour and overall change as questionable.21 The newer anti-amyloid antibodies, such as lecanemab, slow decline in early Alzheimer’s by a small amount. On the scale most trials use to rate dementia severity (CDR-SB, which runs from 0 to 18), a 2026 meta-analysis of aducanumab, lecanemab and donanemab found an average benefit of 0.41 points,23 and a 2023 meta-analysis of lecanemab alone found 0.45 points. 24 These drugs also carry risks. Across the seven trials of these three drugs in the 2026 meta-analysis, brain swelling appeared on scans in about 24% of treated patients, compared with 2% of those on placebo. 23 A 2026 Cochrane review, whose meta-analysis pooled 17 trials of seven different anti-amyloid antibodies in 20,342 people with early Alzheimer’s, found little to no difference from placebo in thinking or in the severity of dementia. 25 A 2026 summary of the review reports that, across these seven antibodies, brain swelling affected 119 in every 1,000 people treated, compared with 12 in every 1,000 on placebo. 26 The 2026 meta-analysis concluded that the slowing of decline was statistically significant but may be too small for patients to notice.23 A fairer summary would be that there are treatments, but their benefits are modest and none is a cure. Dr Perlmutter’s own wording was usually “no meaningful treatment”, and later in the conversation THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 13 he added that this “is not to say that there are not some very promising things in development right now” and that “the future looks bright from that perspective”. One Immune Mechanism Behind All Dementias? ““As you look at those seemingly very disparate diagnoses [Alzheimer’s, vascular, Lewy body and frontotemporal dementia]… they’re all united by one common mechanism, and that is disruption of the brain’s immune system. That is the huge ‘aha’ in neuroscience right now.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Inflammation in the brain is involved in many types of dementia. The evidence points to several interacting processes, not a single immune cause shared by all of them. A 2025 review describes inflammation, and the brain’s own immune cells called microglia, as playing a part in Alzheimer’s, Lewy body dementia, frontotemporal dementia and dementia caused by damage to blood vessels. It described these dementias as having distinct starting points but sharing inflammatory pathways further down the line.27 In Alzheimer’s, reviews describe microglia interacting with the two hallmark proteins, amyloid and tau, and shaping how the disease develops.28,29 Genetic studies add human evidence for Alzheimer’s, although not for the other dementias. A 2021 genetic study of 1,126,563 people identified microglia, immune cells and the breakdown of proteins as relevant to late onset Alzheimer’s,30 and another 2021 study found that genetic variants linked to Alzheimer’s risk are THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 14 concentrated in stretches of DNA that are active in microglia and related immune cells.31 Saying they are all united by one mechanism goes further than the research allows. A 2025 review describes Alzheimer’s as the result of several interacting problems, involving amyloid, tau, blood vessels, energy production in cells, the cell’s waste-disposal system and inflammation.29 Inflammation is one of these problems, but it has not been shown to be the main driver in every type of dementia. Much of this evidence comes from combining genetic, post-mortem, imaging and laboratory studies. There are few human trials that test whether changing the immune response changes the course of disease. In the ADAPT trial, 2,528 adults aged 70 and over with a family history of Alzheimer’s were given the anti-inflammatory drug naproxen or celecoxib, or a placebo, for an average of two years, and neither drug improved thinking.32 In extended follow-up, an early excess of Alzheimer’s cases in the drug groups was no longer evident overall.33 In 2026, the first trial in early Alzheimer’s of an antibody designed to activate TREM2, a receptor on microglia, did not slow decline on the main dementia scale over 96 weeks in 381 people, and its most frequent side effect was brain changes resembling the swelling seen with anti-amyloid drugs.34 Is Amyloid Still the “Party Line”? ““I think still the party line is one that believes that the central player in Alzheimer’s disease is the accumulation of a protein called beta- amyloid in the brain. Get rid of beta-amyloid and problem solved. That is not reality, and it’s unfair.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 15 IN SHORT Amyloid remains central to how Alzheimer’s is diagnosed and treated. But few researchers believe that removing amyloid alone solves the problem. Amyloid is still at the heart of the field. The Alzheimer’s Association’s 2024 criteria define Alzheimer’s by its biology, and an abnormal amyloid test is enough to make the diagnosis.35 The approved antibody drugs work by clearing amyloid from the brain, and they do so effectively while slowing decline only slightly. The version Dr Perlmutter describes, “get rid of beta-amyloid and problem solved”, is not what most of the field now believes. A 2021 review describes amyloid as important but not sufficient by itself to explain the common, late-onset form of the disease. 36 The original amyloid cascade idea, in which amyloid triggers a fixed chain of events, has been revised towards models in which amyloid raises the probability of decline alongside many other factors. 36 In a 2025 study of 256 people with amyloid in the brain, assessed at a single point in time, between 31% and 36% had the combination of tau levels and symptoms that the amyloid cascade predicts. The others had either more symptoms than their brain changes would predict, which the authors attributed to other conditions, or fewer, which they described as resilience.37 The field does not treat amyloid as the only explanation. Later in the conversation, Dr Perlmutter took a similar middle position himself, saying of amyloid: “It’s not the pivotal player, but it’s important… It’s a player, there’s no question.” THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 16 A Coin Toss by Age 85? ““If you live to be age 85, your risk of developing dementia, and generally Alzheimer’s, is close to a flip of a coin. It’s close to the high 40%… [Later:] I want you to imagine… you’re 85 years of age… Half of you have now been diagnosed with dementia.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The lifetime risk of dementia from age 55 is about 42%, and higher in women and people who carry the APOE4 gene. This does not mean that half of all 85-year-olds already have dementia. A 2025 study of more than 15,000 Americans followed from middle age found that the lifetime risk of dementia between ages 55 and 95 was 42%. It was higher in women, Black adults and carriers of the APOE4 gene variant, ranging from about 45% to 60% in those groups. 38 A separate analysis of five cohorts reported a similar lifetime risk of 43% for dementia, with risk rising steeply after age 75.39 The difficulty is the image of half of all 85-year-olds already having a diagnosis. The chance of developing dementia over the rest of your life and the share of people who have it at a given age are different measures. The Alzheimer’s Association estimated that 35.8% of Americans aged 85 and over are living with Alzheimer’s dementia. 16 Prevalence does reach very high levels at the extremes of age: in one meta-analysis, about two in three centenarians had dementia.40 Studies of lifetime risk differ in how they define dementia and how long they follow people, so “risk by 85”, “risk at 85” and “prevalence at 85” should not be used as if they meant the same thing. THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 17 The Third Leading Cause of Death? ““In America, Alzheimer’s is the third leading cause of death. People don’t realise that, and it is largely preventable.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Official US statistics rank Alzheimer’s sixth, and fifth among people aged 65 and over. Rankings of third come from other ways of estimating deaths caused by dementia, not from official figures. In official US mortality statistics, which are based on death certificates, Alzheimer’s disease was the sixth leading cause of death in 2024 and the fifth among Americans aged 65 and over.16 It ranked sixth in 2019 and slipped to seventh in 2020 and 2021, when COVID-19 entered the list,41 before moving back towards sixth.17 Studies that try to count every death to which Alzheimer’s contributed give a higher ranking. One such study estimated that about 503,400 deaths among Americans aged 75 and over in 2010 could be attributed to Alzheimer’s dementia, against fewer than 84,000 listed on death certificates.42 A 2020 study of 7,342 Americans aged 70 to 99 estimated that 13.6% of deaths between 2000 and 2009 were attributable to dementia. Death certificates, which gave dementia as the underlying cause in 5.0% of deaths, underestimated its contribution by a factor of 2.7.43 A 2018 conference abstract by government statisticians, which has not been peer reviewed, also reported that dementia of all types, counted together, would have been the third leading cause of death in 2016.44 These are legitimate estimates, and they suggest the official figures understate dementia’s toll. But they measure something different, and THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 18 none of them makes Alzheimer’s disease alone the official third leading cause of death. Is Alzheimer’s a Metabolic Disease? ““We should be talking to people in their 30s and in their 40s and asking those very same questions. Alzheimer’s is primarily a metabolic condition of the brain… So when we say that Alzheimer’s is fundamentally a metabolic condition, let’s say that in the context of where the research is going.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Insulin resistance and other metabolic problems contribute to Alzheimer’s. But the disease has many causes, and the human evidence does not yet show that it is primarily metabolic. A 2024 review describes inflammation, oxidative stress and changes in insulin signalling as key processes linking diabetes and Alzheimer’s.45 A 2025 systematic review notes that these shared features have led to the concept of Alzheimer’s as “type 3 diabetes”.46 What the evidence does not show is that metabolism is the main cause, ahead of everything else. Most post-mortem studies find no link between type 2 diabetes and the amyloid plaques and tau tangles that define Alzheimer’s, and most imaging studies find no link with amyloid in the brain. 47 Diabetes is linked to a higher risk of dementia, but it is still unclear whether it causes Alzheimer’s disease itself or acts mainly through damage to blood vessels. 47 Genetic studies, which use inherited differences in the risk of diabetes to test for cause and effect, have mostly found no causal link with Alzheimer’s. 48 A meta- analysis of 8 such studies found no significant link (odds ratio 1.02, THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 19 range 1.00 to 1.04), 48 and a 2024 study using large genetic datasets found the same (0.98, range 0.95 to 1.01).49 Metabolism is a growing area of Alzheimer’s research. The human evidence does not yet show that Alzheimer’s is “primarily a metabolic condition”. What Is the AD-SMART Trial? ““There is a new trial being started right here in England called the AD- SMART trial. And the government has put up £33 million, $44 million, to begin a study of two medicines in the treatment of Alzheimer’s disease. One is called metformin, which is a diabetes pill, and the other is actually an ADHD drug called Strattera. And this is a trial of about 1,000 people. It’s going to take 18 months… your prime minister is very much in favour of it, in that his father died of dementia.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT AD-SMART is a real UK trial testing metformin and atomoxetine (Strattera). It aims to recruit 1,200 people, each taking part for 18 months. The £33 million was announced for a different initiative. AD-SMART is a large UK trial designed to test several existing drugs for Alzheimer’s at the same time, sharing one placebo group. It is funded by the National Institute for Health and Care Research. A 2026 paper describing how drugs were chosen for it ranked atomoxetine first and metformin second, and these two are the first being tested.50 According to the UK Dementia Research Institute, the trial launched in October 2026 and aims to recruit 1,200 people across more than 30 NHS sites. 51 According to its trial registration, its main outcome is the THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 20 change over 18 months.52 The £33 million that the government announced on 8 October 2026 went to the BARBARA Alliance, a separate programme to speed up dementia trials, and not to AD- SMART specifically. 53 Andy Burnham’s father, Roy, died in September 2026 after living with Alzheimer’s disease.54 Neither drug is yet proven for Alzheimer’s. In a small 12-month trial in 39 people with early memory problems, atomoxetine was safe and lowered markers of tau in spinal fluid, but it did not improve thinking or daily functioning over that time.55 For metformin, observational studies with better designs suggest that people with type 2 diabetes who take it have a lower risk of dementia, but trials are still few and small.56 Has Amyloid Research Fallen to 14%? ““For example, 10 years ago, about a third of Alzheimer’s research was really focused on what we talked about, this beta-amyloid protein. That number is down to about 14% now, in favour of looking at things like metabolism and looking at the brain’s immune system, these microglial cells.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Amyloid’s share of Alzheimer’s drug development fell from 33% of drugs in trials in 2017 to about 20% in 2026, while drugs aimed at inflammation and the immune system rose from 6% to about 20%. The current figure is about 20%, not 14%, and these counts cover drugs in trials, not research as a whole. Each year, researchers publish a review of every Alzheimer’s drug being tested in clinical trials. The 2026 review looked back over the THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 21 decade from 2017. In that time, drugs targeting amyloid fell from 33% of the drugs in trials to about 20%. Drugs aimed at inflammation and the immune system rose from 6% to about 20%, and drugs aimed at tau also rose from 6% to about 20%. 57 The shares for amyloid and for inflammation have been stable since 2022. In 2026, there were 158 drugs in 192 trials.57 Other analyses have grouped drugs differently. A 2021 analysis found that 10.7% of drugs in clinical development targeted amyloid, compared with 19.2% at the earlier, preclinical stage, and its authors concluded that a shift away from amyloid “has not materialized”.58 These reviews count drugs in trials, not money spent on research, so they cannot tell us how funding has been divided between amyloid and other areas. The Most Feared Disease? ““And you ask people, what disease do you fear most? Overwhelmingly, the answer is Alzheimer’s. It’s not cancer. It’s not heart disease. It’s not anything else.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Dementia is one of the most feared diseases, and it comes first among older people and, according to one commentary, in a recent US study. In other surveys cancer comes slightly ahead, so “overwhelmingly” goes further than the evidence. Surveys support the idea that people fear dementia deeply. In an international survey of about 70,000 people in 54 countries, almost 80% said they were concerned about developing dementia. 59 In the THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 22 United States, a 2024 commentary cited a recent study finding that Alzheimer’s had overtaken cancer as the disease Americans fear most.60 In one Australian survey of hospital outpatients, cancer was the most feared disease (34%) with dementia close behind (29%), although among people aged 65 and over dementia came first. 61 In a Japanese sample the gap was wider, with 43.8% naming cancer and 18% dementia. 62 Among Norwegians over 50, more people reported a strong fear of Alzheimer’s (39%) than of cancer (30%).63 People describe fearing dementia because of its practical, emotional and social impact, and the burden on their families. 62,64 The surveys suggest that dementia is feared most in older age groups, and possibly now in the United States as a whole. They do not show that people everywhere overwhelmingly fear it more than cancer. These surveys are mostly small samples from clinics or online panels, and the results depend on the country, the age group and how the question is asked. How Long Does Alzheimer’s Last, and What Ends a Life? “STEVEN BARTLETT “From when, you know, his behaviour changed to his passing?” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “Five years. Took about five years.” STEVEN BARTLETT “Is it the disease that ends up killing the person, or is it something else?” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “It’s the disease. Yeah. Everything ultimately just shuts down.” THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 23 IN SHORT Survival after diagnosis ranges from about 2 to 9 years, depending mainly on age and sex. Alzheimer’s is a fatal disease, but people often die from complications such as pneumonia. Dr Perlmutter was describing his father’s illness. How long people live depends heavily on their age at diagnosis. A 2025 meta-analysis covering more than 5.5 million people found that median survival after a dementia diagnosis ranged from 8.9 years in women diagnosed around 60 to 2.2 years in men diagnosed around 85. 65 People with Alzheimer’s lived about 1.4 years longer on average than those with other types of dementia. 65 In a large Taiwanese study, median survival after diagnosis was about 7.7 years, but fell to about 3 years in people aged 80 or over with three or more other illnesses.66 Alzheimer’s is a fatal disease, but death does not usually come from a general shutdown. As the disease advances, people have difficulty swallowing, move less and become vulnerable to infection. Pneumonia is a common immediate cause. In two large meta-analyses, it was involved in about a quarter to a third of dementia deaths overall, and in about half of the deaths examined at post-mortem.67,68 Survival studies usually count from diagnosis, which often comes years after the first changes in behaviour, and pneumonia is often left off death certificates. THE STATE OF ALZHEIMER’S 24 02 Exercise, Blood Sugar and the Brain Dr Perlmutter describes exercise as a multi-tool for the brain. Studies in people find that it improves insulin sensitivity and lowers inflammation, and the Framingham study he cites found a lower risk of dementia in the most active people. The idea that exercise works better than any drug, and the details of how muscles signal to the brain, have much less support. Is Metabolism Behind Other Brain Diseases Too? ““Really upstream of what makes a good brain go bad are changes in metabolism, the metabolic markers: our blood sugar, our blood pressure, our body fat, our blood lipids… if we can target our metabolism earlier in life, keep our blood sugar under control, exercise, get enough sleep, have relationships with other people… we really are creating an environment that helps us reduce the risk across the spectrum of brain degenerative issues, not just Alzheimer’s.” 25 Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Metabolic health and lifestyle are consistently linked to the risk of dementia and Parkinson’s. But metabolic syndrome is linked to dementia in general more than to Alzheimer’s specifically, and for ALS and frontotemporal dementia the evidence is more limited and mixed. For dementia, there is a link, but it is modest, and it is clearer for some types than others. Two 2025 meta-analyses, covering 4.3 million and 6.8 million people, found that metabolic syndrome was linked to a higher risk of dementia of any kind, with ratios of 1.12 and 1.11. 1,2 A ratio above 1 means a higher risk, and below 1 a lower risk. One also found a higher risk of vascular dementia (1.33), while in the other the link with vascular dementia was not statistically significant (1.40, range 0.96 to 2.06).1,2 Neither found a clear link with Alzheimer’s disease itself, and the larger meta-analysis rated the certainty of its evidence as low.1,2 For Parkinson’s disease, large studies also find links. In the UK Biobank, a study of about 467,000 adults, people with metabolic syndrome (a cluster of high blood pressure, high blood sugar, excess abdominal fat and abnormal blood fats) were more likely to develop Parkinson’s, with a hazard ratio of 1.39. 3 A Korean study of 17.2 million adults found a hazard ratio of 1.24, and each part of the syndrome was separately linked to higher risk.4 In a meta-analysis of 15 studies covering 29.9 million people, diabetes was linked to a 27% higher relative risk of Parkinson’s (RR 1.27).5 Lifestyle factors show a similar pattern. In UK Biobank, vigorous activity, less time sitting and better sleep were all linked to a lower risk of Parkinson’s, and loneliness to a higher risk over 15 years, with a hazard ratio of 1.37. 6,7 Loneliness was also linked to a higher risk of frontotemporal dementia in a study of almost half a million people.8 EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 26 For motor neurone disease (ALS) and frontotemporal dementia, the evidence is more limited and less consistent. In UK Biobank, metabolic syndrome was linked to a higher risk of ALS, with a hazard ratio of 1.50,9 but other studies have found the opposite pattern: in a summary of the research, taking diabetes medicines and a higher body weight were linked to a lower risk of ALS.10 Most of these studies are observational, so they show associations and cannot prove that changing these factors prevents disease. The evidence differs across “the spectrum” of brain diseases. Is Exercise a Multi-Tool? ““So number two: sedentary, meaning being sedentary. This is huge. Exercise and movement is a multi-tool for the brain. It targets the function of insulin. It targets inflammation. It targets mitochondrial function. It targets blood flow. These are all hugely important mechanisms in keeping the brain healthy.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““When we exercise, we reduce inflammation. Inflammation is a key mechanism that changes our brain’s immune cells from being supportive to being destructive… And exercise improves the blood going to your brain.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Exercise improves insulin sensitivity and lowers inflammation in people, and improves blood flow in parts of the brain. Direct evidence that it changes the behaviour of immune cells in the human brain is still limited. EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 27 In a German study of 21 sedentary adults with overweight or obesity, with no comparison group, eight weeks of supervised aerobic training restored the brain’s response to insulin to the level seen in people of healthy weight. Their brain scans showed stronger insulin-induced activity in the striatum, a deep brain region, and stronger connections in the hippocampus, a region central to memory, and the changes were associated with better performance on thinking tests.11 Across the body as a whole, the evidence is broader. A 2026 meta- analysis of 86 randomised trials in 4,358 adults with overweight or obesity found that aerobic, resistance, combined and high intensity interval training all reduced fasting insulin and a standard measure of insulin resistance (HOMA-IR) compared with usual care, although the certainty of the evidence was rated low to moderate.12 Exercise also lowers inflammation. In a meta-analysis of 38 trials in healthy people, regular exercise lowered three common blood markers of inflammation (IL-6, CRP and TNF-alpha), with the largest effects from moderate-intensity training kept up for more than 12 weeks. 13 Meta-analyses in older adults found the same, for aerobic, resistance and combined training.14,15 For blood flow, scans show consistent increases in specific regions, including the hippocampus, after exercise training. 16 Changes in total blood flow to the brain are less consistent, although one meta-analysis found that exercise raised the speed of blood flow in the brain’s arteries in older adults.17 The part about immune cells needs more caution. Studies report lower inflammation in the blood, but human evidence that exercise changes the activity of immune cells in the brain is limited. One imaging study in 12 pairs of twins, which measured a marker of brain immune activity, found no change after a six month exercise programme.18 Evidence on mitochondria in the brain comes mainly from animals.19 EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 28 What Did the Framingham Study Find? ““There’s an interesting study that came out in the Journal of the American Medical Association in November of 2025. The author was named Marino… people from the Framingham Heart Study… about 1,000 in each group, 4,000 total, into younger people, 25 to 44 years; middle-aged people, 45 to 64; and older, 65 to 88… in middle-aged people, those who were the most active, compared to those who were the least active, had about a 45% risk reduction for this disease, Alzheimer’s… Similar results in the older age group.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““In the older population, those who were most active and carried something called APOE4, a risk marker dramatically associated with Alzheimer’s risk, had about a 67% reduced risk of becoming an Alzheimer’s patient if they… were in the group that exercised the most, in comparison to the group that exercised the least.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The study did find that the most active people in midlife and later life had a lower risk of dementia, with similar findings for Alzheimer’s. The group sizes he gives do not match the paper. The most active older APOE4 carriers had a 66% lower risk of dementia from any cause, although the estimate was imprecise. The study he refers to was published in JAMA Network Open in November 2025 by Francesca Marino and colleagues, using data from the long-running Framingham Heart Study in Massachusetts. It found that being more active in midlife and later life was linked to a lower EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 29 risk of dementia, while activity in early adulthood was not, and the findings were similar for Alzheimer’s.20,21 In midlife, the most active fifth of people had a hazard ratio for dementia of 0.59 compared with the least active fifth, and in later life the figure was 0.55. 20 A hazard ratio below 1 means a lower risk, and these correspond to about 41% and 45% lower risk. The group sizes are different from those he gives. The groups held 1,526, 1,943 and 885 people, not about 1,000 each.20 In later life, APOE4 carriers in the most active fifth had a hazard ratio for dementia from any cause of 0.34 compared with the least active fifth (95% confidence interval 0.12 to 0.94), which corresponds to a 66% lower risk. 20 The outcome was dementia from any cause, not Alzheimer’s specifically, and the wide confidence interval shows the estimate is imprecise. In midlife, more activity was linked to lower risk only in people without APOE4.20 Other research on APOE4 and activity is mixed.22 An earlier Framingham analysis found that the benefit of activity was limited to people without APOE4, 23 and a 2025 meta-analysis of studies in Alzheimer’s found stronger protection in non-carriers.24 Like all observational studies, this one cannot show that exercise caused the lower risk. People in the early stages of dementia may also become less active, which would make activity look more protective than it is. Are Muscles a Gland? ““Your muscles are an endocrine gland… Your muscles create a suite of chemicals called myokines… they make their way to the brain. For example, they help stimulate the brain to produce something called BDNF, so it’ll grow new brain cells and nurture your neurons and nurture the connections between neurons, which are the synapses.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 30 IN SHORT Working muscles do release signalling chemicals called myokines. Animal studies suggest some reach the brain, but there is no direct human evidence that they cause the brain to grow new cells. During exercise, muscles release a range of signalling molecules known as myokines. A 2019 review lists several, including BDNF, IGF-1, irisin and cathepsin B, as possible links between exercise and brain health.25 Irisin can cross the protective barrier around the brain in animals.26 A 2026 review found that endurance, resistance and interval training raise irisin in the blood and BDNF, a protein that supports brain cells, in the hippocampus, although the hippocampus findings come mostly from animal studies, and its author concluded that causal conclusions remain premature.26 The full chain he describes has not been shown in people. A 2019 review concluded that the evidence linking these chemicals to better brain function is weaker than the evidence that exercise itself benefits the brain. 25 BDNF in the blood does not come only from muscle, and its source after exercise is still unclear. 25 Most of what is known about exercise and the growth of new brain cells comes from rodents, because the process cannot be measured directly in living human brains.27 There is some human evidence for parts of the chain. In a randomised trial of 120 older adults, aerobic training increased the size of the front part of the hippocampus by 2% and improved spatial memory, and the larger volume was associated with higher levels of BDNF in the blood.28 A 2024 meta-analysis of 35 randomised trials found that exercise training raised resting BDNF in older adults, with an effect size of 0.56, although the rise was seen in participants with health conditions and not in healthy participants.29 Neither shows that exercise makes the human brain grow new cells. EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 31 The evidence for this chain comes mainly from animals. It should not be presented as established in humans. Does HbA1c Predict Alzheimer’s? ““When we activate our muscles, we improve how insulin works… Elevated blood sugar is a powerful risk for brain degeneration. A simple blood test like the HbA1c, measuring your average blood sugar, is a powerful indicator, if it’s elevated, of risk for Alzheimer’s.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT High blood sugar and HbA1c are linked to a higher risk of dementia, mostly in people with diabetes. Calling HbA1c a powerful indicator of Alzheimer’s risk specifically goes further than the evidence. HbA1c is a blood test that reflects average blood sugar over the previous two to three months. A meta-analysis of 144 studies found that diabetes was linked to a 1.25 to 1.91 times higher risk of cognitive problems, and that prediabetes and a high HbA1c were also linked to higher dementia risk.30 Another meta-analysis covering 10.1 million people found diabetes was linked to a higher risk of dementia, with a risk ratio of 1.59.31 A ratio above 1 means a higher risk. Much of the research on HbA1c alone comes from people who already have diabetes. In a UK study of almost 458,000 people with type 2 diabetes, each 1% rise in HbA1c was linked to a higher risk of dementia, with a hazard ratio of 1.08, and HbA1c that swung up and down was also a warning sign.32 Individual studies found that risk rose with HbA1c, with hazard ratios of 1.40 to 3.88 in the highest groups. 33 When a 2025 meta-analysis pooled the three studies that could be combined, though, the result (1.18, range 0.97 to 1.45) was not statistically EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 32 significant,33 and a 2024 meta-analysis also found no significant effect of HbA1c on the onset of dementia.31 This evidence has two limitations. Most of these studies measured dementia of any cause, not Alzheimer’s specifically. And there is much less evidence in people without diabetes. In one 2013 study, 2,067 older adults were followed for a median of 6.8 years. Among the 1,835 without diabetes, higher average blood sugar, estimated from glucose and HbA1c measurements, was linked to a higher risk of dementia, with a hazard ratio of 1.18 for a level of 115 mg per decilitre compared with 100 mg per decilitre.34 A 2026 UK Biobank study of 432,887 adults, followed for a median of 13.7 years, found that prediabetes was linked to a higher risk of vascular dementia, with a hazard ratio of 1.36, and that harmful outcomes rose with HbA1c even below the level used to diagnose diabetes. 35 Vascular dementia is caused by reduced blood flow to the brain, and is not Alzheimer’s. The evidence does not show that HbA1c is a powerful indicator of Alzheimer’s specifically. Aerobic or Strength Training? ““Is it aerobics or is it strength training, resistance training? And it turns out that a recent meta-analysis asked that question and looked at a fairly large number of studies, and came to the conclusion that it’s both… If these chemicals that your muscles are making are good for your brain, the more you have of these chemicals, the better. So build muscle.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Combined training helps, but reviews do not show it beats every other approach. Different types of exercise seem to help different aspects of thinking. EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 33 Several large meta-analyses have compared types of exercise, and they do not agree on one winner. 36–39 In a 2025 meta-analysis of 58 trials in healthy older adults, resistance training produced the largest gains in overall thinking and in self-control, aerobic exercise was best for memory, and mind-body exercise such as tai chi was best for switching between tasks. 36 In people with mild cognitive impairment, a 2026 meta-analysis ranked programmes combining several types of exercise highest.37 A 2025 meta-analysis of 35 trials and 5,734 people found that combined training improved overall thinking compared with no exercise, especially in older people and in clinical groups. 40 But that meta-analysis compared combined training with control groups, not with single types of exercise. The research does not show that one approach is best for the brain overall, and these comparisons are uncertain because the trials differ so much in who took part and what they measured.39 Better Than Any Drug? “STEVEN BARTLETT “This is better than any drugs or medicine that I could possibly buy.” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “It is. And it’s interesting because what I’ve just described to you in terms of the activity of muscle activation is very similar to the drug that’s now being used in the Alzheimer’s trial right here in England… Metformin. Metformin does many things that exercise does. Exercise does more.” EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 34 IN SHORT Exercise is linked to lower dementia risk, but trials of exercise alone show modest effects. Some blood pressure drugs have stronger trial evidence for preventing dementia. In observational studies, active people are less likely to develop dementia. One meta-analysis of 29 studies found a hazard ratio of 0.72 for Alzheimer’s, which became 0.85 after adjusting for the most confounding factors, and the protection was seen only in studies with less than 15 years of follow-up. 41 A 2026 meta-analysis found a risk ratio of 0.75 for dementia overall. 42 A ratio below 1 means a lower risk. These studies cannot rule out other explanations, such as healthier people being more active to begin with. Trials show more modest results. In older adults without dementia, long-term exercise alone has had small or no effects on overall thinking, and it has not clearly reduced the number of people who go on to develop memory problems or dementia. 43 In the LIFE trial, 1,635 sedentary adults aged 70 to 89 were randomised to a 24 month programme of walking, strength and flexibility exercises, or to health education. Thinking did not differ between the groups after two years, and mild cognitive impairment or dementia developed in 13.2% and 12.1% (odds ratio 1.08, range 0.80 to 1.46), although this was an exploratory measure that the trial was not designed to test.44 Programmes that combine exercise with diet and other changes show small benefits.43 Some drugs have stronger trial evidence. A 2015 systematic review reports that in the European Syst-Eur trial, treating high blood pressure in older people reduced the risk of dementia by 55% (3.3 against 7.4 cases per 1,000 patient years), and in the PROGRESS trial, blood pressure treatment reduced cognitive decline by 19%.45 For metformin, a meta-analysis found that people with diabetes who take it had a lower risk of dementia (hazard ratio 0.76), 46 but a 2024 review EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 35 noted that trials in people without type 2 diabetes are few, small and short, although they suggest that metformin could help prevent dementia, and that results from larger trials are needed. 47 No study has compared exercise with metformin directly. The evidence does not show that it beats every drug for preventing dementia. EXERCISE, BLOOD SUGAR AND THE BRAIN 36 03 Connection and the Brain’s Immune Cells Much of Dr Perlmutter’s argument rests on microglia, the brain’s resident immune cells. He describes them as switching between a supportive state and a destructive “evil twin”, and he links that switch to social connection, blood sugar, inflammation and energy production in cells. Most of the steps in this chain have been shown in cells and animals. Few have been shown in the human brain, and the division into “good” and “bad” microglia is now considered too simple. Do Blue Zones Show the Value of Connection? ““We first got an inkling that social connectivity was good for health, good for the body and good for the brain from the Blue Zones information that looked at people who lived long and had lower risk of dementia… what we learned from the Blue Zone data is that these are people who are connected to other people… And that is good for the brain. It’s good for inflammation. It’s good for metabolism.” 37 Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT People with poor social connections are consistently more likely to develop dementia. Blue Zones have unusually long-lived populations, but there is no direct evidence that they have lower rates of dementia. Blue Zones are regions said to have unusually high numbers of very old people, such as Okinawa in Japan, Sardinia in Italy, Nicoya in Costa Rica and Ikaria in Greece. 1,2 A 2025 review found that some of these places do show higher longevity than their national averages, although the evidence is uneven and some proposed Blue Zones have not been studied well. 2 A 2025 review describes the strict procedures used to check ages, and notes that Blue Zones face criticism over the reliability of the data, particularly the accuracy of centenarians’ ages.1 What the published research does not show is that people in Blue Zones have lower rates of dementia. The studies are about how long people live, and no peer-reviewed work confirms lower dementia rates there. On social connection more broadly, large studies following people over time consistently find that weak social ties are linked to higher dementia risk. 3–5 In one meta-analysis, infrequent social contact and loneliness had risk ratios of 1.57 and 1.58. 3 In another meta-analysis, a small social network had a risk ratio of 1.59 and poor social support 1.28.4 A 2024 meta-analysis of more than 600,000 people found loneliness linked to a higher risk of dementia and of Alzheimer’s, with hazard ratios of 1.31 and 1.39. 5 A ratio above 1 means a higher risk. A 2022 study of 462,619 people found that social isolation was linked to a higher risk of dementia, with a hazard ratio of 1.26, even after allowing for loneliness and depression. Loneliness was not clearly linked once all these factors were allowed for (1.04, range 0.94 to 1.16), and 75% of its link with dementia was explained by symptoms of depression.6 These studies are observational and measure different things, from CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 38 loneliness to how often people see others, but the overall pattern is consistent. Does Connection Raise Oxytocin? ““When we connect to people and when we develop a relationship, our bodies, our brains increase their output of a chemical called oxytocin… It’s called the love hormone. But it’s more than that. This oxytocin is secreted when we’re feeling trust for another individual, when we’re feeling connected to another.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Oxytocin plays a part in social bonding in mammals. But experiments in people do not reliably show that trust or connection raises oxytocin levels. Oxytocin is involved in bonding and social behaviour across mammals, which is where the nickname “love hormone” comes from. 7 There is less evidence that connection with others raises oxytocin in people. A 2023 meta-analysis of 51 studies found that in experiments social contact did not produce the expected rise in oxytocin. In observational studies there was a small but statistically significant link between social interaction and oxytocin levels, and the clearest effects were seen in studies of interactions between parents and children. 8 Observational studies cannot show that the contact caused the change. A 2025 experiment in 30 healthy adults found that touch, from another person or from themselves, raised oxytocin in saliva, although not in women in the first half of their menstrual cycle. The study was small and was not randomised.9 CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 39 The link with trust is weaker still. Early studies reported that oxytocin given as a nasal spray made people more trusting, but a critical review concluded that this finding has not replicated well, that studies linking blood oxytocin to trust have serious methodological problems, and that genetic studies give inconsistent results. 10 Part of the problem is measurement: oxytocin in the blood, saliva or urine does not reliably reflect what is happening in the brain, and laboratories measure it in different ways.7 Oxytocin has a real but complicated role in social life. It is not a simple marker of trust or connection. Can Oxytocin Calm Microglia? ““And this oxytocin stimulates our good brain immune cells, these microglial cells, and helps keep them in their supportive, loving, nurturing role as opposed to letting them drift to becoming what I call the evil twin: increasing inflammation, increasing the destruction of our synapses.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT In cells and animals, oxytocin can calm inflammatory microglia. This has not been shown in the human brain. Laboratory studies have reported that oxytocin can reduce inflammation.11 In microglia grown in a dish, adding oxytocin before an inflammatory trigger reduced their activation and the inflammatory chemicals they released.12 Other studies have traced how it does this.13–15 In mice, oxytocin given through the nose reduced microglial activation and improved memory in a model of Alzheimer’s, and reduced brain inflammation in a mouse model of sepsis.16,17 CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 40 One study found oxytocin in microglia in human brain tissue, and showed that mouse microglia release it, but it did not show that oxytocin changes brain inflammation in people. 18 No trial was found that tested whether oxytocin protects against dementia. The language of “supportive” and “destructive” microglia is itself debated, as the next section explains. Are There Good and Bad Microglia? ““Our brain’s microglial cells can exist in a supportive, loving state. We call this the M2 configuration… They’re shoring up our synapses… They are making sure that our very valuable blood-brain barrier is working… they can shift. They can become what I call the evil twin… the M1 microglial cell… These are the cells that destroy our synapses… I fully recognise that there are countless transitional states between M2 and M1. It isn’t a binary issue.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““This shift from M2 to M1 is the fundamental upstream event in Alzheimer’s disease. These cells are sensitive to our metabolism. These cells are sensitive to inflammation.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The M1 and M2 labels are now considered too simple, because microglia take on many overlapping states. There is no evidence that a shift from M2 to M1 starts Alzheimer’s. The terms M1 and M2 were borrowed from research on immune cells in the blood, where they described inflammatory and healing states. CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 41 Many researchers now consider them a poor description of microglia.19 In 2016, Richard Ransohoff, a leading neuroimmunologist, argued that the terms should be dropped because they were slowing progress in the field. 20 In 2022, a large group of experts concluded that dividing microglia into “good” and “bad” does not fit what modern techniques show, which is a wide range of states that change with age, location and disease. 19 A 2026 review in Nature Reviews Neuroscience reached the same conclusion. 21 On the blood-brain barrier, a 2020 review that used the M1 and M2 labels described inflammatory microglia as contributing to leaks in the barrier, and anti- inflammatory microglia as protecting it.22 Dr Perlmutter acknowledges that it is not a simple switch, which is closer to current thinking. Later in the conversation he also said he wanted to “be a little kinder to our M1 microglia”, because “there’s a time and a place” for them after an injury or infection. No study was found showing that this shift is the “fundamental upstream event” in Alzheimer’s. The classic amyloid cascade model places amyloid build-up at the start of a fixed chain of events, followed by tau build-up and the loss of nerve cells. 23 A 2025 review describes amyloid, tau and inflammation as feeding into one another in repeating cycles, and what first triggers the common late-onset form of Alzheimer’s remains uncertain.24 A 2021 review argued that the amyloid cascade fits the rare inherited forms well but explains less of the common form.23 Those inherited forms make up 1% to 5% of cases and are caused by mutations in three genes, APP, PSEN1 and PSEN2.25 The roles of microglia are also more complex than a protective early phase followed by a harmful later one. In mice, they can help wall off amyloid plaques and limit damage to nearby nerve fibres, but they can also help form plaques and spread tau. 26,27 In a 2024 study, removing microglia before plaques appeared led to fewer plaques, while removing them later left the plaques looser and the surrounding nerve fibres more damaged. Removing them did not change the levels of CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 42 dissolved amyloid, which questions the idea that microglia mainly clear it away. 26 An earlier study found that removing microglia almost stopped tau from spreading between two brain regions.27 These findings come from mouse models, and they do not show that microglia start the common form of Alzheimer’s in people. In people, a 2022 study of individuals without dementia who had Alzheimer’s brain changes found that higher levels of TREM2, a protein linked to microglial activation, were linked to slower build-up of amyloid. In those who also had tau, they were linked to slower build- up of tau and slower decline in thinking. The authors noted that not all microglial activation is beneficial.28 How Many Brain Cells Are Microglia? ““So a microglial cell is an immune cell that lives within your brain. It makes up about 10% of your brain cells.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Microglia are a minority of brain cells. Estimates depend on the species, the brain region and the counting method, and the familiar figure of 10% is an approximation, not a precise count. The answer depends on what you count. A large study across 33 mammal species, including counts from whole brains, found that microglia make up about 7% of the brain’s non-neuronal cells, and that this proportion is similar across species and regions.29 Because neurons are left out of that figure, microglia make up a smaller share of all brain cells. Older counts in human brain tissue found anything from 0.5% to 16.6% of all cells, with more microglia in the white CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 43 matter, which carries signals between regions, than in the grey matter.30 So 10% falls within the range that has been reported, but it is an approximation. The 7% figure refers only to non-neuronal cells and cannot be compared directly with a share of all brain cells, and estimates also differ between species, regions and counting methods.29,31 Do Blood Sugar and Inflammation Activate Microglia? ““When our blood sugar is elevated, it shifts them to being destructive. When there’s inflammation in the body making its way to the brain from any source, it shifts the M2 supportive microglia to being destructive. Any metabolic issue.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT In cells and animals, high glucose and inflammation in the body shift microglia into a more inflammatory state. Brain scans in people support the link with inflammation. When microglia are grown in a dish with high glucose, they produce more inflammatory chemicals and damaging molecules, and react more strongly to an inflammatory trigger. 32 High glucose also primes a part of the immune system called the inflammasome, and blocking it reversed the behavioural and inflammatory changes in diabetic mice.33 A 2023 review of 267 studies concluded that diabetes changes the state of microglia in many ways.34 CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 44 For inflammation in the body reaching the brain, there is direct evidence in people. When five healthy young men were given a small dose of bacterial toxin into a vein, a brain scan marker of immune activity (TSPO, discussed below) rose by 53% within five hours.35 When the dose was repeated a week later, the response was different, which shows that the brain’s immune system adapts.35 The blood sugar evidence comes mainly from cells and rodents, with a smaller number of observational and post-mortem studies in people. 34 Repeated inflammation can also lead to tolerance, so the effect is not always a simple escalation.35 Does Energy Supply Control Microglia? ““This is a cell [M2] that gets its energy from active function of the mitochondria… This cell [M1] uses a totally different energy production facility called glycolysis. So upstream of this shift are changes in the mitochondria… Mitochondrial energy production is what dictates the shift between M2 and M1.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““But again, upstream of all of this is the energetics. It’s the mitochondrial function. And this is what is so sensitive to our metabolism, to insulin, to exercise on the positive side, to metformin on the medication side, to rapamycin on the experimental side.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 45 ““When these microglia shift to being more destructive, they downregulate, or reduce, the function of their mitochondria… It goes back upstream, creates a feed-forward process… these cells are pro- inflammatory… they’re secreting chemicals of inflammation throughout their environment… And those chemicals come back and target the healthy microglial cells and further shift them over to the dark side.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT How microglia behave is closely tied to how they produce energy, and damaged mitochondria can feed a cycle of inflammation in laboratory studies. Evidence for exercise and metformin comes from animals, and there is none yet for rapamycin. Cells make energy in two main ways. Mitochondria burn fuel with oxygen, a process called oxidative phosphorylation, while glycolysis breaks down glucose more quickly without it. In laboratory studies, inflammatory microglia lean towards glycolysis, while calmer, repair- focused microglia rely more on their mitochondria.36 Blocking glycolysis can reduce the inflammatory chemicals microglia release, 36, 37 and in one cell model of Alzheimer’s, changes in energy use came before the microglia changed state. 38 This suggests metabolism helps drive their state. The link seems to be less fixed than the word “dictates” suggests, though. In that same cell model, which was designed to mimic late stage Alzheimer’s, inflammatory microglia shut down both ways of producing energy,38 and in related immune cells glycolysis also supports some healing functions. 39 The relationship depends on the setting. CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 46 In cells, inflammation damages mitochondria, damaged mitochondria cause more inflammation, and activated microglia release chemicals that inflame their surroundings and can harm nearby neurons.40,41 What has not been shown in people is that one group of activated microglia converts neighbouring healthy microglia in the living brain. On treatments, three months of treadmill running in Alzheimer’s mice improved energy production in microglia and reduced amyloid and inflammation.42 In mice on a high-fat diet, both exercise and metformin restored energy production in the brain, though not specifically in microglia.43 Rapamycin blocks a pathway called mTORC1, which promotes the clearing and renewal of mitochondria and reduces inflammation. 44 No study was found that tested its effect on microglial energy directly, and no human study was found for any of the three. Do a Leaky Gut and Damaged Proteins Activate Microglia? ““These M2 microglial cells… have receptors that are receiving information: inflammatory chemicals from anywhere in the body. Like a leaky gut… creates increased inflammation in the body. Those chemicals can make their way to the brain… Free radicals created by excessive stress, from chemical exposure, you name it. Increased blood sugar changes the shape of our proteins: glycated proteins… A1C… That’s a bonding of glucose to haemoglobin.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT A leaky gut, oxidative damage and sugar-damaged proteins can each drive inflammation. Nobody has shown in people that they activate microglia in this way. CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 47 Research has examined each step. When the gut lining becomes more permeable, fragments of bacteria can enter the bloodstream and cause low-grade inflammation throughout the body.45 In ageing mice, a leakier gut led to the build-up of a sugar-damaged molecule inside microglia, which harmed their mitochondria and reduced their energy supply. The same molecule rises with age in human blood and brain, but the effect on microglia was shown only in mice. 46 Human studies with bacterial toxin show that inflammation in the body can activate immune responses in the brain. 35 In one study of eight healthy men, scan signals rose by 30% to 60%. 47 A 2024 review reported that levels of this bacterial toxin (LPS) in the blood and brain are raised in people with Alzheimer’s, but noted that whether lowering it reduces the risk or progression of the disease has not been tested. 48 In one small post- mortem study, the toxin was on average more than seven times as abundant in the neocortex and more than 21 times as abundant in the hippocampus of people with Alzheimer’s, comparing four people with Alzheimer’s and four controls for the neocortex, and three of each for the hippocampus.49 The part about HbA1c needs a correction. HbA1c is an early form of glycation, where glucose sticks to haemoglobin. It differs from the advanced glycation end-products (AGEs) that act on immune cells. 50 A mouse study found that AGEs from food can weaken the gut barrier and trigger inflammation in the colon, but no study was found showing in people that AGEs from a leaky gut activate microglia.51 CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 48 Can a Scan Show Harmful Microglia? ““We can actually, in living humans, image the microglial cells when they shift from being supportive to being destructive, this M1 configuration. We can actually see that on a special scan called a TSPO scan… when we image on this PET scan for TSPO in the Alzheimer’s brain, you can see it is absolutely just lit up. [Later:] the TSPO receptor, that is found on the M1, dangerous microglia.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““We see positive TSPO imaging, meaning activation of the brain’s immune cells, across a wide variety of neurodegenerative issues… look at all of the neurodegenerative conditions that are characterised by exactly what I just talked to you about… the shift of the microglial cells from being supportive to being destructive.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT TSPO scans show more brain immune activity in Alzheimer’s and many other neurodegenerative diseases. But the scan cannot tell harmful microglia from protective ones, so it is not a marker of the “M1” state. TSPO is a protein that increases in activated immune cells, and PET scans can detect it in living people.52 A 2023 meta-analysis of 156 studies found a higher signal in the cortex of people with Alzheimer’s, and an earlier meta-analysis of 28 studies found raised levels throughout the brain, with smaller rises in people with mild cognitive impairment.53,54 The 2023 meta-analysis also found raised signals in other neurodegenerative disorders, as well as in mood disorders and multiple sclerosis. 53 In Parkinson’s, a 2021 meta-analysis of 15 studies CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 49 found increases in many brain regions with older, first-generation tracers, but only in the midbrain with newer ones. 55 Individual studies disagree. A 2020 study of 24 patients and a 2026 study of 71 patients found increases, the second mainly in advanced disease and in Parkinson’s dementia.56,57 A 2015 study that looked only at the striatum, a 2017 study and a 2018 study of 16 patients found none. 58–60 The 2017 study included only patients without cognitive problems. 59 For frontotemporal dementia, a 2026 study that has not yet been peer reviewed found that the scan signal matched the number of activated microglia seen after death.61 The evidence does not support TSPO as a marker of the dangerous M1 microglia specifically. TSPO is found in microglia but also in other brain cells, including astrocytes and the cells lining blood vessels. 62 In rodent cells, inflammation raises TSPO about ninefold, but in human microglia it does not rise at all. 63 A 2022 review states that TSPO lacks cellular specificity and cannot separate protective from harmful inflammation.52 They show that there is more immune activity, not what kind. Elsewhere in the conversation, Dr Perlmutter described the scan more cautiously, as “simply a way in living humans to demonstrate activation of their microglial cells”. Can Microglia Be Turned Back? ““Here’s the empowering part: these cells can shift back and become supportive again. And the influence that directs them back to being supportive is under our control.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 50 IN SHORT Microglia can change state, and laboratory studies show their activity can be reversed. In people, there is little evidence that lifestyle alone can return activated microglia to a healthy resting state. Microglia are not fixed. They move between many states depending on age, surroundings and past exposures. 21 A human study in five young men gave one example: after a first dose of bacterial toxin, the TSPO scan signal rose by 53%, but after a second dose a week later it fell 38% below its starting point. That shows the brain’s immune response can be reset.35 It does not show microglia returning to a healthy resting state. The lifestyle evidence is suggestive. In 167 older adults whose brains were examined after death, those who had been more physically active had fewer activated microglia, and this accounted for about 30% of the link between activity and better thinking, rising to more than 40% in people with extensive Alzheimer’s changes.64 But this was an observational study. In a six-month exercise trial in 12 pairs of identical twins, exercise had no effect on the TSPO signal. 65 Talking therapy for depression did lower the signal in one study, which shows that a treatment without drugs can change it.66 No study has yet measured healthy microglial markers in people before and after a lifestyle change. It has not been demonstrated. CONNECTION AND THE BRAIN’S IMMUNE CELLS 51 04 Building and Pruning the Brain’s Connections Dr Perlmutter connects the way the young brain wires itself with the way the ageing brain loses connections. Babies form synapses at great speed, and microglia trim the excess. Research by Beth Stevens suggests Alzheimer’s may reuse this trimming in later life. The figures he gives for how fast connections form and are pruned in infancy do not come from published studies. A Million Connections a Second? ““The rate of synapse, or connection, formation at age one year is about one million synapses being formed every second… connecting to each other at the rate of a million connections per second in the one-year- old.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 52 IN SHORT Babies do form synapses at a high rate. But no study has measured “one million per second”. Synapses, the connections between neurons, start forming before birth and multiply rapidly after it.1,2 Counts in human brain tissue show they reach their peak at different times in different regions: around three months of age in the part of the brain that processes sound, and after 15 months in the frontal cortex.1 The “one million per second” figure appears in the preface of a book on the newborn brain, but it does not come from any primary study. 3 The human studies count how densely packed synapses are at different ages.1,2,4 None of them measures how many form each second, so the figure is an estimate that has been repeated often, not a measurement. How Fast Is Pruning? ““Now, this idea of synaptic pruning, the refinement part, is happening at about 20% of that rate. That means that while these connections are being made, about 20% of those connections, or others, are actually being destroyed.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT No study supports pruning at 20% of the rate of formation. Most pruning happens later, through childhood and adolescence, and timing differs between brain regions. BUILDING AND PRUNING THE BRAIN’S CONNECTIONS 53 None of the human studies reports pruning as a percentage of synapse formation.1,5 What they show is a pattern over time. Synapses are overproduced in infancy and then gradually removed. In the visual cortex, about 40% of synapses were lost between eight months and 11 years of age. 5 Pruning in the auditory cortex is finished by about age 12, but in the prefrontal cortex, which handles planning and self- control, it continues into the mid-teens. 1 A study of dendritic spines, the small structures that receive synapses, suggests pruning in the prefrontal cortex carries on through people’s twenties.6 So pruning is a long, region-by-region process that mostly happens after the peak of synapse formation, not a fixed fraction running alongside it in infancy.1,6 Do Microglia Do the Pruning? ““What is the mechanism whereby this synaptic pruning, this refinement, takes place? The microglial cells. The microglial cells are the ones that are appropriately destroying or taking away these synapses, which is a normal activity.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Microglia are central to pruning during development, working with complement proteins. But astrocytes, another type of brain cell, also remove synapses, and most of the evidence comes from animals. A widely cited study in mice showed that during development, microglia engulf connections in the visual system, that this depends on brain activity and on complement proteins, part of the immune system that tags targets for removal, and that blocking this process leaves lasting faults in wiring. 7 Later research described a system of BUILDING AND PRUNING THE BRAIN’S CONNECTIONS 54 “eat me” signals that mark unwanted synapses and “don’t eat me” signals that protect others.8 But microglia are not the only cells involved. In mice, astrocytes also engulf synapses through their own pathways, and mice that lack these pathways keep too many connections. 9 A 2018 review describes the complement proteins that trigger pruning as coming from microglia, astrocytes and neurons.10 A 2025 study in mice found that when synapses were removed in response to a change in sensory input, astrocytes did not engulf them. Instead, astrocytes drew back from the synapses before microglia removed them, in response to signals released by the microglia.11 Human studies show when pruning happens, 6 but the studies of how it happens described here were in mice.7,9,11 Are Autism and Fragile X Caused by Too Little Pruning? ““There are situations when there’s not enough synaptic pruning, and there are consequences. Autism spectrum disorder being one of them. Fragile X syndrome is another. These are situations where the brain doesn’t adequately synaptically prune. And they’re called hyperconnection syndromes, where the brain is actually over- connected.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Reduced pruning is seen in at least some people with autism, and Fragile X brains show excess immature connections consistent with incomplete pruning. But autism cannot be summed up as an over- connected brain. BUILDING AND PRUNING THE BRAIN’S CONNECTIONS 55 Several studies suggest that pruning is reduced in at least a subset of people with autism. In brain tissue examined after death, people with autism had more dendritic spines in the temporal lobe, along with signs that a pathway that controls cell growth and clean-up was overactive.12 A 2021 study linked excess synapses to over-connected circuits in mice and in a subset of children with autism, 13 and newer post-mortem work found extra small nerve fibres in the temporal lobe that had not been trimmed back with age.14 For Fragile X syndrome, the most common inherited cause of intellectual disability, post-mortem studies found more dendritic spines than usual, many of them long and immature. The researchers interpreted this as a failure of normal maturation or pruning that persists into adulthood. 15 The studies are small and cannot separate too little pruning from connections that never matured. The label “hyperconnection syndromes” fits some findings, but not all.
