In the prefrontal cortex, the same post-mortem work found an imbalance consistent with too many local connections and too few long-range ones.14 The first study to measure synapses in living autistic people, a 2024 brain scan study of 12 autistic adults and 20 matched non-autistic adults, found that synaptic density across the whole cortex was 17% lower in the autism group, and that people with more autistic features had lower density. 16 A 2026 review also stresses that no single microglial fault explains autism, and that in different people pruning may be insufficient, excessive or misdirected.17 BUILDING AND PRUNING THE BRAIN’S CONNECTIONS 56 Is Alzheimer’s Pruning Switched Back On? ““These same microglial cells that are involved in appropriate synaptic pruning when we are growing up… are the same microglial cells that inappropriately digest away or destroy our synapses when we are older. And that can manifest as Alzheimer’s disease. So there’s a thinking now in neuroscience that Alzheimer’s is the reactivation of a normal developmental event.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““There’s a doctor, Beth Stevens at Harvard, who’s done some amazing work, and she’s identified the fact that these synapses are sending ‘eat me’ signals to the microglial cells, and the microglia come and digest away that synapse… The synapse binds a chemical called complement. And complement triggers these microglia… She’s demonstrated this. You can actually see it happening.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT In mice, Alzheimer’s reuses the brain’s developmental pruning machinery. It is one process among several, and it has not been shown directly in living patients. In a highly cited 2016 study in mouse models of Alzheimer’s, complement proteins built up on synapses before plaques formed, microglia engulfed synaptic material when exposed to small clumps of amyloid, and blocking the complement pathway protected synapses from early loss. The authors concluded that the machinery used to prune synapses during development is wrongly reactivated in Alzheimer’s.18 Later studies in other mouse models also linked BUILDING AND PRUNING THE BRAIN’S CONNECTIONS 57 complement to synapse loss, and showed that astrocytes take part as well.19–21 In older people examined after death, the proportion of activated microglia was closely linked to amyloid, tau and decline in thinking, with an effect comparable to that of the APOE4 gene variant.22 In people, the loss of synapses is closely linked to decline in thinking. In a 1991 post-mortem study of 15 people with Alzheimer’s and 9 controls, the density of synapses in the cortex was strongly linked to scores on thinking tests, while plaques and tangles were only weakly linked.23 A 2022 brain scan study of 45 living people with early Alzheimer’s found that synaptic density was linked to overall thinking and to five separate areas of thinking, and predicted thinking better than the volume of grey matter. 24 Neither study shows that microglia removed the synapses. The idea that Alzheimer’s borrows developmental pruning comes from studies in mouse models. That it is fundamentally a reactivation of pruning goes further, because Alzheimer’s involves several processes at once. Nobody has yet watched microglia remove tagged synapses in the brain of a living patient. Is Age the Biggest Risk Factor? ““The biggest input that relates to the shift of the microglial cell from supportive to destructive is our chronological age. And I say that because that’s what the data says, but I believe it should be our biological age… that helps us understand why the number one risk factor for Alzheimer’s is the number of birthdays we’ve had.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO BUILDING AND PRUNING THE BRAIN’S CONNECTIONS 58 IN SHORT The risk of Alzheimer’s rises steeply with age. Measures of biological age add some predictive value, but the role of age-related changes in microglia is less clear. The risk of Alzheimer’s rises steeply with age. The chance of developing it doubles roughly every five to six years in later life, 25 and a 2025 analysis of global data shows dementia rising sharply after age 60.26 Microglia change with age too: in human brains, their branches become shorter and simpler, and these changes are more marked in Alzheimer’s.27 Whether these changes are the main reason age raises risk has not been shown. Biological age, estimated from blood tests or chemical marks on DNA, does seem to add information. In one study of 1,930 older adults, each extra year of biological age was associated with a higher risk of death (hazard ratio 1.11) and of a first major illness (hazard ratio 1.07). 28 In 6,069 women followed for about nine years, one such measure predicted memory problems and dementia regardless of actual age. 29 These studies show that biological age adds some predictive value. They do not show it should replace counting birthdays. BUILDING AND PRUNING THE BRAIN’S CONNECTIONS 59 05 Sleep Sleep is the third pillar of Dr Perlmutter’s plan. He describes it as the time when the brain clears its waste, stores memories and keeps blood sugar in check, and he recommends tracking it with a wearable. The effects of a single bad night on insulin and amyloid are well documented. His account of when deep sleep happens does not match the research, and the theory that sleep clears waste from the brain is still being debated. Can a Ring or Watch Measure Sleep? ““You could subject yourself to a formal polysomnogram… But anyway, you have access to an Oura Ring, an Apple Watch, a Whoop, and you can measure your sleep and, with pretty good reliability, get a sense as to its quality and its quantity.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 60 IN SHORT On average, wearables measure how long you sleep within minutes of a sleep study, although some underestimate it in older adults. They are much less accurate at telling sleep stages apart, and devices differ. The gold standard for measuring sleep is polysomnography, an overnight test that records brain waves, eye movements and muscle activity. Compared with it, consumer devices do well at the basics. In a study of 53 people who wore several devices in a sleep laboratory, all were right about whether people were awake or asleep about 86% to 89% of the time. 1 On average, Oura overestimated total sleep time by 1.5 minutes and Whoop underestimated it by 12 minutes, while the Apple Watch overestimated it by about 40 minutes.1 Sleep stages are harder to measure. In the same study, the devices agreed with the laboratory test on the exact stage only 50% to 65% of the time. 1 Newer studies are more favourable for Oura: in 96 people tested over several nights, the third-generation ring was right about sleep and wake about 92% of the time, and its accuracy for individual stages ranged from 75.5% for light sleep to 90.6% for REM. 2,3 In a comparison of 11 trackers, performance varied widely between devices.4 A 2025 meta-analysis of 24 studies of wrist-worn devices found that, on average, they measured total sleep time within about 17 minutes of a sleep study, but still differed significantly from it. 5 Accuracy is also lower in people with sleep problems: in a sleep clinic, ring trackers matched the sleep study well on average, but readings for individual nights were often far off, and Oura identified the correct sleep stage only about half the time. 6 A 2026 meta-analysis of 28 studies of finger- worn devices, such as smart rings, found that they told sleep from wake correctly 87% of the time, but their accuracy for light, deep and REM sleep was 65%, 81% and 74%. 7 Accuracy may also be lower in older adults. In a 2026 study that compared four devices with a sleep SLEEP 61 study, the Fitbit and the Oura Ring underestimated total sleep time in older adults by 74.5 and 75.5 minutes on average, and the devices may be less accurate in older than in young adults. 8 Most of these studies were done in healthy adults over one or a few nights. Wearables are useful for tracking sleep duration and patterns, but their sleep-stage figures should be read with caution, and they cannot replace a sleep study. Is REM Sleep When We Store Memories? ““Restorative sleep is so important. It allows us to consolidate our memories, our experiences, in REM sleep.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Sleep does help to fix memories. But much of this happens in deep non-REM sleep, and the different stages work together. Sleep strengthens memories, and REM sleep plays a part, but it is not the main stage for this. A review describes deep non-REM sleep, also called slow-wave sleep, as especially important for remembering facts and events, while REM seems to contribute more to skills and emotional memories.9 Memory for facts can improve after only one or two hours of sleep.9 Newer studies suggest the stages work together. In a nap study, replaying cues linked to emotional images during REM sleep impaired memory for them, while the benefit of cues during deep sleep depended on how much of both stages people had. 10 In another study, deep sleep predicted better memory for negative images, while REM was linked to stronger communication between the hippocampus and the cortex overnight.11 SLEEP 62 So REM sleep has a role, but memories are not stored mainly “in REM sleep”, and different types of memory cannot be neatly assigned to one stage. Does Sleep Clear Waste from the Brain? ““It allows activation, as you know, of the glymphatic system that allows us to take out the garbage, basically, which new research indicates happens when we move as well. Yet another part of the motion multi- tool.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO “STEVEN BARTLETT “…at nighttime, it’s almost like the street cleaners come out in our brain.” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “That is a perfect metaphor. But they’re only coming out if you’re… In stage four. If you’re in deep sleep.” IN SHORT The idea that sleep clears waste from the brain is a leading theory, but it is being actively debated, and human measurements are indirect. “Stage four” is no longer a recognised sleep stage. The glymphatic system is a proposed network of channels through which fluid washes through the brain and carries waste away. In the original 2013 mouse study, sleep enlarged the spaces between brain cells by about 60% and increased the removal of amyloid. 12 In people, one night without sleep slowed the clearance of a tracer injected into SLEEP 63 the spinal fluid from most of the brain, and the next night’s sleep did not make up for it.13,14 The theory has been challenged. In 2024, a mouse study in Nature Neuroscience reported that clearance was lower during sleep and anaesthesia, not higher. 15 In 2025, other researchers argued that its methods did not support that conclusion.16 The question is unresolved. A 2025 review links fluid movement mainly to deep non-REM sleep, 17 and human scans, including one 2026 study not yet peer reviewed, have shown the largest fluid-related signals in that stage. 18,19 But a detailed 2024 mouse study found that slow pulses in blood vessels during non-REM sleep predicted clearance better than how much deep sleep the mice had. 20 The terminology has also changed: under the sleep-scoring rules introduced in 2007, the old stages three and four were combined into a single stage, N3.21 Regular exercise may help fluid movement in the brain, according to animal studies and early human work, but human measurements are indirect, and in rodents clearance dropped briefly while they were running.22 Does One Bad Night Affect Insulin? ““But sleep helps us reduce inflammation as well. It improves insulin functionality… So even one night’s poor sleep dramatically changes the function of insulin and has a significant effect upon our blood sugar control, and really does set the stage for degeneration.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO SLEEP 64 IN SHORT A single short night can measurably reduce insulin sensitivity in healthy adults, by about 16% to 25% in laboratory studies. In a Dutch study of nine healthy people, sleeping about four hours instead of seven and a half made the body markedly less responsive to insulin the next day. The liver kept releasing glucose when it should have stopped, the muscles took up less, and about 25% less glucose was needed to keep blood sugar steady during the test. 23 In another study of 16 healthy young men, one night of about four hours’ sleep raised a standard measure of insulin resistance by 16%. 24 A week of five hours a night had similar effects, 25 and a 2022 meta-analysis of randomised trials found that restricting sleep reduces insulin sensitivity.26 In a 2025 trial of 11 healthy, active young men, one night of three hours’ sleep reduced glucose tolerance, the body’s ability to clear sugar from the blood after a sugary drink, the next morning. Insulin levels during the test did not differ, and 30 minutes of brisk walking beforehand did not prevent the change.27 “Dramatically” may suggest more than the studies show. In the Dutch study, blood sugar and insulin levels the next morning were normal, and the change was only detected during detailed testing.23 The studies were also small, mostly in healthy young men, and used strict sleep restriction, not the broken sleep most people experience. Whether short sleep sets the stage for brain degeneration can only be studied over many years. In the Whitehall II study, 7,959 people were followed for 25 years. Sleeping six hours or less at ages 50 and 60 was linked to a higher risk of dementia than sleeping seven hours, with hazard ratios of 1.22 and 1.37, although the estimate at age 70 was imprecise (1.24, range 0.98 to 1.57). Persistently short sleep from 50 to 70 was linked to a 30% higher risk. 28 The study was observational, and it concerns habitual short sleep, not a single night. SLEEP 65 Does One Bad Night Affect Decisions? ““Not only that, but it significantly affects our decision-making ability after just one night of poor sleep… [Later:] sleep dramatically affects your decision-making ability the next day… when you’re up all night, you make bad decisions the next day. You eat crap, and you know you do.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT One night without sleep changes how the brain responds during decisions, and makes high-calorie food more tempting. Effects on risk-taking in general are mixed. On food choices, two studies found effects. In a study of healthy adults, a night without sleep reduced activity in brain areas involved in weighing up choices, increased activity in the amygdala, and made people want high-calorie foods more. 29 In another study of 32 men, sleep loss made them willing to pay more for food, but not for other rewards, even though they were no hungrier.30 For decisions in general, the evidence is mixed. A recent review found that sleep loss can make people either riskier or more cautious, depending on the task and how the choice is framed. 31 In a study of 56 adults, one night without sleep did not change how much risk people took in a laboratory game, although it dulled the brain’s response to winning and losing.32 That one bad night always leads to worse decisions in general is less clear. SLEEP 66 When Does Deep Sleep Happen? “STEVEN BARTLETT “And how long does it take to get into deep sleep?” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “To get into it? It’s something that typically happens later on in your sleep cycle. So for me, it’s around hour five and a half.” STEVEN BARTLETT “So you have more deep sleep further into the night.” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “That’s right.” IN SHORT Deep sleep is concentrated early in the night, not later, and it declines with age. In a study of healthy adults, deep sleep peaked in the first sleep cycle of the night and decreased across the night. 33 A study of age and deep sleep found that the differences between older and younger people were greatest at the start of the night, when the pressure to sleep is highest.34 Deep sleep also declines with age. 35,36 A meta-analysis of 65 studies and 3,577 people found that the proportion of deep sleep falls with age in adults,37 and a 2026 meta-analysis found shorter deep sleep in older people.38 The idea that deep sleep generally comes later in the night does not match the research. SLEEP 67 Where Is the Amygdala? ““So within the temporal lobe, which is here, inside of it, in the insular portion, which means the part that during development rolled in, is this almond-shaped… area, that’s called the amygdala… The amygdala is an impulsive, self-centred area that makes rapid decisions.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The amygdala is in the temporal lobe, but it lies deep in its inner part, not in the insula. Its role is broader than impulsive decision-making. The amygdala is an almond-shaped group of more than ten nuclei located deep in the inner (medial) part of the temporal lobe, on both sides of the brain. 39 The insula is a separate area of cortex folded inside the deep groove between the temporal and frontal lobes. At this point Dr Perlmutter placed the amygdala in the insular part of the temporal lobe, although a moment earlier he had described it as lying “deep within the temporal lobes”. Describing the amygdala as impulsive and self-centred simplifies its role. It is central to fear, as Dr Perlmutter noted when he said that “some people call it the fear centre”, and to judging how emotionally important something is, and it plays a part in quick, value-based decisions.39 But a 2021 review describes a much wider role: the amygdala constantly evaluates information from the surroundings and assigns it emotional value, and takes part in decision-making and in learning.40 “Impulsive” and “self-centred” are not terms used in brain science, so they cannot be tested directly. SLEEP 68 Does Inflammation Weaken the “Adult in the Room”? ““One of the things is inflammation. Inflammation can dramatically reduce the functionality, the ability of the adult in the room to be in charge… And if you are chronically inflamed, then your decision making is suffering day in and day out.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Inflammation induced in experiments does weaken the brain circuits that regulate emotion. Evidence that everyday chronic inflammation impairs decisions is more limited. The “adult in the room” refers to the prefrontal cortex, which helps keep emotional reactions in check. Studies link inflammation to weaker connections in these circuits. In a placebo-controlled study, healthy men given a small dose of bacterial toxin showed weaker connections between the amygdala, insula and cingulate cortex and wider networks in the front of the brain. 41 In people with depression, higher levels of an inflammation marker in the blood were linked to weaker links between the amygdala and the prefrontal cortex, and to more anxiety.42 A meta-analysis of 24 imaging studies found inflammation affected the same regions.43 One study looked at decisions. After a typhoid vaccine, which causes mild inflammation, the brain signals involved in weighing evidence and learning from rewards were dampened. 44 But this was a short-term effect in a laboratory task, and it does not show that ongoing low-level inflammation harms everyday decisions. SLEEP 69 Sleep loss also tends to weaken the link between the prefrontal cortex and the amygdala, 45 although one study found the opposite in some circumstances.46 Does One Bad Night Raise Amyloid? ““And even one night of non-restorative sleep does the same thing, even more so. One night of not getting a good night’s sleep dramatically reduces your ability to clear beta-amyloid.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT One night without sleep measurably raises amyloid levels in the human brain and spinal fluid. Whether this is because less is cleared, more is made, or both, is not settled. Several human studies have found that amyloid rises after a night without sleep. In a brain-scan study of 20 healthy adults, one night without sleep increased amyloid in the hippocampus and thalamus. 47 In five people whose spinal fluid was sampled, sleep deprivation raised amyloid levels by about 35% to 55%, while levels in the blood fell slightly, which the authors interpreted as reduced clearance from the brain.48 A trial in 12 adults found amyloid and tau in spinal fluid were consistently lower after sleep than after a night without sleep, 49 and a study of 39 people found that sleep moved more of these proteins from the brain into the blood.50 A 2026 meta-analysis of six studies with 169 people found that sleep deprivation raises amyloid in spinal fluid. 51 Why it rises is less clear. 52 A 2018 study that measured how fast amyloid was made found that sleep deprivation raised overnight amyloid by 25% to 30% through increased production.53 On the other hand, a study using a tracer SLEEP 70 injected into the spinal fluid found that one night without sleep slowed its clearance from the brain. 13 The studies are small, and they measure amyloid in different places: the brain, the spinal fluid and the blood. A rise after one night does not mean lasting harm, and “dramatically” is stronger than the evidence allows. SLEEP 71 06 Sugar and Ultra-Processed Food Diet is the fourth pillar of Dr Perlmutter’s plan, and he aims most of his criticism at sugar and ultra-processed food. The wider claims, that these foods are reshaping behaviour and that our genes have not changed since the Stone Age, go further than the evidence. Are Ultra-Processed Foods Inflammatory? ““We used to call it the standard American diet… Then it became the Western diet, and now it’s pretty much the global diet that so aggressively features these ultra-processed foods that are pro- inflammatory… The world is eating foods that are increasing inflammation, and inflammation separates the connection between the adult in the room and the impulsive… Globally, this allows us to relate the change in global nutrition to some of the things we’re seeing happening in our world today.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 72 IN SHORT Eating more ultra-processed food is linked to higher levels of inflammation in the blood. But the evidence is observational, and it does not show that diet is making whole societies more impulsive. The narrower claim, that these foods are linked to inflammation, has been studied directly. Ultra-processed foods are industrial products made mostly from refined ingredients and additives, such as packaged snacks, sugary drinks and ready meals. A 2025 review of 24 studies found that in adults, 11 of 17 analyses reported higher levels of C- reactive protein (CRP), a standard blood marker of inflammation, in people who ate more of these foods.1 Other inflammatory signals tended to be higher in some studies too. 1 In Australian adults, every extra 100 grams of ultra-processed food a day was linked to 4% higher CRP, or 2.5% after allowing for body weight. 2 In Brazilian teenagers, those who ate the most had higher CRP and 79% higher levels of another inflammatory signal than those who ate the least. 3 A 2025 study of almost 2,000 adults found that body fat explained between about 13% and 70% of the link, depending on the marker, so part of the effect seems to come through weight gain.4 On behaviour, a 2026 study in Kuwaiti children found that eating more ultra-processed food was linked to more severe ADHD symptoms, especially hyperactivity, and the link was stronger in boys.5 But studies like this look at a single moment in time and cannot show cause and effect. None of them tests whether changes in the world’s diet are behind “things we’re seeing happening in our world today”. Ultra-processed food has also been linked to the brain and to mental health. A 2023 meta-analysis of 10 observational studies with 867,316 people found that high intake, compared with low intake, was linked to a higher risk of dementia (risk ratio 1.44, range 1.09 to 1.90), while moderate intake was not (1.12, range 0.96 to 1.31), and the results varied greatly between studies. 6 A 2024 umbrella review of meta-analyses, SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 73 covering 45 pooled analyses and almost 9.9 million people, rated the evidence as convincing for links with deaths from cardiovascular disease, type 2 diabetes, anxiety (odds ratio 1.48) and common mental disorders (odds ratio 1.53), but rated most of the analyses as low or very low quality. 7 These are associations, and they do not show that diet is changing behaviour across societies. Almost all the evidence linking these foods to inflammation is observational, and only one randomised trial was included in the 2025 review.1 How Much of the American Diet Is Ultra- Processed? ““There’s very little polyphenols in these highly processed, or what we call ultra-processed, foods. In America, 60% of the calories that adults consume are derived from ultra-processed foods. They’re processed such that these polyphenols are processed out of the foods.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Ultra-processed foods supply more than half of the calories American adults eat, about 53% to 57% in recent surveys, a little below the 60% quoted. Processing often lowers polyphenols, but not always. The figure comes from the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES), which tracks what Americans eat. Analyses of it found that ultra-processed foods made up 57% of adult calories in 2017 to 2018, 8 54% in 2015 to 2018, 9 and 53% in 2021 to 2023. 10 Data from around 2007 to 2012 were closer to 58% to 60%, 11–13 and the national survey reported a small fall among adults over the decade to 2023.10 The newest estimates are nearer 53% to 55% than 60%.10,14 SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 74 Polyphenols are natural plant compounds found in foods such as berries, cocoa, tea and olive oil. A review shows that industrial and home processing affect how much of them food contains and how well the body can absorb them, and that the effects depend on the plant and the method. 15 Cocoa is one example: in one study, treating cocoa with alkali, as is done to make it darker and milder, cut its flavanols, a type of polyphenol, from 34.6 mg per gram to as little as 3.9 mg per gram.16 The system used to decide which foods count as ultra-processed has its own problems, and some foods are classed differently by different researchers. Is Orange Juice as Bad as Soda? ““Let’s say this is 12 ounces… Seven. And if this were a can of soda, just two more. But I won’t do it. There are eight teaspoons of sugar in your wonderful glass of orange juice.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““‘Oh, but what about the vitamin C? What about the fibre?’ It doesn’t offset what this is doing to your body.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Orange juice contains a similar amount of sugar to many sodas. But in studies, juice does not behave exactly like a sugary drink, although it provides less fibre than whole fruit. A laboratory analysis of 286 orange juices found an average of 8.9 grams of sugar per 100 ml, with a range of 6.6 to 11.1 grams. 17 For soda, SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 75 a UK survey of 169 sugary fizzy drinks found an average of 30.1 grams of sugar per 330 ml, ranging from 3.3 to 52.8 grams, and cola- flavoured drinks averaged 35.0 grams.18 The second claim, that the vitamin C and fibre “don’t offset” the sugar, goes further than the evidence. In a study of 36 adults, a glass of orange juice raised blood sugar less than an orange-flavoured drink with the same sugar, and people ate less for the rest of the day. 19 A 2026 symposium report suggests this may be due to other components of juice, including polyphenols, pectins, vitamins and minerals.20 A meta-analysis of 15 trials found that orange juice slightly lowered cholesterol and improved a measure of insulin resistance, with no significant effect on other heart and metabolic markers. 21 In a large meta-analysis of long-term studies, sugary drinks were linked to a higher risk of type 2 diabetes, with a risk ratio of 1.27, while fruit juice showed no clear link, with a risk ratio of 0.98.22 A ratio above 1 means a higher risk. Newer meta-analyses are mixed. A 2025 dose-response meta-analysis found that each daily serving of fruit juice had a risk ratio of 1.05, compared with 1.25 for sugary drinks, 23 while another 2025 meta-analysis found no link for 100% juice and a higher risk only for juice drinks that are not 100% juice.24 So in these studies juice did not behave like soda, but a 2021 review notes that it provides less fibre than whole fruit.25 Sugar levels vary between sodas, 18 and studies of fruit juice differ in whether they include juice drinks that are not 100% juice. 24 The 2026 symposium report on juice was sponsored by the Fruit Juice Science Centre.20 SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 76 How Much Sugar Is in Sauces and Dressings? ““One of them is barbecue sauce, which I loved. It’s 40 to 50% sugar, which shocked me. Balsamic glaze, 40 to 50% sugar. I had this commercial fat-free French dressing, which turned out to be 35% sugar.” Steven Bartlett on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Sugar levels vary widely between sauces and dressings, and published surveys do not give typical figures for these products. The figures for a particular product can be checked against its nutrition label. There are few published studies of the sugar in condiments. An Iranian survey of 47 salad dressings found average sugar levels of about 9% in 2017 and under 2% in 2019, which shows how much these products can vary. 26 A 2022 study of six sweet salad dressings did not report sugar at all.27 No published survey gives typical sugar levels for barbecue sauce, balsamic glaze or fat-free French dressing. The research cannot confirm or rule out a 35% figure for a fat-free French dressing, or 40% to 50% for a barbecue sauce or balsamic glaze. The only way to check a particular product is to read its label, because recipes change between brands, countries and years. SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 77 Do Cashews and Exercise Raise Blood Sugar? ““For me, it was cashews. I had no clue that cashew nuts would raise my blood sugar the way they did. I had the glucose monitor on, and it lit up when I ate cashews… I was also very surprised at the degree of my blood sugar elevation with exercise, though it was transient, while I was beginning to exercise. That’s a normal response.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT In trials, cashews do not raise blood sugar much. Hard exercise can briefly raise blood sugar through stress hormones. A continuous glucose monitor (CGM) is a small sensor worn on the skin that measures blood sugar every few minutes. Studies of cashews do not suggest they usually cause large spikes. A meta-analysis of six trials with 521 people found that eating cashews had no effect on fasting blood sugar, insulin or insulin resistance. 28 In a small study of crackers made partly with cashew flour, the rise in blood sugar was no different from wheat crackers.29 People do respond differently to the same food, and a 2024 review of CGM use in people without diabetes describes large differences between individuals. 30 Dr Perlmutter also mentioned that he “had a tendency to overeat cashews”. The research does not show that cashews do this for most people in normal amounts. Few studies have tested plain cashews alone, and some used cashew flour, biscuits or juice.29,31 During hard exercise, stress hormones such as adrenaline can make the liver release glucose faster than the muscles use it, so blood sugar rises for a while. 32 In one case report, a healthy woman’s blood sugar SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 78 rose from 5.0 to 13.5 mmol/L with intense treadmill exercise. 32 The evidence in healthy people is limited. Do Glucose Monitors Help People Without Diabetes? ““I’m not saying you need to wear a CGM at all times, but I think it’s extremely valuable, and now available without a prescription. There was a big pushback in America… there were editorials written in journals saying, ‘Oh my gosh, people are going to be neurotic’… [Later:] to go to the doctor once a year and have a fasting blood sugar measurement… I’m not going to say it’s useless… but we can do so much better.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Glucose monitors help people with diabetes and prediabetes, but there is little evidence of benefit for healthy people, so their value for healthy people is not yet established. For people with blood sugar problems, a 2024 meta-analysis of 25 trials with almost 3,000 people found that feedback from a CGM lowered HbA1c, a measure of average blood sugar over about three months, by 0.28 percentage points. But most of those studies were in people with diabetes, diabetes in pregnancy or obesity.33 A 2026 meta- analysis of 23 studies in people without diabetes found the benefits were mainly in prediabetes, with no clear effect on blood sugar in healthy people. 34 Another 2026 review of 12 studies found that seeing the readings may help people make some changes to their diet, but the certainty of the evidence was low, and there was too little to show whether wearing a monitor helps or harms people’s wellbeing.35 SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 79 The concerns Dr Perlmutter mentions have been raised in the literature. A 2024 review found a lack of consistent, high-quality evidence for CGMs in people without diabetes, and raised worries about effects on eating habits and misleading marketing. 30 Accuracy is another issue. The first independent test of over-the-counter monitors, in 39 adults without diabetes, found they were good for showing trends but less reliable for exact readings, especially after sugary food.36 Over-the-counter monitors are increasingly used by people without diabetes.36 So a monitor gives richer feedback than a yearly test, but the evidence that this improves health in people without blood sugar problems is still modest. Why Do We Like Sweet Things? ““Your brain is programmed to find sweet. That’s your survival mechanism. It tells you that the food in nature is not poisonous, and it helps your body store fat for the winter. I mean, that’s what our genetics do for us. That’s through activation of uric acid.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT A liking for sweetness is evident early in life. But the idea that sweetness signals “not poisonous” is a hypothesis, and genetic studies suggest that raised uric acid is more a result of weight gain than a cause of it, although a 2026 study concluded that it can drive obesity. A 2016 review reports that a preference for sweet tastes is evident early in life, and that it may have served children well when food was scarce, by attracting them to their mothers’ milk and then to energy- rich foods.37 The idea that sweetness tells us a food is safe to eat is an SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 80 evolutionary theory. A 2023 review describes sweet taste as a protection against eating poisonous plants, but adds that this hypothesis is not supported by unanimous scientific evidence.38 Uric acid is a waste product made when the body breaks down certain substances, including fructose, the sugar found in fruit and many sweetened foods. Dr Perlmutter refers to the theory that fructose and uric acid act as a “survival switch” that tells the body to store fat. Genetic studies, which compare people born with naturally higher or lower uric acid or body weight, mostly find that more body fat leads to higher uric acid, while higher uric acid does not lead to more body fat.39–41 One 2024 study did find a link in the other direction, so the question is not fully settled. 42 A 2026 study, which combined human clinical analyses with animal experiments, concluded that uric acid made in the liver “drives obesity” by changing the bacteria in the gut so that more fat is absorbed from food.43 The evidence in this area comes from evolutionary theory, animal work, observational studies and genetic studies, and these do not all agree. What Did Our Ancestors Eat? ““The traditional diet that humans and proto-humans consumed was one that was rich in fibre… rich in polyphenols… and had very good levels of fat, the castigated fat. The low fat that we were so programmed to gravitate towards.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 81 IN SHORT Ancestral diets varied widely with place and season, so there was no single ancestral diet. Many were probably high in fibre, but their polyphenol content has never been measured. Research on hunter-gatherers, both modern groups and those known from archaeology, shows huge variety.44,45 A 2021 review suggests Stone Age diets probably mixed plant and animal foods, but the balance changed with climate, season and geography. 44 A study of 11 tropical hunter-gatherer groups concluded that their diets did not fit the narrow ranges of fat, protein and carbohydrate often assumed by popular “paleo” plans.46 Some reconstructions suggest certain Stone Age diets were moderate to high in fat and protein, and higher in protein than modern Western diets.47 But these are estimates, not direct measurements. Plant-rich diets would have provided polyphenols, but no study has measured how much our ancestors ate. Most of what we know comes from studying modern hunter- gatherers, reconstructing past diets, and archaeology. All of these involve considerable uncertainty, and they are better at showing how much diets varied than at giving exact figures.45,46 Stone Age Genes in a Modern World? ““The central issue here in terms of what’s making us sick is this mismatch between what our genetics expect and what our genetics are seeing… Our genome is a Palaeolithic genome… right now we are experiencing this incredible mismatch between our evolution, meaning our genes, and our environment. I think that’s the central issue related to modern ailments.” SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 82 Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The gap between our genes and modern life may contribute to chronic disease, but it is not proven to be “the central issue”. Human genes have also kept changing since the Stone Age. The idea of “evolutionary mismatch” is discussed in the research literature. It suggests that our diets and surroundings have changed faster than our bodies could adapt, and this may contribute to the chronic diseases common in modern life. 47 A 2021 review adds that Palaeolithic diets were probably balanced between plant and animal foods and “would have been remarkably variable across time and space”.44 The claim that we have “a Palaeolithic genome” differs from recent genetic research. Our genes have kept adapting since the Stone Age ended. A 2026 study of ancient DNA from almost 16,000 people who lived in Europe and western Asia found that hundreds of gene variants have been favoured by natural selection over the past 10,000 years. 48 Extra copies of the amylase genes, which help digest starch, became more common in the same region after farming began. 49 Indigenous people in the Andes also carry many copies, and the evidence suggests these were favoured around 10,000 years ago, when potatoes were first farmed there.50 Mismatch is a broad way of explaining disease, not a single cause that can be tested directly. The evidence found concerns specific changes, such as starch digestion, and does not show that mismatch is the main cause of modern illness. SUGAR AND ULTRA-PROCESSED FOOD 83 07 Gluten and Glyphosate Dr Perlmutter has written at length about gluten, and he argues that far more people react to it than have coeliac disease. Many more people report gluten sensitivity than have coeliac disease, but far fewer react to gluten in blinded tests. His concerns about glyphosate, a weedkiller used on some wheat, are supported for residues in food. The evidence that it harms the human gut or mitochondria at normal levels of exposure is weak. How Many People Are Sensitive to Gluten? “STEVEN BARTLETT “Roughly 1% of the world are truly, like, gluten intolerant. Yet 20% of us… buy gluten-free stuff.” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “Well, I would say, respectfully, that the number of people who are gluten sensitive is probably more than that. There is… a defined entity now, called non-coeliac gluten sensitivity… the reality is that a lot more people have non-coeliac gluten sensitivity than have full-on coeliac disease.” 84 STEVEN BARTLETT “Roughly 0.5% of the general population have non-coeliac gluten sensitivity, with some clinical estimates ranging up to 10%… coeliac disease, it affects roughly 1% of people, and then 0.1% of people have a wheat allergy… up to 20% of adults report feeling much better on a gluten-free diet… double-blind, placebo-controlled food challenge trials show that only a fraction of self-diagnosed individuals react specifically to gluten… a lot of people are allergic to fructans… in Italy… where they don’t have fructans, you don’t have the same response.” IN SHORT Coeliac disease affects about 1% of people. Non-coeliac gluten sensitivity is a recognised condition but difficult to diagnose: about 10% of people report it, but far fewer react to gluten in blinded tests. Fructans are not allergens, and Italian pasta still contains them. Coeliac disease is an autoimmune condition in which gluten damages the lining of the gut. A 2026 meta-analysis and an earlier review estimate that, worldwide, about 0.7% to 0.8% of people have a diagnosis confirmed by biopsy, and about 1.4% to 1.5% have the antibodies found in the disease.1,2 Non-coeliac gluten sensitivity is a recognised condition. Doctors diagnose it when someone without coeliac disease or wheat allergy feels better off gluten and their symptoms return in a blinded test with gluten. There is no blood test for it, and it overlaps with irritable bowel syndrome (IBS), so it remains difficult to define.3 How common it is depends on how it is measured. A 2025 meta- analysis of 25 studies and almost 50,000 people found that about 10% of people worldwide say they are sensitive to gluten or wheat. 4 In Australia, about 14% reported wheat sensitivity, and 24% were avoiding gluten. 5 So the 0.5% figure Steven Bartlett quotes is at the bottom of the range of survey estimates, which a 2021 review gives as 0.49% to 14.9%.6 But when people who say they are sensitive are GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 85 tested without knowing whether they are eating gluten, only 16% to 30% react specifically to it, 7,8 a range confirmed by a 2025 review in The Lancet.3 In blinded tests, many people also react to a placebo. In a 2017 meta- analysis of re-challenge studies, among studies that followed the newest diagnostic criteria, 40% of people relapsed on gluten and 24% on placebo. 8 Fructans, a type of carbohydrate found in wheat, onions and other foods, also play a part. In a blinded trial of 59 people who believed they were sensitive to gluten, symptoms were worse with fructans than with gluten, and gluten caused no more symptoms than a placebo.9 In a 2025 trial, people with the condition had more tiredness, pain and bloating than healthy volunteers, but these symptoms were no worse when they ate gluten than when they ate gluten-free food.10 A few of the other details differ from the research. No study was found that confirms the 0.1% figure for wheat allergy. Fructans are not allergens, as they do not trigger an allergic reaction. They are carbohydrates that ferment in the gut and can cause symptoms. 9 And Italian pasta does contain fructans, as durum wheat has them too. Can Gluten Affect the Brain? ““I was very taken by the work, many years ago, of a doctor, Marios Hadjivassiliou, who’s actually here in England, at Sheffield Hospital, who described issues related to the brain that weren’t related to coeliac disease but were related to gluten consumption… he was describing how some people have headaches from consuming gluten.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 86 IN SHORT Dr Hadjivassiliou’s work helped establish that gluten can cause problems with balance and nerves, even in people without coeliac disease. He did describe headaches, but that evidence comes from a small case series. Dr Hadjivassiliou’s work helped establish gluten ataxia, a loss of balance and coordination, and gluten neuropathy, a form of nerve damage, as recognised conditions in people sensitive to gluten, whether or not they have coeliac disease. A 2019 review found that neuropathy and ataxia are recognised features of gluten-related disorders, and a strict gluten-free diet is the first treatment.11 His group’s 2026 study of a newer antibody test found that 146 people with ataxia and this antibody responded to a gluten-free diet, with improvements on brain scans, although the study had no comparison group.12 He also described headaches. In a 2001 study, ten patients sensitive to gluten who had unusual brain scans all had episodic headaches, and nine of them improved on a gluten-free diet. 13 But this was a small group of selected patients, not proof that gluten causes headaches in people more generally. The 2019 review describes ataxia as the commonest neurological problem, followed by neuropathy, with headache among the other conditions also linked to gluten.11 GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 87 Can Going Gluten-Free Cure Headaches? ““I had a patient with lifelong headaches, who was on narcotics for her headaches… ‘Let’s go gluten-free, see what happens. Come back in six weeks.’ She came back in six weeks and, remarkably, she was headache-free… [An endoscopy showed] she doesn’t have the hallmarks of coeliac disease… She goes back on gluten. What happens? The headaches returned with a vengeance.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT A gluten-free diet has helped headaches in some people with gluten- related disorders. But this case cannot be verified, and blinded trials show that expectations play a large part. There is no way to verify an individual patient’s account, and nothing in the research describes this case. In the small case series described above, most gluten-sensitive patients with headaches improved off gluten.13 In people with coeliac disease, a 2018 meta-analysis of 40 studies found headache in 26% of adults, and a gluten-free diet led to complete resolution of headaches in up to 75% of patients. 14 The same meta-analysis noted that data on how common coeliac disease is among adults with headaches of unknown cause were not yet available.14 A 2025 review of diet and migraine concluded that gluten- free diets may improve headaches in coeliac disease and “may help selected non-celiac patients”.15 In people without coeliac disease, blinded tests make the evidence less certain.16 A combined analysis of ten double-blind trials found that only 38 of 231 people thought to have gluten sensitivity, or 16%, reacted specifically to gluten, while 40% reacted to the placebo.7 Another meta-analysis of 11 studies found that 30% of people had their symptoms return when given gluten, but overall this was not clearly GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 88 more than with a placebo. 8 Only in the trials that used the strictest diagnostic criteria did gluten stand apart, with symptoms returning in 40% of people on gluten and 24% on placebo.8 A single patient’s headaches clearing off gluten and returning on it is not the kind of evidence that shows a cause. A 2022 editorial notes that blinded gluten challenges carry a high nocebo effect, which may confound the results of such studies.16 Is Glyphosate Sprayed on American Wheat? ““In America, though we don’t have GMO wheat… So they’ve not really done the GMO wheat thing yet. So therefore you would think there wouldn’t be any glyphosate on the wheat. That’s a wrong assumption. Glyphosate in America is sprayed on wheat to desiccate it, to dry it out, so they can get more wheat to market more quickly.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Glyphosate is used to dry out some wheat before harvest, and this leaves residues, usually within legal limits. A genetically modified wheat has been approved and grown in Argentina, but the US Department of Agriculture states that none is grown commercially in the United States. Glyphosate is the active ingredient in Roundup, a widely used weedkiller. A 2024 review reports that HB4, a genetically modified (GM) wheat carrying a drought-tolerance gene from sunflowers, received regulatory approval and was grown commercially in Argentina, and was approved for food and feed in other countries. 17 The US Department of Agriculture states that no GM wheat is grown commercially in the United States.18 GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 89 Wheat can carry glyphosate without being GM, because the weedkiller is sometimes sprayed on cereal crops shortly before harvest to dry them out. 19 In field trials on wheat and barley, spraying before sowing left no detectable residue in the grain, but spraying before harvest did, although the levels were still below legal limits. 20 Residues collect in the outer layers of the grain, so they are lower in white flour and higher in wholegrain products. 20 In Canada, glyphosate was found in 42% of almost 8,000 food samples, and 99.4% were within the legal limits.21 A 2025 review reported that glyphosate has been detected in wheat and other staple foods in Europe, Canada and the USA, and that no study had found intakes above the acceptable daily intake, the amount considered safe to eat every day.22 No study has measured how much US wheat is treated this way, and the reason he gives, getting wheat to market faster, has not been directly studied. Does Glyphosate Harm Gut Bacteria? ““Glyphosate is residual in these foods that we eat, and glyphosate has a significant effect on the gut microbiome… we were told that the science was that glyphosate only affects plants… Well, it affects plants and it affects bacteria… So here we are now consuming a chemical, and it’s in all of us, that can affect the bacteria that live within us.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Most people tested have glyphosate in their urine, and animal studies show effects on gut bacteria. But harm to the human gut at normal levels of exposure has not been shown. GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 90 Glyphosate works by blocking a chemical pathway, called the shikimate pathway, that is absent from animal cells. A 2026 review notes that some gut bacteria do use this pathway, which is why there is concern about gut bacteria. 23 Testing confirms that most people are exposed. In a large US survey from 2013 to 2014, glyphosate was found in the urine of 81% of 2,310 people aged six and over. 24 It was found in about three-quarters of pregnant women tested in Canada, 25 and in about half of German children. 26 Levels were generally low, and in European adults most results were too low to measure.27 Effects on the gut microbiome, the community of bacteria living in the gut, are much less certain in people. The same 2026 review found that human data come only from laboratory systems and small studies measuring exposure, and concluded that the size and health relevance of any effects at realistic levels of human exposure remain uncertain.23 The main human study, of 65 pairs of UK twins, found glyphosate in about half of them, and linked it to a greater variety of gut bacteria and some changes in substances in their stools.28 It did not show harm. The 2026 review found that controlled studies in rodents gave the most consistent evidence, with several reporting changes in the types of bacteria, sometimes with inflammation of the gut, after exposure to glyphosate or commercial weedkillers containing it.23,29 But these studies often use doses or conditions that do not reflect a normal diet. A significant effect on the human gut has not been shown. GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 91 Is Glyphosate a Mitochondrial Toxin? ““And here’s a bit of a stretch, but I think really fascinating. Mitochondria were originally bacteria that took up residence in another organism’s cell. And these mitochondria are very much still like bacteria. They have circular DNA like bacteria, and they are sensitive to glyphosate. Glyphosate is a mitochondrial toxin.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Mitochondria did evolve from bacteria. But there is no evidence that glyphosate in food harms mitochondria in people. Mitochondria, the parts of our cells that produce energy, are descended from bacteria that came to live inside other cells long ago, and they still carry their own small, circular DNA. Modern mitochondria have changed a great deal and differ widely between species.30 Dr Perlmutter introduced this point himself as “a bit of a stretch”, and the evidence does not yet show that glyphosate harms mitochondria at the levels people are exposed to. Recent laboratory studies distinguish between pure glyphosate and the commercial weedkillers that contain it alongside other ingredients. A 2026 study in human skin and liver cells found that glyphosate alone had weak and inconsistent effects on cell stress and DNA damage, while most of the commercial products had clearer effects. 31 A 2025 study in liver cells found more stress on mitochondria from the commercial product than from glyphosate alone.32 No human study was found showing that normal exposure to glyphosate harms mitochondria. The closest evidence is a link between glyphosate exposure and markers of cell stress in farmers GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 92 who handle it at work, which is a very different level of exposure from eating food with traces of it. 33 Most of the direct evidence comes from cells or animal tissue, often tested with the commercial products. GLUTEN AND GLYPHOSATE 93 08 Polyphenols Dr Perlmutter returns to polyphenols many times in the conversation. He describes these plant compounds as the reason colourful foods are good for us, as signals to our gut bacteria, and as switches for the body’s own antioxidant defences, and he names coffee, Tartary buckwheat, extra virgin olive oil and dark chocolate as good sources. Most of what he describes has been shown in laboratory and animal studies. The evidence from people is more limited. Do Berries Release Their Sugar Slowly? ““These are foods that are rich in what are called polyphenols. That’s what gives them their colours. That’s why the blackberry is black… Do they contain sugar? Yes, they do, but they contain sugar in a way that it’s released more slowly into the body.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 94 IN SHORT Polyphenols give berries much of their colour, and in two trials, berries eaten with sugar or with a high-carbohydrate breakfast reduced the early rise in blood sugar. Polyphenols are a large family of natural compounds found in plants. One group of them, the anthocyanins, is largely responsible for the red, purple and blue colours of berries, 1 and blackberries get their dark colour mainly from anthocyanins.2 In a randomised trial in which healthy people ate sugar either alone or with berries, the berries reduced the early peaks in blood sugar and insulin, with slightly higher levels later on. The authors concluded that eating berries with sugar can improve blood sugar control after the meal.3 A 2026 trial in 24 healthy adults gave a wild blueberry drink or a placebo drink with a high-carbohydrate breakfast. Blood sugar and insulin after the meal fell as the amount of blueberry rose, and the drinks with 300 mg or more of anthocyanins gave significantly lower levels than the placebo in the first hour.4 Both were small, short studies in healthy adults, so they do not show long-term benefits. Do Gut Bacteria “Read” Polyphenols? ““The polyphenols are chemicals that are read by our gut bacteria. In other words, these are signalling our gut bacteria to do good things: to increase their antioxidant production, to create chemicals that are good for the entire body.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO POLYPHENOLS 95 IN SHORT Polyphenols and gut bacteria do affect each other. “Reading” simplifies how they interact, and evidence from people is still limited. A 2025 review explains that a large share of the polyphenols we eat escapes absorption and reaches the large intestine, where gut bacteria break them down into simpler compounds, some of which can enter the bloodstream. Polyphenols can also change which bacteria thrive in the gut and what they do.5 The language in the quote goes further than the evidence. The same review describes favourable shifts in gut bacteria and in what they produce.5 No study was found showing that polyphenols make gut bacteria increase antioxidant production in people. People also vary a great deal. A 2024 review of 153 human studies found large differences in how people process polyphenols, mainly because of differences in their gut bacteria.6 Do Polyphenols Boost the Body’s Antioxidant Defences? ““A polyphenol is a targeting molecule that activates pathways to create antioxidants in the body. They activate a pathway called the Nrf2 pathway. And that increases our detoxification, it increases our production of antioxidants and it reduces inflammation.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Polyphenols activate the Nrf2 pathway in laboratory and animal studies. Evidence that the amounts found in food do this in people is limited. POLYPHENOLS 96 Nrf2 is a protein inside cells that activates genes for the body’s own antioxidant and detoxifying defences. In many studies in cells and animals, polyphenols and similar plant compounds activate it, and a 2021 review described the mechanisms by which they do so. 7 A 2023 review names Nrf2 among the targets through which polyphenols such as quercetin, resveratrol and curcumin act against oxidative stress and inflammation.8 The evidence in people is much weaker. A 2021 review of 18 human trials found that only about half reported more Nrf2 activity. Many of the positive studies had a high or unclear risk of bias, and the authors concluded there was insufficient high-quality evidence that plant compounds in the diet activate Nrf2 in people. The trials were small, with 727 people in total, and most measured gene activity in blood cells, not in the tissues that would benefit. Only two of the 18 trials had a low risk of bias, and neither found an increase in Nrf2 activation. 7 A 2024 review of 39 trials of dietary compounds in people with kidney disease found that only nine measured Nrf2, and only three of those found an increase, again with a high risk of bias in most studies.9 Some human studies do show lower markers of oxidative stress, but the authors of the 2021 review noted that this can happen without any change in Nrf2 activity, which suggests other mechanisms.7 Is Tartary Buckwheat One of the Richest Sources? ““This Himalayan Tartary buckwheat is probably one of the richest sources of these polyphenols on the planet.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO POLYPHENOLS 97 IN SHORT Tartary buckwheat contains far more of the polyphenol rutin than common buckwheat. But no study shows that it ranks among the very richest polyphenol foods. Tartary buckwheat is a close relative of common buckwheat, and it is richer in polyphenols.10 Grain analyses have found it contains around 1 to 2 grams of rutin per 100 grams, compared with about 10 to 30 milligrams in common buckwheat.11,12 In one study of sprouts, its seeds had 49 times more rutin than those of common buckwheat.13 Whether it is “one of the richest sources on the planet” has not been tested. The studies compare it with other grains and seeds, where it has more rutin. None compares it with the foods at the top of published lists of the richest sources of polyphenols. Its content also varies a great deal with the variety, where it was grown, sprouting and processing.10,12 Is Coffee the Biggest Source? ““What I’m not seeing here is the number one source of polyphenols on the planet, at least in terms of consumption. That is coffee.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Coffee is the largest source of polyphenols in the diets of American adults and many Europeans. But that is not true everywhere, and in some groups tea comes first. In the United States, national survey data from 2013 to 2016 found that coffee provided almost 40% of the polyphenols adults consumed, far POLYPHENOLS 98 ahead of beans and tea at around 10% and 8%. 14 In a large European study of 36,037 adults, coffee was the top source in both Mediterranean and northern European countries, providing 36% and 41% of polyphenols. 15 In Canada, coffee also came first, at 26.8%. 16 But among health-conscious people in the UK, tea came first at 41%, with coffee second at 21%,15 and in Brazil tea led in the south of the country while coffee led elsewhere.17 Coffee is especially rich in one type of polyphenol, the phenolic acids, and it provides most of the phenolic acids in these diets. 15 A 2019 systematic review named tea, coffee, red wine, fruit and vegetables as the main sources of polyphenols.18 Rankings vary by country and population, and the studies do not cover the whole world, so the research cannot show that coffee comes first worldwide. Is Olive Oil Central to the Mediterranean Diet? ““[Extra virgin olive oil] does [contain lots of polyphenols]. And it’s really one of the keys, we’ve now come to understand, of the so-called Mediterranean diet. Unfortunately, now if you travel the Mediterranean, you’ll see that they’re not eating the Mediterranean diet anymore.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The polyphenols in extra virgin olive oil probably contribute to the benefits of the Mediterranean diet, but they are not the only reason. Fewer people in the region follow the traditional diet than before, but it has not disappeared. Studies have examined the role of olive oil polyphenols. A meta- analysis of 26 trials found that olive oil high in polyphenols improved POLYPHENOLS 99 several heart-related markers compared with olive oil low in polyphenols, including lower levels of oxidised LDL cholesterol and higher HDL, the “good” cholesterol.19 The best-known trial is PREDIMED, carried out in Spain. A Mediterranean diet with extra virgin olive oil reduced major heart and circulation events compared with a control diet, with a hazard ratio of 0.69, and a version with nuts had a hazard ratio of 0.72.20 A ratio below 1 means a lower risk. A later analysis found that people who consumed the most extra virgin olive oil had 25% lower cardiovascular risk, while ordinary olive oil, which has fewer polyphenols, showed weaker links.21 But PREDIMED tested a whole diet, not polyphenols alone. Its original 2013 results were retracted and republished in 2018 after study sites that had broken the rules of randomisation were excluded, and the size of the effect stayed the same.22 A letter about the republished trial pointed to remaining sources of bias: the Mediterranean diet groups had more frequent clinic visits in the first three years, and the people assessing outcomes knew which diet each person was on.22 PREDIMED also looked at the brain. In a substudy at one centre in Barcelona, 447 cognitively healthy older adults at high risk of heart disease were randomised to one of the three diets. After a median of 4.1 years, 334 were retested. Compared with the control diet, the olive oil group did better on measures of frontal and overall thinking, and the nut group did better on memory, while all the thinking scores fell in the control group.23 A 2022 systematic review found that most studies in Mediterranean countries reported low or moderate adherence to the traditional diet,24 and a 2023 worldwide review of 57 studies with more than a million people found that adherence had fallen over the previous decade, although it remained highest in Mediterranean countries. 25 In a Greek study that followed 2,583 adults for ten years, almost half POLYPHENOLS 100 moved away from the diet, while 9% moved closer to it. 26 But the diet has not disappeared from the region. How Dark Should Chocolate Be? ““Chocolate’s good. Polyphenols, dark chocolate. So the number that you look for… is you want to go north of 80. That’s really 80%… Because the higher that is… the lower generally is going to be the sugar content.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““‘Pour in 60%,’ or it might have been 70%, of the white sugar into the bowl… I was like, wow, the white chocolate I eat is 70% sugar… And then milk chocolate was like half the size. It was about 50%.” Steven Bartlett on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Darker chocolate generally has more polyphenols and less sugar, and white chocolate usually has the most sugar. But 80% cocoa is a rule of thumb, not a tested cut-off, and the sugar figures vary by product. In an analysis of 12 dark chocolates, polyphenol content ranged from 10.55 to 39.82 mg per gram, and the higher the cocoa percentage on the label, the stronger the antioxidant activity. 27 Sugar falls as cocoa rises. In the same survey, chocolates with 40% to 49% cocoa contained 51 to 60 grams of carbohydrate per 100 grams, while those with 73% to 85% cocoa contained 19 to 33 grams.27 But cocoa percentage is only a rough guide. A study of 26 dark chocolates found that the type of cocoa bean, where it was grown and how it was processed also affected the polyphenol content. 28 Treating cocoa with alkali, as described in Chapter 6, can remove most of its POLYPHENOLS 101 flavanols.29 There is another caveat with dark chocolate. In a study of 139 single-origin chocolates, 16 had more cadmium, a toxic metal, than the EU limit, all of them made from South or Central American cocoa, and six were above a benchmark for lead.30 Large trials of cocoa flavanols themselves have not found benefits for thinking. In COSMOS-Mind, 2,262 older adults took a cocoa extract containing 500 mg of flavanols a day, or a placebo, for three years, and the extract had no effect on overall thinking.31 In a group of 573 people from the same trial who had detailed in-person tests over two years, there was also no overall benefit, although a possible benefit in people with poorer diets was noted for further study.32 A UK survey found chocolate sweets averaged about 47% sugar, but ranged from almost none to 75%. 33 A 2025 analysis of 30 chocolate products found between 30% and 48% sucrose.34 No study gives typical figures for white and milk chocolate separately, so the research cannot confirm 70% and 50%. 35 The label of a particular bar is the only reliable guide. POLYPHENOLS 102 09 Diabetes, Parkinson’s and Inflammation Dr Perlmutter returns to blood sugar and inflammation as common threads behind brain disease. Type 2 diabetes does raise the risk of Alzheimer’s and Parkinson’s, but by less than the figures he gives. The idea of Alzheimer’s as a slow “cytokine drizzle” has some support, although anti-inflammatory drugs have not prevented it. How Much Does Diabetes Raise Alzheimer’s Risk? “STEVEN BARTLETT “I’ve heard you say that people with type 2 diabetes have a 300 to 400% higher risk of Alzheimer’s.” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “Some studies have shown that. I think a number that we can absolutely support by more recent data is at least a 75 to 85% increased risk.” 103 IN SHORT A 2017 meta-analysis found that type 2 diabetes is linked to a 53% higher risk of Alzheimer’s. That is far below 300% to 400%, and below the 75% to 85% Dr Perlmutter gives as well. Diabetes is linked to Alzheimer’s, but the link is smaller than either figure. A 2017 meta-analysis of 17 studies with more than 1.7 million people found that diabetes was linked to a 53% higher risk of Alzheimer’s (relative risk 1.53). 1 A 2026 meta-analysis found a smaller increase of 36%, and in the studies that allowed for APOE4, the main gene variant linked to Alzheimer’s, the link was no longer statistically significant (hazard ratio 1.07).2 Large population studies give similar or lower figures. In everyone in Denmark aged over 65, type 2 diabetes was linked to a 13% higher risk of Alzheimer’s and a 48% higher risk of dementia of any kind, but nearly double the risk of vascular dementia, which is caused by reduced blood flow to the brain. 3 A 2025 study of 24,583 people with type 2 diabetes, each matched with a person without it, did find a 72% to 75% higher risk over up to 25 years, but it counted Alzheimer’s together with other dementias. 4 Genetic studies, which use inherited differences in the risk of diabetes to test for cause and effect, have mostly found no causal link with Alzheimer’s. A meta-analysis of 8 such studies found no significant link (odds ratio 1.02, range 1.00 to 1.04),5 and a 2024 study using large genetic datasets found the same (0.98, range 0.95 to 1.01).6 Across these studies, type 2 diabetes is linked to a higher risk of dementia, and especially of vascular dementia, but the increase for Alzheimer’s itself is usually between about 13% and 53%, not three to four times.1,3 DIABETES, PARKINSON’S AND INFLAMMATION 104 How Much Does Diabetes Raise Parkinson’s Risk? ““But what people may not be aware of is that even Parkinson’s disease is increased in the type 2 diabetic by about 85%.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Type 2 diabetes is linked to a higher risk of Parkinson’s, but the largest recent meta-analysis found a 27% higher risk, not 85%. The largest recent meta-analysis, of 15 studies with almost 30 million people and more than 86,000 cases of Parkinson’s, found that people with diabetes had a 27% higher risk. 7 Other meta-analyses have found increases from 15% to 38%.8–12 One 2024 meta-analysis found 59%, but its authors rated the certainty of that estimate as very low.13 Large single studies give similar figures, mostly between 19% and 41%.14–17 Risk does seem to rise with how long and how severe the diabetes is. In Korea, people who had had diabetes for five years or more had a 62% higher risk, 18 and a US study found a 75% higher risk in those who had had it for more than ten years. 16 In a Korean study that scored how severe diabetes was, risk ranged from 9% higher with one feature of severe disease to almost three times higher with six.19 Genetic studies, which help test whether diabetes itself causes the increase, give smaller figures. One found an 8% higher risk, 8 and a 2026 study found no overall increase.20 Most of the evidence is observational, so other factors may explain part of the link.7,13 DIABETES, PARKINSON’S AND INFLAMMATION 105 Do Microglia Destroy the Parkinson’s Brain? ““When you look at the Parkinson’s brain, and this data became evident in the late 1980s, you see activation of the microglial cells destroying that part of the brain called the substantia nigra, the black substance that makes dopamine.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Activated microglia were found in the part of the brain damaged in Parkinson’s from the late 1980s onwards. But it has not been shown that they are what destroys it in people. In Parkinson’s, the nerve cells that make dopamine, in a small dark area of the brainstem called the substantia nigra, gradually die. In 1988, researchers reported large numbers of activated microglia, the brain’s resident immune cells, in this area in every person with Parkinson’s they examined after death.21 Later brain-scan studies found signs of this in living patients. A 2006 study found signs of activated microglia across several brain regions, 22 and a 2020 study found them in the midbrain, where the substantia nigra lies, and in connected areas.23 Whether microglia are “destroying” this area is less certain. The authors of the 2020 study concluded that their results confirm a role for microglial activation in the disease.23 But in both studies, the amount of microglial activity was not linked to how severe the symptoms were or how long people had had the disease.22,23 That makes it difficult to say whether microglia drive the damage or respond to it. These scans use an indirect signal that is not unique to microglia, although a 2025 study in eight people with progressive supranuclear DIABETES, PARKINSON’S AND INFLAMMATION 106 palsy, a different disease involving tau, found that most of the signal came from microglia.24 Did a Contaminated Drug Cause Parkinson’s in a Day? ““The original work was fascinating. It was seen at Stanford University when suddenly people started showing up in the emergency room who developed Parkinson’s in one day from shooting an IV designer drug that was contaminated with something called MPTP. And that was a mitochondrial toxin. And it basically fried those cells in their brains that made dopamine, and it became a powerful research tool.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT MPTP did cause lasting Parkinson’s-like symptoms in drug users and changed Parkinson’s research. But the first case was reported in 1979, and no paper says symptoms appeared in one day. In 1983, doctors reported four people who had developed severe Parkinson’s-like symptoms after injecting an illegal drug, and tests found it was contaminated mainly with MPTP. 25 But this was not the first case. In 1979, doctors described a 23-year-old man who developed lasting symptoms after misusing a drug related to the painkiller pethidine. His symptoms persisted for 18 months, and the damage found in his brain after death matched that seen in Parkinson’s.26 Some details differ from the published accounts. Neither of the original papers says that symptoms appeared in a single day.25,26 DIABETES, PARKINSON’S AND INFLAMMATION 107 In the brain, MPTP is converted into a substance that builds up in mitochondria and blocks an essential step in energy production, starving and damaging the dopamine-making cells. A 2021 review describes MPTP as the basis of “the most validated and successful preclinical model of any neurological disease”.27 Can RNS60 Protect Mice from MPTP? ““I was at a conference last week where MPTP was used in laboratory mice to damage that part of the brain, to create Parkinson’s, and now they’ve developed an offset for that. They’re giving the mice MPTP, but giving them something else called RNS60, that was able to keep these mice from having that damage occur by activation of their microglial cells.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT RNS60 protected the brains of mice given MPTP. But it worked by reducing inflammatory molecules made by microglia, not by activating them, and there is no evidence it helps people with Parkinson’s. RNS60 is an experimental treatment. In a 2014 study, it reduced inflammatory signals in microglia exposed to MPTP’s toxic product in the laboratory, and in the brains of mice given MPTP. It protected the dopamine-making cells, restored chemical messengers in the brain and improved the mice’s movement.28 The mechanism differs from the one Dr Perlmutter describes. RNS60 did not protect the mice by activating their microglia. It reduced the production of inflammatory molecules by microglia grown in the laboratory and in the affected brain area of the mice.28 DIABETES, PARKINSON’S AND INFLAMMATION 108 RNS60 has not been tested in people with Parkinson’s. The human studies so far are in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), a different disease of the nervous system. A trial found no difference in the blood markers it measured, although some measures of breathing and swallowing favoured RNS60, and it seemed to be safe. 29 A later look at the same patients found they lived about six months longer on RNS60, but this was a post hoc analysis, done after the trial, and the result narrowly missed statistical significance (p = 0.0519). 30 In a 2025 trial in 82 people having treatment for a stroke, RNS60 was safe and limited the growth of the damaged area, but its effects on disability were not significant.31 Is Belly Fat a Source of Inflammation? ““You don’t want it around your organs. That’s called visceral fat. So when you’re 30 pounds overweight, over ideal body weight, that’s 30 of these… We used to consider fat as being pretty inert, just a resource for calories… But it is creating chemicals that have function throughout the body. And by and large, these are chemicals that are damaging. They’re pro-inflammatory chemicals called cytokines.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Visceral fat is active tissue that releases inflammatory signals into the body. But in one study, most of these signals came from other cells within fat tissue, not from the fat cells themselves. Visceral fat is the fat stored deep in the belly, around the organs, as opposed to the fat under the skin. Fat releases hormones and signalling molecules, and visceral fat releases more inflammatory ones than fat under the skin. In studies of fat tissue taken from people with DIABETES, PARKINSON’S AND INFLAMMATION 109 obesity, visceral fat released more of several cytokines, the chemical messengers of the immune system, than fat under the skin. 32,33 In one study, these substances made liver and muscle cells less responsive to insulin.33 In people with severe obesity, levels of one inflammatory cytokine were about 50% higher in the vein draining the gut, which collects blood from visceral fat, than in an artery in the arm.34 The one qualification concerns “by and large… damaging”, and Dr Perlmutter made it himself a moment later, when he said that these inflammatory chemicals are “not necessarily always bad” and that “we need inflammation”. In a 2004 study of fat tissue from people with obesity, more than 90% of the release of these signalling substances, apart from adiponectin and leptin, came from cells other than the fat cells themselves.32 So visceral fat tissue releases inflammatory substances, but much of this output comes from other cells within it. Is Alzheimer’s a “Cytokine Drizzle”? ““We need inflammation. If we suddenly were able to reduce all the inflammation in your body immediately, you would perish… It’s the excessive inflammation that gets us into trouble, or lower levels of inflammation over a longer period of time… I think that we should consider things like Alzheimer’s as not a cytokine storm, but as a cytokine drizzle.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Long-term, low-level inflammation is linked to a higher risk of dementia, and of Alzheimer’s in one study of APOE4 carriers. But trials of anti-inflammatory drugs have failed, so the disease cannot be reduced to a “cytokine drizzle”. DIABETES, PARKINSON’S AND INFLAMMATION 110 A “cytokine storm” is a sudden, extreme immune reaction. Dr Perlmutter’s image suggests that Alzheimer’s results from a mild version of this over many years. Some studies link the two. In the Framingham Offspring Study, people whose CRP, the blood marker of inflammation, was raised on repeated tests had a higher risk of Alzheimer’s if they also carried the APOE4 gene variant. In carriers without heart disease, long-term inflammation was linked to a hazard ratio of 6.63. 35 Pooled studies give a less specific result. A 2018 meta- analysis of 13 population studies in six countries found that CRP (hazard ratio 1.37), interleukin-6 (1.40) and three other inflammatory markers were each linked to a higher risk of dementia of any kind, but none was significantly linked to Alzheimer’s itself, and the authors concluded that these markers are not specific for Alzheimer’s.36 But other evidence suggests a more complicated relationship.
