A genetic study of 41 inflammatory signals in the blood found no consistent sign that they cause Alzheimer’s, which suggests some may follow the disease or be linked to it for other reasons. 37 And anti- inflammatory drugs have not prevented it. In the ADAPT trial, 2,528 older people were given naproxen, celecoxib or a placebo. The drugs did not protect thinking, and early results even suggested they made things slightly worse. Later follow-up suggested that any benefit, if there is one, would depend on the stage of the disease. 38 A 2024 meta- analysis of nine randomised trials found that anti-inflammatory painkillers neither treated Alzheimer’s nor protected against it. 39 A Dutch observational study did find a link: in 11,745 people followed for an average of 14.5 years, those who had taken these drugs for more than two years had a lower risk of dementia, with a hazard ratio of 0.88, but studies of this kind cannot rule out other explanations.40 So inflammation is involved, but Alzheimer’s is not established as a slow excess of cytokines that can be fixed by dampening inflammation. The drug trials tested only one approach at one stage, so they do not rule out a role for the brain’s immune system. DIABETES, PARKINSON’S AND INFLAMMATION 111 10 Alcohol, Hormones and Other Triggers Dr Perlmutter lists the everyday factors he believes shift microglia from protecting the brain to harming it: alcohol, high blood sugar, stress, sitting too much, poor sleep, loneliness and polluted air. He also links the drop in oestrogen at menopause to women’s higher rate of Alzheimer’s. Genetic studies suggest that no amount of alcohol benefits the brain. For most of the other factors, the effects on microglia have been shown in animals but not in people. Is Any Amount of Alcohol Safe for the Brain? ““There was a lot of data that showed that there was what we call a U- shaped curve in terms of dementia risk… With consumption, the risk went down a bit: one glass of wine per day for women, two glasses of wine per day for men. But then the U comes back up… And it turns out that reassessing that indicates that really any alcohol consumption is probably not good for the brain.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 112 IN SHORT Older studies did show a U-shaped curve, with light drinkers at the lowest risk. Newer genetic studies suggest this apparent benefit is not real, although they cannot set an exact safe limit. Studies that compare drinkers with non-drinkers still tend to find a U shape. In a 2025 study of people in UK Biobank and the US Million Veteran Program, non-drinkers and heavy drinkers both had a higher risk of dementia than light drinkers. Heavy drinkers, at more than 40 drinks a week, had a hazard ratio of 1.41. 1 A ratio above 1 means a higher risk. The same study also used Mendelian randomisation. This method uses gene variants that affect how much people tend to drink, which are fixed from birth, to test whether alcohol itself is the cause. It found no protective dip. Instead, risk rose steadily with genetically predicted drinking. The researchers also found that people who went on to develop dementia tended to cut down on alcohol in the years before, which could make light drinking look protective when it is not. 1 In a 2024 study of current drinkers, the observational data showed the lowest risk at 12.2 units a week, but a genetic analysis did not find a significant non-linear causal relationship. 2 A 2026 meta-analysis of 20 studies with 78,657 people looked at thinking, not dementia. Heavy drinking, of 67 grams of alcohol a day or more, was linked to poorer thinking, while the evidence for a protective effect of light to moderate drinking “remains uncertain”.3 Is Alcohol Toxic to Neurons and Microglia? ““Alcohol itself is a neurotoxin… [How does alcohol harm the brain?] It is directly toxic to neurons. And from my perspective… it helps polarise our microglial cells away from being supportive.” ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 113 Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO “STEVEN BARTLETT “You said in your book, on page 102, about this study where they found that microglia exposed to binge-level alcohol for 24 hours showed a 15% decrease in their ability to clean out beta-amyloid.” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “…I can’t afford a 15% reduction. They’re already downregulated, towards M1, because of my age.” IN SHORT Even moderate drinking is linked to a smaller brain, and alcohol activates microglia and worsens Alzheimer’s changes in animals. A study in cells did find that a high concentration of alcohol reduced how much amyloid microglia took up, by 16%, but a study in older rats found the opposite in females. Brain scans show that alcohol is linked to harm even at moderate levels. In the Whitehall II study of British civil servants, people who drank 14 to 21 units a week had higher odds of shrinkage in the hippocampus, an important memory area, compared with non- drinkers, with an odds ratio of 3.4. 4 In UK Biobank, less grey matter was seen at 7 to 14 units a week, 5 and another analysis of 36,678 adults found changes in brain structure at only one to two units a day.6 These studies show a link with brain harm, but they do not show directly that alcohol kills neurons in people. In rodents, alcohol activates an immune sensor in microglia called TLR4 and increases inflammatory signals in the brain. 7,8 In mice bred without TLR4, these effects largely disappeared.8 A 2018 experiment exposed microglia taken from rats to a high concentration of alcohol for 24 hours. Alcohol changed the activity of 312 genes, including genes involved in engulfing material, and reduced ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 114 how much amyloid the cells took up. The average amount of amyloid taken up fell by 16%, and the authors stated that this concentration is reached in people after binge drinking or in heavy drinkers. 9 This was a study of isolated cells, not of animals or people. A 2022 study in older rats that drank alcohol for about four months found the opposite in females, with more microglia and slightly less amyloid, and no effect in males.10 Studies in mice point in the same general direction. Repeated binge drinking worsened amyloid build-up in one Alzheimer’s mouse model after a month, 11 and in older female mice it increased inflammation, microglial activity, tau changes, cell death and memory problems. 12 But in another mouse model, alcohol worsened amyloid and memory while microglia seemed to engulf amyloid as well as before, which argues against a simple drop in clearance.13 Do Stress, Inactivity, Poor Sleep and Loneliness Activate Microglia? “STEVEN BARTLETT “And alcohol flips the M2 into the M1.” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “Alcohol, high blood sugar, inflammation, stress, being sedentary, lack of restorative sleep, all the things, even social isolation.” IN SHORT Stress, poor sleep and isolation change microglia in animals. Evidence in people is emerging for sleep but limited for stress and isolation. As Chapter 3 explains, sorting microglia into “good” M2 and “bad” M1 types is now seen as too simple. But the broader question is whether these factors activate microglia. ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 115 For stress, a review of 18 animal studies found that stress consistently increased microglial activity. In all 11 studies of the hippocampus, a marker of microglia was 20% to 200% higher. 14 In people, a brain-scan study of healthy people with social and psychological risk factors found no clear difference in microglial activity between higher- and lower-risk groups.15 For inactivity, the evidence is mostly indirect. In older mice that did not exercise, microglia showed signs of ageing and altered energy use, and exercise reduced these changes. 16 No study was found that tested whether sitting still activates microglia. For sleep, a mouse study found that ongoing sleep restriction, but not a single episode of sleep loss, made microglia look more active and increased how much synaptic material they engulfed. 17 In people, a 2025 brain-scan study of 39 healthy adults aged 50 to 81 measured microglial density with a scan marker called TSPO. Shorter sleep, more napping, daytime tiredness and not getting enough sleep were linked to higher levels in part of the frontal cortex. The authors concluded that too little sleep was linked to higher microglial density in frontal and striatal areas, and too much sleep in the limbic system, a set of deep brain areas. The study looked at one point in time, and the authors called for studies that follow people over time to clarify which comes first.18 For social isolation, studies in mice show changes in microglia that differ by brain region and sex. 19 No study was found that showed this in people. ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 116 Do Microglia Destroy Synapses and the Blood- Brain Barrier? ““Those microglial cells create havoc in the brain. They destabilise our neurons. They destabilise the blood-brain barrier… Most fundamentally, they destroy synapses. So neuroscience is now looking at Alzheimer’s as, a new term, a synaptopathy: a disease of synapses, a loss of synapses.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Microglia drive synapse loss in mouse models of Alzheimer’s, and loss of synapses is an early feature of Alzheimer’s. But no study was found showing that microglia break down the blood-brain barrier in people. As Chapter 4 describes, a widely cited mouse study found that small clumps of amyloid caused synapse loss through the complement system, and blocking it protected the synapses.20 A later study in another mouse model found microglia engulfing about seven times more synaptic material than in normal mice. 21 A review also says that loss of synapses is an early feature of Alzheimer’s and tracks decline in thinking better than plaques or tangles do.22 The evidence for the blood-brain barrier is more limited. This barrier is a tightly sealed lining of blood vessels that controls what passes from the blood into the brain. A review describes how microglia interact with the cells that make up the barrier and can affect how it works.23 But no study has shown that microglia are what break it down in people. Most of the evidence for cause and effect in this area comes from mice. ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 117 Why Do Women Have More Alzheimer’s? ““Why do women have twice the risk of Alzheimer’s in comparison to men? Is it because they live longer? Not really.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT More women than men have Alzheimer’s, and living longer explains part of this, although other factors, including hormones and genes, may also play a part. A clear doubling of risk at every age has not been shown. Women make up most people with Alzheimer’s, but a 2023 review states that it is important to work out how far sex differences in Alzheimer’s are due to differences in longevity, survival bias and other health conditions. 24 A Swedish study of 16,926 twins aged 65 and over found that women had higher rates of Alzheimer’s than men, with the gap opening at around age 80. The authors concluded this fitted with women living to older ages. 25 A 2022 meta-analysis of 205 studies in 43 countries found no difference between women and men in the rate of new cases of dementia or Alzheimer’s at the same age, except after 90, although more women were living with Alzheimer’s at most ages. 26 A 2023 meta-analysis of individual participant data from 21 cohorts across six continents did find a higher risk of dementia in women, with a hazard ratio of 1.12. 27 A 2024 review of the UK Cognitive Function and Ageing Studies found that the rate of new dementia cases at the same age was similar in women and men, and suggested that higher death rates in men may explain findings of greater risk in women. 28 And a 2025 study of 4,726 people with repeated brain scans found that men showed greater decline than women in more brain regions, and concluded that sex differences in brain ageing are unlikely to explain why Alzheimer’s is diagnosed more often in women.29 ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 118 These studies point to longevity and survival as part of the explanation.25,28 The 2023 review also notes that medical, genetic, psychosocial and behavioural factors, as well as hormones, can affect the risk of Alzheimer’s differently in women and men.24 No study found a clear twofold difference in risk at the same age, or worked out how much of the gap is due to survival and how much to biology. Does Menopause Tag Synapses for Removal? ““What has recently been identified is that this sudden drop of oestrogen, particularly one oestrogen called oestradiol, at menopause causes the synapses to be labelled with signalling molecules called complement. That is basically what is called an ‘eat me’ signal.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Falling oestradiol has been linked to complement tags on synapses and to synapse loss in laboratory and animal studies. This has not been shown directly in women going through menopause. One study found higher levels of a modified form of a complement protein in the brains of women with Alzheimer’s than in men. In laboratory experiments, the researchers linked falling oestradiol to this protein, to microglia engulfing more synapses, to synapse loss and to memory problems.30 This supports a pathway from falling oestradiol to synapses being tagged for removal. But the study did not show that menopause itself causes this in women. A rat study is consistent with it. In middle-aged rats whose ovaries were removed, which mimics menopause, genes for microglia and for complement proteins became more active in the hippocampus, and giving oestradiol reduced this.31 ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 119 So the idea is supported by laboratory and animal work, and by findings in brain tissue after death, but no study has followed women through menopause to show that their synapses become tagged. If falling oestradiol were a major cause of Alzheimer’s in women, replacing it might be expected to lower the risk. A 2025 meta-analysis of studies with more than a million women found no clear link between hormone therapy and dementia either way, whatever the timing, type or duration. 32 Randomised trials have tested this more directly. In WHIMS, 4,532 women aged 65 and over were given oestrogen plus progestin or a placebo, and hormone therapy doubled the risk of probable dementia (hazard ratio 2.05, range 1.21 to 3.48), with Alzheimer’s the most common type in both groups. 33 In KEEPS- Cog, 693 recently menopausal women with an average age of 52.6 were given one of two types of hormone therapy or a placebo for up to four years, and neither type affected thinking.34 Does Polluted Air Activate Microglia? ““These cells are activated for the reasons we’ve talked about: these metabolic changes, the inflammation, the exposure to polluted air, breathing these PM2.5 particles from wildfires, all of the things that are known to activate this microglial cell and cause it to do that.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Fine particle pollution activates microglia in laboratory and animal studies, and it is linked to dementia in people, including pollution from wildfire smoke. But the evidence that this works through microglia in people is indirect. ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 120 PM2.5 refers to particles of air pollution smaller than 2.5 micrometres across, small enough to reach deep into the lungs. A 2021 review of laboratory, animal and clinical evidence concludes that these particles can activate microglia and promote inflammation and Alzheimer’s-like changes in the brain. 35 In a 2021 study using a model of human brain tissue grown in the laboratory, exposure to PM2.5 drew in microglia and later made them much more inflammatory. But the particles did not activate the microglia directly at first, which suggests that other brain cells passed on the signal.36 In people, long-term exposure is linked to dementia. A 2025 meta- analysis of 28 long-term studies found at least a 14% higher risk of dementia at common pollution levels, compared with very clean air, and a significant link with Alzheimer’s.37 Wildfire smoke has been studied directly. In a 2024 study of more than 1.2 million older adults in southern California, each small rise in long- term exposure to fine particles from wildfires was linked to higher odds of a dementia diagnosis, with an odds ratio of 1.18, compared with 1.01 for the same rise in other fine particles. 38 A US national study also found that fine particles from wildfires and from farming were the sources most consistently linked to dementia.39 A 2026 review of smoke from military burn pits, wildfires and other burning describes how these exposures converge on the activation of microglia and other brain cells. 40 But these human studies are observational. In a small brain scan study of 20 World Trade Center responders, longer time spent at the site was linked to a higher scan signal of brain immune activity, but this marker is not specific to microglia, and no study was found showing directly that breathing smoke activates microglia in living people.41 ALCOHOL, HORMONES AND OTHER TRIGGERS 121 11 Flickering Light and Amyloid Drugs Dr Perlmutter is enthusiastic about light and sound pulsing 40 times a second as a way to restore the brain’s natural rhythms and get microglia to clear away amyloid, and he keeps such a device on his desk. The early mouse studies reported benefits, but later studies have not always repeated them, and the largest completed human trial missed its main goal. He considers the approved anti- amyloid drugs ineffective and dangerous. A 2026 Cochrane review found little to no difference from placebo in thinking or dementia severity, and they can cause brain swelling and bleeding, although some of the figures he gives are higher than the published ones. 122 Can 40 Hz Light and Sound Clear Amyloid in Mice? ““There’s a doctor, Li-Huei Tsai, at MIT… She’s demonstrated that the non-Alzheimer’s brain has a beautiful background rhythm called gamma oscillation… The Alzheimer’s brain has a disrupted rhythm… in a rodent that is geared to get Alzheimer’s… a light flashing at 40 times per second, 40Hz… restores the background gamma.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““‘40Hz sensory stimulation… has emerged as one of the most intriguing non-invasive approaches to recruiting microglia to clean up the brain.’… ‘The stimulated microglia actively engulf and clear both soluble and aggregated forms of amyloid beta, as well as pathological phosphorylated tau, in animal models.’… ‘In animal models, just one hour of daily 40Hz light stimulation drove significant drops in plaque and tangle burden.’” Steven Bartlett on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The original mouse studies found that 40 Hz light and sound reduced amyloid and tau and changed how microglia behaved. But some later studies could not repeat this, and a 2024 study found that the removal of amyloid depended on the flow of fluid through the brain. Brain cells fire together in rhythms. Gamma rhythms, at 30 to 80 cycles a second, play a key role in sensory processing and thinking. 1 In a 2016 study, Professor Li-Huei Tsai’s team found that gamma rhythms were already weakened in a mouse model of Alzheimer’s before plaques formed or memory declined. Using a genetic technique to drive certain brain cells at 40 Hz, but not at other frequencies, FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 123 lowered amyloid, increased the activity of genes linked to changes in the shape of microglia, and brought more microglia into contact with amyloid. Light flickering at 40 Hz lowered amyloid in the visual part of the brain in mice before plaques had formed, and reduced plaques in older mice that already had them.2 Later work extended this. In 2019, the same group found that seven days of 40 Hz sound drove brain rhythms in the hearing part of the brain and in the hippocampus, improved memory and reduced amyloid in mice, with changes in microglia, astrocytes and blood vessels. It also reduced the abnormal form of tau in a second mouse model. Only the combination of light and sound, not either alone, made microglia cluster and reduced amyloid in the prefrontal cortex. 3 A 2024 study from the same group found that combined 40 Hz light and sound increased the flow of fluid through the brain, and that when this flow was blocked, the amyloid was no longer removed. The authors concluded that the brain’s fluid clearance system is recruited to remove amyloid.4 The results have not always been repeated. A 2023 study by another group, using detailed recordings from deep in the brain, found that 40 Hz flickering light did not drive gamma rhythms in two mouse models of Alzheimer’s, and found no reliable change in plaques, amyloid levels or microglia. 5 Other groups have reported lower amyloid in mice after 40 Hz ultrasound or combined light and sound. 6,7 A 2025 review noted that recent studies in mouse models report contradictory findings.8 The claim that 40 Hz reliably recruits microglia to clear amyloid and tau has not been consistently repeated, and results in mice do not show that it helps patients. FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 124 Does 40 Hz Stimulation Help People with Alzheimer’s? ““What she found was that 40Hz light and 40Hz sound (she has a device that does this) restores this gamma oscillation in the brain and reverts microglial cells back to being supportive… She’s actually done interventional trials in patients with existing Alzheimer’s disease, and her results are better than any drug that targets beta-amyloid.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Small human studies show that 40 Hz light and sound is safe and hint at some benefits. But the largest completed trial missed its main goal, a larger trial has not yet reported its results, and no study was found showing it works better than anti-amyloid drugs. The early human studies were small. In a 2022 pilot study of 15 people with mild Alzheimer’s, daily 40 Hz light and sound was well tolerated and drove brain rhythms at that frequency. After three months, the treated group had less brain shrinkage, better recall of faces and names and more regular daily rhythms than the control group. The authors called these findings preliminary and noted the small numbers and missing data.9 In the OVERTURE trial, early results from 22 people suggested better night-time sleep and that daily activities were maintained.10 The full OVERTURE report, a six-month trial in 76 people with mild to moderate Alzheimer’s that compared the device with a fake version, found it safe, with none of the brain swelling seen with anti-amyloid drugs. Its main measures were safety and tolerability, and it was not designed to show whether the device works. It did not beat the fake device on its main clinical measure, which combined thinking and daily function, or on two other standard measures of dementia and FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 125 thinking. Some secondary measures, including daily activities, a standard memory and thinking test and brain volume, showed less decline that reached nominal significance. 11 A 2025 meta-analysis of 11 controlled studies with 341 people found significant changes in brain structure but no significant improvement in thinking or daily living, and concluded that its effect on these remains to be established. 12 Another 2025 meta-analysis, of eight studies with 291 people that also included stimulation applied directly to the head, reported a large benefit in thinking.13 The longest follow-up so far comes from the 2022 pilot study. Five of its participants used the device for an hour a day for two years, and no adverse events occurred. The three women with late-onset Alzheimer’s declined less on standard measures of thinking and daily function than similar patients in large research databases, and in the two people whose blood was tested, a blood marker of tau fell by 47% and 19%. 14 But this was five people compared with outside records, not with a control group. A larger trial, HOPE, is testing the OVERTURE device for 12 months in people with mild to moderate Alzheimer’s at 60 sites in the United States, and its results have not yet been reported. 15 This information comes from a conference abstract, not a peer-reviewed paper. A 2026 review of 20 completed studies of 40 Hz stimulation, including stimulation applied directly to the head, reported it to be safe and well tolerated, with possible benefits for how brain regions connect, brain shrinkage, thinking and sleep. It concluded that small samples, differences in methods and stimulation settings, and short follow-up limit the ability to draw definitive conclusions about whether it works.16 How do the studies differ? A 2025 review of 40 Hz light concluded that, despite their limitations, initial clinical findings provide a rationale for continued investigation. 8 A 2026 review described marked methodological heterogeneity, FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 126 including inconsistent stimulation settings, small samples and a lack of randomised trials across several centres.17 The studies do not all deliver the same thing. Another 2026 review described 40 Hz stimulation by sound, by light, by electrical or magnetic stimulation applied to the head, and by several senses combined, and noted that the human evidence comes largely from small, often uncontrolled studies.18 The light itself varies. Earlier animal work used 40 Hz stroboscopic light. A Danish group, noting that an hour a day of it can be strenuous, tested “invisible spectral flicker”, which they described as “essentially imperceivable”. In 13 healthy volunteers it drove 40 Hz brain activity, but less strongly than stroboscopic light. 19 A 2026 review of 22 studies in healthy adults found that the type of stimulation, its frequency and colour, and differences between people all shape the brain’s response.1 In young adults, red light, followed by white light, drove the strongest rhythms.20 In older adults, brighter light produced stronger rhythms than dimmer light, and light flickering at 32 or 34 Hz produced stronger rhythms than light at 38 or 40 Hz. 21 In about two thirds of older participants, the frequency their brain responded to best was 32 or 34 Hz.22 Studies also disagree on how far the rhythm reaches. In mice, recordings from neurons found that the 40 Hz rhythm was strongly followed in the relay station between the eye and the brain, more weakly in the visual cortex, and not at all in the hippocampus, the memory centre. 23 The 2023 mouse study described above also found weak responses in the hippocampus.5 In people, recordings from electrodes placed inside the brains of 11 people with epilepsy found that 40 Hz light did reach the hippocampus and areas in the temporal and frontal lobes.24 The length of treatment also differs. The original mouse studies used a single hour or seven days of daily sessions, 2,3 but a 2023 study that gave mice 40 Hz light for an hour a day for five weeks found no fall in FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 127 amyloid.25 In people, the 2022 pilot study gave daily stimulation for three months, OVERTURE for six months and the extension study for two years. 9,11,14 A single 30 minute session of light and sound drove brain rhythms but did not improve perception, attention or memory.26 The human trials also differ in design. An early study of 10 people with mild cognitive impairment due to Alzheimer’s used a delayed start design.27 The 2022 pilot study, in 15 people with mild Alzheimer’s, was single blind.9 OVERTURE, in 76 people with mild to moderate Alzheimer’s, was double blind and compared the device with a fake version designed not to produce 40 Hz brain rhythms. 11 The Danish trial was double blind and compared invisible flicker with a placebo. 28 The trials measured different outcomes. OVERTURE’s main clinical measure combined thinking and daily function, 11 while the pilot study assessed safety, compliance and brain rhythms, with memory for faces and names among its exploratory measures. 9 A 2025 meta-analysis of 11 studies found high heterogeneity between them. 12 A 2026 review noted that later work found either no effect on brain rhythms or even more amyloid, revealing a strong dependence on the stage of the disease, the state of the brain’s networks and the stimulation settings.29 No study was found comparing 40 Hz stimulation directly with anti- amyloid drugs. The drug trials were also far larger and used different measures. So the research so far cannot show whether the results are “better than any drug that targets beta-amyloid”. FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 128 Does a 40 Hz Desk Light Protect the Brain? ““I have a 40Hz light generator on my desk… According to the science, it is keeping my microglia doing what they need to do… I keep a device right by my computer screen, and it’s passively shining that light into my eyes. Can I prove that it’s doing anything wonderful for me? No. But am I a believer in the science? You bet I am.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT No study was found testing whether a 40 Hz light on a desk protects microglia or prevents Alzheimer’s in healthy people. The microglial findings come from mice, and have not always been repeated. Dr Perlmutter is open that he cannot prove the device is helping him. Studies in healthy volunteers show that flickering light can drive brain rhythms under experimental conditions, and that this depends on the colour, brightness and speed of the flicker. In young adults, light flickering at 34 to 38 Hz drove stronger rhythms than light at 40 to 50 Hz, and the brightest light caused more moderate to severe side effects.20 A 2026 trial in 33 healthy older adults, using 40 Hz light without visible flicker, found no difference in brain rhythms between groups after 1 or 28 sessions. It reported possible improvements in some memory measures, and the light was well tolerated.30 The evidence about microglia comes from mouse models of Alzheimer’s,2,3,7 and as described above, not all studies have found it. 5, 25 No study was found that measured microglia in healthy people using a desk light. A 2022 review by the Epilepsy Foundation found that light flashes, patterns or colour changes can provoke seizures in up to 1 in 4,000 people, and that flashes brighter than 20 candelas per square metre at 3 to 60 per second, filling at least 10% to 25% of the field of vision, are a risk.31 FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 129 Do Anti-Amyloid Drugs Work? ““We’re going to put you on an IV therapy every two weeks and hope for the best.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO ““The beta-amyloid drugs are the FDA-approved treatment, targeting beta-amyloid. And I make it very clear in my recent book that these drugs are, for the most part, ineffective and significantly dangerous. And my vote is that they are not to be used.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Lecanemab and donanemab slow decline slightly over 18 months, by less than the amount usually considered noticeable to patients. They can cause brain swelling and bleeding. Lecanemab and donanemab are antibodies that target amyloid in the brain. In the main lecanemab trial, the drug was given every two weeks.32 After 18 months, people on lecanemab had declined by 1.21 points on a standard 18-point dementia scale, compared with 1.66 points on placebo, a difference of 0.45 points. Other measures also favoured the drug, and amyloid on brain scans fell by 59.1 centiloids more than on placebo. 32 Donanemab is given every four weeks, not every two. In its main trial, after 76 weeks, people on donanemab had declined by 1.72 points on the same scale, compared with 2.42 points on placebo, a difference of 0.7 points.33 Both trials met their main goals.32,33 Dr Perlmutter later put his view in context, saying that “when I say that the drugs are ineffective, again, it’s in the context of hope that there will be effective drugs”. A meta- analysis of 19 publications with 23,202 people found that the FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 130 improvements with these antibodies, including lecanemab and donanemab, were all below the smallest difference that patients and doctors would usually notice.34 The drug companies’ longer follow-up studies, of up to three years, report that the benefit of lecanemab continued to accrue and that the clinical benefits of donanemab increased. But after the first 18 months, there was no longer a randomised placebo group. The lecanemab study compared people who started the drug early with people who started it later, and the donanemab study also compared treated patients with untreated people from a separate research study. 35,36 A 2026 analysis of the same data found that, in both trials, people who had taken the drug from the start declined faster once the blinded phase ended, at about the same rate as the placebo group in each trial had. People who started the drug later showed no meaningful change between the two phases.37 These drugs can cause amyloid-related imaging abnormalities (ARIA), which appear on scans as brain swelling or small bleeds. In the donanemab trials, swelling with symptoms was reported in 5.8% of people treated, and serious swelling in 1.5%. 38 In the lecanemab trial, swelling occurred in 12.6% of people. 32 A 2023 review of these drugs reports that bleeds occurred in 17.3%, and that in people with two copies of the APOE4 gene variant, bleeds occurred in 39% and swelling in 32.6%. 39 In the donanemab trial, 24% had swelling, and there were three deaths linked to treatment, compared with one on placebo. 33 A 2023 meta-analysis of 31 trials of anti-amyloid drugs also found that the antibodies that cause ARIA sped up the enlargement of the brain’s fluid-filled spaces, the ventricles, by 38.7% compared with placebo, and that the more often a drug caused ARIA, the more the ventricles enlarged. The authors projected that people with mild cognitive impairment treated with these drugs would reach brain volumes typical of Alzheimer’s dementia about eight months earlier than if untreated, and concluded that the drugs have the potential to compromise long-term brain health.40 FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 131 What Did the Cochrane Review Find? ““This Cochrane analysis looked at 20,342 individuals in 17 different studies, 18 months each study… They found that the drugs basically have no meaningful effect in slowing the progression, much less leading to improvement. And that about 25% of the individuals receiving these drugs either experienced haemorrhage into their brain or brain swelling, and some even died… The benefit was, in the words of the Cochrane analysis, trivial.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The 2026 Cochrane review found little to no difference in thinking or dementia severity, and Cochrane’s announcement described the effects as “absent or trivial”. But brain swelling affected about 119 in every 1,000 people treated, or about 12%, compared with 12 in 1,000 on placebo, and the review did not find a clear increase in deaths. A 2026 Cochrane review included a meta-analysis that pooled 17 trials with 20,342 people with early Alzheimer’s, testing seven different anti- amyloid antibodies, with most results at 18 months. 41 It found little to no difference from placebo in thinking or in the severity of dementia. 41 Cochrane’s announcement of the review described these effects as “absent or trivial”.42 Two details differ from Dr Perlmutter’s figures. The review found that brain swelling occurred in 107 more people in every 1,000 treated than on placebo. 41 A 2026 summary of the review gives this as 119 in every 1,000 people treated, or about 12%, compared with 12 in 1,000 on placebo.43 Results for brain bleeds varied too much between trials to be combined. 41 The review concluded, with high certainty, that the drugs do not increase deaths or serious side effects at 18 months. Its FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 132 best estimate was 2 more deaths for every 1,000 people treated, with a range from 3 fewer to 11 more.41 The review pooled seven antibodies. The 2026 summary of the review noted that four of them cleared no plaque or cleared it incompletely, and concluded that the class average is not an estimate for any single drug. In the two approved drugs, dementia severity differed by 0.45 and 0.67 points, larger than the class average but below thresholds of clinical importance. 43 All the trials in the review were funded by drug companies.41 FLICKERING LIGHT AND AMYLOID DRUGS 133 12 The Gut, From Birth Onwards Dr Perlmutter traces the health of the brain back to the earliest stages of life, and to the gut. The study of sugar rationing in post- war Britain that he describes found a lower risk of dementia, although by less than he suggests. Babies acquire their gut bacteria at birth, and caesarean birth changes them. The links he draws from gut bacteria and indigestion drugs to the brain rely mostly on animal studies and associations. 134 Did Sugar Rationing Lower Later Alzheimer’s Risk? ““It was done looking at women who conceived in the year 1953… here in England, and whether they had sugar rationing or they didn’t have sugar rationing at that time. Sugar rationing from World War II was still in place in 1953… the women who had sugar rationing… through pregnancy and then the first couple of years of life: the risk of that child, now that that child is 60, 70, 80 years old, developing Alzheimer’s was reduced by 40%.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Studies of the end of sugar rationing in 1953 link less sugar early in life to a lower risk of dementia later on, by about 23% for dementia and 28% for Alzheimer’s in the main study. One newer study reported a larger effect. Sugar rationing in the UK ended in September 1953, so children conceived shortly before then spent their time in the womb and their first months or years under rationing, while those conceived later did not. Researchers used this as a natural experiment, comparing people in UK Biobank born either side of the change. In the main study, published in the journal Neurology, people exposed to rationing in the womb and through their first one to two years had about a 23% lower risk of dementia (hazard ratio 0.77) and a 28% lower risk of Alzheimer’s (hazard ratio 0.72). Their Alzheimer’s began 2.87 years later, on average. Early rationing was also associated with more grey matter, fewer white matter changes on brain scans, and better processing speed and reasoning. 1 In the same study, type 2 diabetes and high blood pressure together explained 25.5% of the link with dementia.1 THE GUT, FROM BIRTH ONWARDS 135 A second study reported a 27% lower risk of dementia and a 46% lower risk of Alzheimer’s in people exposed during their first 1,000 days after conception.2 These studies are natural experiments using observational data, not randomised trials. Does Sugar Change Gut Bacteria? ““I think sugar in and of itself, raising blood sugar, compromises brain function, and even the genesis of the brain. But I think sugar also changes the milieu, the makeup of the gut bacteria… Higher levels of dietary sugar change the array, the function, the diversity of the bacteria and other organisms that live within the gut, like yeasts, for example.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Reviews report that added sugars can change the mix of gut bacteria. But results differ between studies. As Dr Perlmutter acknowledged, the rationing study did not look at gut bacteria. A 2025 review concludes that added sugars can change the diversity of gut bacteria, favouring those that feed on sugar and reducing those that produce short-chain fatty acids, compounds made when bacteria break down fibre. 3 A 2020 review concluded that high sugar intake may shift the balance of gut bacteria towards more inflammatory properties.4 In a small study of 12 healthy women, a large daily dose of fructose from syrup reduced some beneficial bacteria, and had a different effect from the same amount of fructose eaten as fruit.5 But the 2025 review observed inconsistent results across studies, which it suggested may be due to differences in the dose and form of sugar and in people’s gut bacteria.3 THE GUT, FROM BIRTH ONWARDS 136 Is the Womb Sterile? “STEVEN BARTLETT “And the baby has the mum’s gut microbiome.” DR DAVID PERLMUTTER “Actually, no, the baby does not really have much of a microbiome until such time as it’s born. The method of birth is really fundamentally important for the development of that newborn’s microbiome.” IN SHORT A study of placentas and a 2023 expert analysis suggest that a healthy womb has no resident bacteria, and that a baby acquires its microbiome at and after birth, although studies of the fluid around the baby still disagree. Some studies have claimed to find bacteria in the placenta or the unborn baby. In a study of placentas, researchers found no evidence of a placental microbiome, and concluded that most of the bacteria detected came from contamination in the laboratory or from labour and delivery. 6 A 2023 analysis by experts in several fields reached the same conclusion for the unborn baby, and pointed out that living communities of bacteria in a healthy foetus would be difficult to reconcile with basic immunology and with the fact that scientists can deliver animals completely free of germs.7 The placenta study did find one potentially harmful type of bacteria in about 5% of placentas collected before labour, and the authors concluded that the placenta has no microbiome but can contain potential pathogens.6 Studies of the amniotic fluid, the fluid around the baby, have reached different results. In a 2025 study that followed amniotic fluid from the second trimester to delivery, no microorganisms were detected in healthy pregnancies. 8 But a 2026 study of 154 samples, using strict THE GUT, FROM BIRTH ONWARDS 137 controls for contamination, grew microorganisms from 33.1% of them and found a low-level community of bacteria with little variety.9 Does Caesarean Birth Change a Baby’s Bacteria? ““Caesarean section versus vaginal delivery has a huge impact… Being born by caesarean section, that newborn will develop a microbiome based upon what was on the surgeon’s glove at the time that baby was delivered. In fact, there is research being done, I think at NYU… they take a swab of the vagina… that swab… is placed in the baby’s nose and its mouth to help restore that specific type of microbiome.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Caesarean birth changes a baby’s gut bacteria in the early months. But babies acquire bacteria from sources besides the surgeon’s glove, and the benefits of vaginal seeding remain uncertain. The way a baby is born shapes its gut bacteria. In one study, babies born by caesarean section lacked Bacteroides, a common group of gut bacteria, until 6 to 18 months of age.10 In another, the arrival of Bifidobacterium was significantly delayed. 11 A 2021 review reports that the overall mix largely normalises during the first year, 12 and a 2026 review that the differences are most pronounced in early infancy and may partly diminish with age.13 Babies born by caesarean are colonised by bacteria from the environment, including potential pathogens from the hospital, 12 and their bacteria are enriched in skin bacteria.14 One study found evidence that bacterial strains pass to the baby from the mother’s rectum rather than her vagina,10 and another found evidence of bacteria passing from the mother’s gut to vaginally born babies but not THE GUT, FROM BIRTH ONWARDS 138 to babies born by caesarean. 11 The largest study, the UK Baby Biome Study, analysed 1,679 gut samples from 596 babies. Those born by caesarean had disrupted transfer of their mothers’ Bacteroides strains, a common group of gut bacteria, and high levels of opportunistic bacteria linked to the hospital environment, such as Enterococcus, Enterobacter and Klebsiella. The same effects were seen, to a lesser extent, in vaginally born babies whose mothers had been given antibiotics and in babies who were not breastfed.15 The research Dr Perlmutter remembers was led by Maria Gloria Dominguez-Bello, then at NYU. In a pilot study, four babies born by caesarean were wiped with fluid from their mothers’ vaginas, and their bacteria became more similar to those of vaginally born babies, especially in the first week. 14 Later trials have given mixed results. A 2021 trial in 25 babies born by caesarean, who were given their mothers’ vaginal bacteria by mouth, found no difference in gut bacteria at one or three months. 16 A 2023 trial in 68 babies reported faster maturation of the gut bacteria and higher development scores at six months.17 The 2026 review considers vaginal seeding experimental, because its efficacy, safety and long-term clinical relevance have not been adequately established.13 Another 2026 review concluded that its efficacy remains uncertain, and found that only three of the studies it included had looked at the babies’ health outcomes.18 THE GUT, FROM BIRTH ONWARDS 139 Do Gut Bacteria Change the Brain? ““[What is the link between the microbiome and Alzheimer’s and dementia?] I think the link is quite profound… They’re making many types of short-chain fatty acids, for example, that are important for brain functionality. The microbiome, these organisms, are secreting chemicals that are changing gene expression throughout our bodies, and of course in the brain.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Gut bacteria and the compounds they make can affect the brain’s immune cells in animal studies. But no study was found showing that they cause Alzheimer’s in people, and in one mouse study these compounds increased amyloid plaques. Studies in mice show that gut bacteria shape the brain’s immune system. Mice raised completely free of germs have immature, poorly working microglia, and giving them gut bacteria partly restores them. Short-chain fatty acids, made by gut bacteria from fibre, were identified as important for this. 19 One of them, acetate, has since been shown to help microglia mature by changing which of their genes are active, at least in mice. 20 In another mouse study, a diet containing a soluble fibre raised short-chain fatty acids in the gut, and microglia taken from these mice released less TNF-α, an inflammatory chemical, when stimulated. Acetate and butyrate added to microglia in a dish also reduced their inflammatory response.21 In a mouse model of Alzheimer’s, germ-free mice had far fewer amyloid plaques, and giving them short-chain fatty acids increased plaques.22 No study was found showing that gut bacteria or their products cause Alzheimer’s in people. In one 2023 study of 164 cognitively normal people, the 49 who had early Alzheimer’s changes THE GUT, FROM BIRTH ONWARDS 140 on biomarker tests had a different mix of gut bacteria from the others, and the differences were linked to amyloid and tau but not to markers of damage to nerve cells. 23 Because the study looked at a single point in time, it cannot show which came first. Do Indigestion Drugs Harm the Gut and Brain? ““For example, proton pump inhibiting drugs… When you change the pH of the gut, you’re changing the organisms that live there and their function. And when you do that, it can lead to increased leakiness or permeability of the gut lining that sets the stage for inflammation in the body. Those inflammatory chemicals can… make their way into the brain… They polarise our supportive, loving M2 microglia to being destructive.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Proton pump inhibitors change gut bacteria. But no study was found showing that they make the gut leaky in people, and the link with dementia is inconsistent. Proton pump inhibitors (PPIs), such as omeprazole, are widely used drugs that reduce stomach acid. A 2020 review of 23 groups of patients found that PPIs were associated with moderate changes in gut bacteria, increasing some types and reducing others, such as Bifidobacteriaceae and Ruminococcaceae, although in most studies they did not affect diversity. 24 A 2023 meta-analysis of four studies found lower diversity and fewer bacteria that make short-chain fatty acids in people taking PPIs. 25 In people taking PPIs, bacteria from the mouth overgrow in the upper gut, 24 and a 2024 review suggests that THE GUT, FROM BIRTH ONWARDS 141 the movement of mouth bacteria into the gut may be the main way PPIs change gut bacteria.26 The next steps in the chain are not established. No study was found showing that PPIs make the gut lining leaky in people, or that changes in the gut caused by PPIs lead to inflammation in the brain. And the M2 and M1 labels for microglia are now seen as too simple, as Chapter 3 explains. The link with dementia is unclear. Early German studies found that people taking PPIs had a higher risk, with a hazard ratio of 1.38 in a study of 3,327 people aged 75 and over, 27 and 1.44 in a study of 73,679 people aged 75 and over. 28 A ratio above 1 means a higher risk. A 2018 review noted that unrecognised differences between the people who did and did not take the drugs may have affected these results. 29 A Finnish study that allowed for a three-year delay between taking PPIs and diagnosis found no link with Alzheimer’s (odds ratio 1.03).30 A 2026 meta-analysis of 18 studies with more than 6.3 million people found no statistically significant overall link (risk ratio 1.14, 95% confidence interval 0.98 to 1.33), although the studies varied greatly. A subgroup analysis found a higher risk in people aged 65 and over, with a risk ratio of 1.21.31 A 2024 genetic study found no robust evidence that PPIs cause dementia.32 THE GUT, FROM BIRTH ONWARDS 142 13 Head Injury Dr Perlmutter sees head injury as another situation in which microglia become active for good reason but then fail to settle back down. Brain scans do show that microglia can stay active for years after a serious injury, and chronic traumatic encephalopathy is common in the donated brains of former American football players. Whether microglia drive the damage is less clear, and the evidence on diabetes and recovery from head injury is mixed. Do Microglia Stay Active After a Head Injury? ““There’s a time and a place, when we have an acute injury, acute infection… When we have a head injury, for example, we activate our M1 microglia. But we have to be able to revert back… When we keep our microglia in their destructive phenotype… after the event has occurred, it leads to delays in improvement or even lack of improvement.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 143 IN SHORT Brain scans show that microglia can stay active for up to 17 years after a moderate or severe head injury. But it is not clear whether they cause further damage or are responding to it. In a 2011 brain-scan study, ten people examined at least 11 months after a moderate to severe head injury had raised signs of activated microglia in several deep brain areas, and these could be present up to 17 years after the injury. Higher levels in the thalamus, a relay centre deep in the brain, were associated with more severe problems with thinking.1 A 2017 review reports that this activation can persist for years, detectable both in scans and after death.2 What is less clear is whether the microglia are stuck in a “destructive” state. The same review notes that microglia activated early after injury may help restore balance in the brain, but that if they stay activated they release inflammatory molecules that can cause further damage. 2 In mice, activated microglia persisted for a year after injury, alongside a gradually expanding area of damage, loss of nerve cells in the hippocampus and loss of myelin. But the researchers noted that it is unclear whether the persistent inflammation causes the ongoing damage or is a response to it.3 A 2011 commentary on the brain-scan study noted its small size, and that the signal the scan detects is also found in other brain cells, notably activated astrocytes.4 HEAD INJURY 144 Does CTE Keep Progressing in Football Players? ““We know that these football players, for example, get this encephalopathy. When they bang their heads playing football too many times, they experience this progressive neurodegenerative condition that ultimately may kill them, but they’re not banging their heads anymore. Why so? Because the microglial cells that appropriately responded don’t revert back.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Chronic traumatic encephalopathy is very common in the donated brains of former American football players, and microglia are linked to its severity. But the brains were a convenience sample, not a random one, and no study was found showing that it keeps progressing after players retire. Chronic traumatic encephalopathy (CTE) is a brain disease linked to repeated blows to the head. It can only be diagnosed after death, by finding a particular pattern of abnormal tau in the brain. In a 2017 study of 202 former American football players whose brains were donated for research, CTE was found in 177, including 110 of 111 former professional players in the NFL. The authors described the brains as a convenience sample.5 A 2018 review describes this as by far the largest case series ever reported.6 A 2026 study counted all 1,712 former NFL players who died between 2008 and 2021. Of the 338 whose brains were examined, 315 had CTE. The researchers calculated that the share of all these players who had CTE at death lay somewhere between 18.5% and 98.7%. For 2016 to 2021, when brain donation was most frequent, they concluded that at minimum nearly a quarter had CTE.7 HEAD INJURY 145 In 66 former football players, more years of head impacts and more severe CTE were both linked to more activated microglia. Statistical modelling suggested that the effect of head impacts on tau was partly mediated through these activated microglia.8 In another study, microglia accumulated in step with the tau deposits typical of CTE, and an inflammatory chemical, CCL2, was linked to years of playing.9 A third found changes in microglia and blood vessel cells that appeared after repeated head impacts, before CTE tau built up, and that correlated with years of contact sport.10 This evidence comes from brains examined after death. No study was found showing that the disease always progresses once the head impacts stop, or that microglia failing to “revert back” is the reason. Can Any Bump to the Head Cause Harm? ““Right now I’m thinking about Muhammad Ali. How tragic was that? Right? That’s called pugilistic parkinsonism. Pugilist means a boxer. But any bump to the head, whether it’s playing soccer…” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Repeated blows to the head cause the boxing-related syndrome once called dementia pugilistica, and frequent heading in football (soccer) is linked to later problems with thinking. Ali’s own case could not be checked. The condition was first described in boxers. A 2011 review explains that it was termed dementia pugilistica, and that as evidence grew that it was not restricted to boxers, the term CTE became the most widely used. It involves problems with memory and thinking, changes in behaviour and personality, parkinsonism (the slowness and stiffness HEAD INJURY 146 seen in Parkinson’s) and, occasionally, motor neuron disease. The review notes that it was first seen in boxers, who receive numerous blows to the head.11 No study was found showing harm from every minor knock. But even one head injury recorded in hospital is linked to a higher risk of dementia, and the risk rises with more injuries. In a study of all 2.8 million people in Denmark aged 50 and over, a history of head injury was linked to a higher risk of dementia (hazard ratio 1.24) and of Alzheimer’s (1.16). One head injury was linked to a hazard ratio of 1.22, rising to 2.83 with five or more, and the risk was also higher than after fractures elsewhere in the body. 12 These were injuries serious enough to be recorded in hospital registers. Repeated impacts in football (soccer) have also been studied. In a Swedish study of 6,007 elite male footballers, neurodegenerative disease was more common than in a comparison group of 56,168 men, with a hazard ratio of 1.46, and so were Alzheimer’s and other dementias, with a hazard ratio of 1.62. A ratio above 1 means a higher risk. The risk was raised in outfield players (hazard ratio 1.50) but not in goalkeepers (1.07), although the study had no data on how often players headed the ball. Parkinson’s disease was less common among the footballers than among the comparison group, with a hazard ratio of 0.68.13 A 2025 meta-analysis found higher odds of dementia in professional footballers, with an odds ratio of 2.16, but no significant increase in Parkinson’s (1.14, 95% confidence interval 0.55 to 2.89). 14 In a study of 459 retired professional players, those who headed the ball more than 15 times a match had higher odds of later problems with thinking, with an odds ratio of 3.53.15 A 2026 meta-analysis of 28 datasets found that repeated heading was linked to poorer performance on tests of thinking, with an odds ratio of 1.67.16 The 2025 meta-analysis noted that these studies are often limited by their observational design. 14 In the study of 459 players, concussion involving memory loss was also linked to problems with thinking (odds HEAD INJURY 147 ratio 3.16). 15 No published medical study of Muhammad Ali’s case was found, so whether his Parkinson’s symptoms were caused by boxing cannot be confirmed here. Are Formula 1 Drivers at Risk of CTE? ““There was a study that came out last week that looked at F1 drivers as being at risk. The first F1 driver, I think, that developed CTE, chronic traumatic encephalopathy, made the news just a week ago.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The only published study of former racing drivers examined living people, so it could neither diagnose nor rule out CTE. No confirmed case in a Formula 1 driver could be found in the medical literature, which does not mean that one has never occurred. There is one published study of the brain health of former racing drivers, a 2026 study of 12 former professional drivers, most of whom had raced single-seaters or sports cars. All had had at least one concussion, with an average of 3.5 each. Three quarters had at least one abnormal eye movement, 58% at least one abnormality on a standard brain scan and 92% at least one abnormality on a scan of the brain’s white matter. None received a diagnosis of CTE, neurodegenerative disease or dementia, and the authors stated that no causal links can be established.17 Because CTE can only be confirmed by examining the brain after death, a study of living drivers like this one can neither diagnose it nor rule it out. A 2023 paper setting out the criteria for diagnosing CTE lists boxing, American football, rugby, ice hockey, football (soccer), wrestling and even bull riding among the sports in which CTE has been confirmed HEAD INJURY 148 after death, but not motor racing. 18 No published case of CTE in a racing driver could be found. The absence of a published case does not mean that one has never occurred. Does Diabetes Worsen Recovery from Head Injury? ““There’s an interesting study that shows that if you have head trauma… your risk of poor outcome is increased if you happen to have been a type two diabetic prior to the head trauma. The outcome of head trauma in type two diabetics is much worse. Why? Because that type two diabetes is shifting their microglia.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT In animals, diabetes worsened damage after stroke, seizures and spinal cord injury. In people with severe head injuries, two studies found different results, and one found a higher risk of death only in people whose diabetes had already caused complications. No large review or study was found showing that type 2 diabetes makes recovery from head injury “much worse” across the board. Two large studies of the same database of hospital records point in different directions. A 2025 analysis of 26,019 people with severe head injuries found that those with diabetes were less likely to die, although they had more strokes, seizures, pneumonia and serious infections.19 A 2026 analysis separated people by how advanced their diabetes was. Those whose diabetes had already caused complications were more likely to die within 90 days, 33.8% compared with 29.7% (odds ratio HEAD INJURY 149 1.21), while those without complications did about as well as people without diabetes.20 The studies found linking diabetes to changes in microglia after injury are animal and laboratory studies of spinal cord injury, stroke or seizures, not head injury.21–23 In diabetic mice with spinal cord injuries, an inflammatory pathway caused excessive death of microglia, and repair was worse. 21 In diabetic animals with strokes, diabetes made the damage to white matter worse and shifted microglia towards an inflammatory state, and in the laboratory high glucose had a similar effect on microglia.22 No study was found showing that diabetes worsens recovery from head injury in people through changes in microglia. HEAD INJURY 150 14 Mitochondria, Metformin and New Treatments Mitochondria, the energy producers inside our cells, are central to Dr Perlmutter’s account of brain health. Exercise builds healthier mitochondria in muscle. In older adults, metformin reduced some of the gains from exercise, as Steven Bartlett points out. The Cuban trial of a nasal spray for Alzheimer’s that Dr Perlmutter describes did report large benefits, and no repeat by an independent group was found. Can Mitochondria Be Transplanted? ““When mitochondrial function is restored, it helps keep our microglia in their supportive role. There are transplant techniques now for transplanting healthy mitochondria into human hearts, and very soon into human brains.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 151 IN SHORT Healthy mitochondria have been transplanted into children’s hearts in small studies. In the one with a comparison group, more treated children came off heart-lung support, but the children were not randomly assigned. Use in the brain has reached its first human safety tests, but no benefit has yet been shown in people. Mitochondrial transplantation means taking healthy mitochondria, usually taken from the patient’s own body, and delivering them to damaged tissue. It has been used in the human heart. A 2021 review found two human studies, both in children on heart-lung support after heart surgery whose heart muscle had been damaged by a loss of blood supply. Neither study had a comparison group or was blinded.1 A later study from the same hospital, Boston Children’s Hospital, looked back at 24 children treated there between 2002 and 2018 and compared 10 who received mitochondria with 14 who did not. Eight of the 10 (80%) came off heart-lung support, compared with 4 of the 14 (29%), and the treated children recovered heart function sooner. The children were not randomly assigned, and the authors described it as a pilot study.2 Use in the brain has reached its first human tests. A phase 1 trial, the earliest stage of testing in people, delivered mitochondria into the brain during a procedure to remove a blood clot in people having a stroke. No significant adverse events were observed, and safety was similar to that of matched patients who did not receive them. 3 But the trial was designed to test safety, not benefit. 4 In a rat study, fresh mitochondria raised survival three days after cardiac arrest from 55% to 91%. 5 The 2021 review concluded that clinical translation remains limited,1 and a 2026 review of their use in stroke that clinical studies have not demonstrated a therapeutic effect.6 None of these studies tested a link with microglia. MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 152 Do Exercise and Metformin Build Mitochondria? ““There are ways that we increase our mitochondria in terms of their numbers, in terms of their function, and in terms of our ability to get rid of defective mitochondria… Exercise does it, and metformin does it as well.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Exercise increases the number and quality of mitochondria in muscle. A review reports evidence that metformin helps build mitochondria, but in a trial in older adults it blocked the gains in mitochondrial function from exercise. A 2019 review describes how exercise both builds new mitochondria in muscle and clears out damaged ones, a process called mitophagy. 7 In 22 sedentary older adults, four months of supervised training raised the amount of mitochondria in their muscles to the level seen in people who had exercised all their lives, while in people who had trained all their lives the pattern was dominated by mitophagy. 8 Even two weeks of high-intensity interval training improved how well muscle mitochondria used oxygen, by 14% to 24% on different measures.9 A 2020 review of metformin and ageing notes substantial evidence that metformin improves the building of mitochondria by increasing PGC-1α, a protein that controls it, but no consensus on whether this effect is specific to certain tissues.10 In a trial in older adults, described below, metformin taken during training inhibited the increase in muscle mitochondrial function that the placebo group had.11 MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 153 Does Metformin Blunt the Benefits of Exercise? ““For generally healthy non-diabetic people, the current scientific consensus is not to take metformin… number one, it blunts the benefits of exercise… Multiple randomised controlled trials have demonstrated that metformin actively interferes with exercise’s adaptations. Number two… intensive lifestyle modification consistently outperforms metformin in reducing cardiovascular risk.” Steven Bartlett on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT In trials of older adults, metformin held back the gains in fitness, insulin sensitivity and muscle from exercise. Lifestyle change prevented more cases of diabetes than metformin, but over 21 years neither reduced heart attacks, strokes or cardiovascular deaths. Steven Bartlett was reading from an article here, in response to Dr Perlmutter, who had said a moment earlier: “What you’re not hearing from me right now is that we should all jump on board and take metformin. I prefer to exercise.” Earlier he had also said that “Metformin does many things that exercise does. Exercise does more.” Steven Bartlett’s first point applies to trials in older adults. In a trial of 12 weeks of aerobic training, metformin reduced the improvement in insulin sensitivity and in VO2max, the maximum amount of oxygen the body can use, and inhibited the increase in muscle mitochondrial function.11 In the MASTERS trial of strength training in older adults, people on placebo gained more muscle than those on metformin. The researchers concluded that there was no case for using metformin to boost the benefits of exercise in healthy older people. 12 In a 2026 trial of 91 adults at risk of metabolic syndrome, metformin attenuated the improvement in insulin sensitivity after 16 weeks of high-intensity exercise.13 MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 154 In a trial of adults with raised blood sugar, fitness and body weight improved to a similar extent with metformin and with placebo during training, but metformin plus exercise was not better than exercise alone for blood sugar after meals, which the authors interpreted as a blunting effect of metformin. 14 A 2026 meta-analysis of nine studies with 827 people found that adding metformin to exercise modestly attenuated improvements in fitness and blood pressure, while blood sugar and cholesterol measures were unchanged.15 In the Diabetes Prevention Program, intensive lifestyle change cut new cases of type 2 diabetes by 58%, and metformin by 31%, compared with placebo, so lifestyle change worked better than the drug for preventing diabetes.16 But a 2022 analysis followed the 3,234 people in the original randomised groups for a median of 21 years. Neither lifestyle change nor metformin reduced heart attacks, strokes or deaths from cardiovascular disease compared with placebo. The authors noted that all participants were later offered a lifestyle programme, and many took other heart medicines, which may have reduced the differences between the groups. 17 Even so, the trial does not show that lifestyle change outperforms metformin for cardiovascular events. Is the TAME Trial Waiting for Funding? ““There is a big study called the TAME trial. Nir Barzilai at Albert Einstein is trying to get this started, but looking for funding. It’s, I think, about a $70 million project. He can’t get funding because metformin has gone off patent… it’s looking at the intervention of taking this drug metformin to see what effect it has on longevity, and dementia for that matter.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 155 IN SHORT TAME is a well-defined planned trial of metformin in 3,000 older adults over four years, and dementia is one of its outcomes. The $70 million cost and the reason it has struggled for funding are not documented in the published research. TAME, which stands for Targeting Aging with Metformin, was set out in a 2016 paper by Dr Nir Barzilai and colleagues. Its published design described a trial of 3,000 adults aged 65 to 79 without diabetes, who would take either metformin or a placebo for four years. 18,19 Its main outcome is the time until any of several major diseases of ageing appears, including heart attack, stroke, heart failure, most cancers, mild cognitive impairment or dementia, and death. It was designed with input from the US Food and Drug Administration, with the aim of creating a route for treatments that target ageing itself and not a single disease.18 A 2016 paper by Dr Barzilai and colleagues said the trial had received planning funding from the American Federation for Aging Research and had been under review through several funding mechanisms. 19 No published paper was found giving a cost of $70 million, or showing that metformin being off patent is why funding has been difficult to obtain. Does Metformin Lower Vitamin B12? ““And I don’t think mentioned in this article was the possibility of B12 deficiency that we see in people taking metformin.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 156 IN SHORT Long-term metformin use is linked to vitamin B12 deficiency in large studies, and a 2026 meta-analysis called for routine monitoring. But a 2026 study that used a stricter definition of deficiency found no meaningful excess risk. Vitamin B12 is needed for healthy nerves and blood cells. In the Diabetes Prevention Program and its long follow-up, low B12 was more common in people taking metformin than placebo after five years, at 4.3% compared with 2.3%. After 13 years, about 20% of the metformin group had low or borderline-low levels, compared with about 16% on placebo, and each year of use was linked to higher odds of deficiency, with an odds ratio of 1.13. 20 In UK Biobank, deficiency was found in 0.84% to 1.20% of people not taking metformin, and in 6.02% to 12.84% of those taking it, depending on a gene variant that raised the risk. 21 A 2026 meta-analysis of 20 studies found deficiency in 32.1% of people with type 2 diabetes taking metformin. 22 But a 2026 Danish study of 3,958 people with diabetes, which defined deficiency using two blood markers, found it in 2.3% of metformin users and 1.3% of non-users (adjusted prevalence ratio 1.56, 95% confidence interval 0.92 to 2.63). Its authors concluded that there was “no meaningful excess risk” and that routine screening “may be redundant”.23 A 2016 paper on its use against ageing states that metformin “has been used with an excellent safety record for over 60 years”, but also notes that the risk of B12 deficiency increases with the duration of use. 19 The definitions of deficiency differ between these studies. MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 157 What Slows the Ageing Clock? ““Dr David Sinclair… recently put out a paper several months ago that looked at all these kind of cool interventions as they relate to these ageing clocks… and it ranked them. And metformin was described and rapamycin was described. What led the list?… What led the list was exercise, in terms of these ageing clocks.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT In a 2026 review, some exercise studies lowered ageing clocks, while metformin, and rapamycin combined with home-based exercise, did not. The review grouped results by whether they were significant, and these clocks are research measures based on DNA, so a change in them does not show that a treatment extends life or prevents dementia. Epigenetic ageing clocks estimate a person’s biological age from chemical marks on their DNA. A 2026 review by Johnson and Sinclair of 41 human studies using the newest clocks concluded that a diverse range of interventions can decrease epigenetic age, listing exercise first, followed by a plant-rich diet, the weight-loss drug semaglutide, calorie restriction and others, including omega-3 fats and a multivitamin supplement. But the largest category was effects that were not significant, which included 18 studies and interventions, among them metformin, and rapamycin combined with home-based exercise.24 The metformin result came from a trial in 40 people with HIV but without diabetes, in which none of nine epigenetic clocks differed significantly between metformin and placebo after 96 weeks, although all the estimates favoured metformin. 25 A 2022 review states that diet and exercise are still the most accepted ways to intervene in ageing, MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 158 and that the “exciting effects” of rapamycin in preclinical studies have not been seen in clinical trials.26 Can a Nasal Spray Improve Alzheimer’s? ““I just saw a powerful study come out of Havana, Cuba, of all places, giving an intranasal treatment of something called erythropoietin, which is something made by the kidneys, that had effects in existing Alzheimer’s patients that were mind-blowing… patients who… did decline in the placebo group by four points on a standard scale that we use, improved four points. So they didn’t just stabilise, they got better.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT A single Cuban trial of a nasal spray form of erythropoietin did report large improvements in thinking over 48 weeks in people with mild to moderate Alzheimer’s. No repeat by an independent group was found. Erythropoietin, or EPO, is a hormone made by the kidneys that stimulates the production of red blood cells. The ATHENEA trial tested a nasal spray version, called NeuroEPO, in 174 adults aged 50 and over, 170 of whom were treated, with mild to moderate Alzheimer’s. It was double-blind and placebo-controlled, and participants used the spray three times a week for 48 weeks.27 On ADAS-Cog, a standard test of thinking on which lower scores are better, the placebo group worsened by a median of 4 points, while the two NeuroEPO groups improved by 3 and 4 points. These differences from placebo were statistically significant. 27 A 2026 review described ATHENEA as a rigorous trial showing a significant improvement in thinking.28 MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 159 A follow-up by the same researchers, to 96 weeks, reported that people who kept using the spray continued to do better than those who never had it, but that study was observational, not randomised. 29 The researchers concluded that larger trials are warranted, 27 and the 2026 review that larger trials across several centres are required.28 MITOCHONDRIA, METFORMIN AND NEW TREATMENTS 160 15 Lifestyle Change and the Ketogenic Diet Toward the end of the conversation, Dr Perlmutter brings his pillars together. The diet in the Ornish and Tanzi trial was low-fat and vegan, not Mediterranean. He makes the case for the ketogenic diet as a way to give an energy-starved brain another fuel. Small trials in memory problems, Alzheimer’s and Parkinson’s show some benefits for daily life, but none shows that the diet slows the disease. 161 Can Lifestyle Change Improve Early Alzheimer’s? ““A relatively small study from Harvard University… by Rudolph Tanzi and Dean Ornish, where they took a group of 51 individuals who have Alzheimer’s disease, or mild cognitive impairment… They increased their exercise, they changed their diets, they reduced their stress, and they had them meditate for an hour a day. So these people didn’t just slow their decline in the relatively short intervention of 20 weeks, but 70% of them either stabilised or improved.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT This small, 20-week randomised trial found that intensive lifestyle change improved several measures in people with early Alzheimer’s. On a doctor-rated measure of overall change, 10 of 24 people in the lifestyle group improved and 7 stayed the same, about 71% in all. Published in 2024 by Dean Ornish and colleagues, it was a randomised trial of 51 adults aged 45 to 90 with mild cognitive impairment or early dementia due to Alzheimer’s. Half followed an intensive programme of plant-based eating, moderate exercise, stress management and support groups, and half received usual care. The authors described it as the first randomised trial to test whether lifestyle change alone could slow, stop or reverse the disease.1 After 20 weeks, the lifestyle group did significantly better than the usual-care group on three of the four main measures of thinking and daily function, with the fourth narrowly missing significance. A blood marker of amyloid improved in the lifestyle group and worsened in the others, and their gut bacteria changed. On a measure of overall change judged by doctors, 10 of 24 people in the lifestyle group LIFESTYLE CHANGE AND THE KETOGENIC DIET 162 improved and 7 stayed the same, about 71% in all. In the usual-care group, none improved and 8 of 25 stayed the same.1 It was carried out at several centres, not as a single Harvard study. 1 A 2025 review noted that it did not meet its original target of 100 participants, and that its main results were analysed with one-tailed statistical tests. 2 The trial lasted 20 weeks and did not test long-term prevention. What Diet Did the Trial Use? ““[What did they do to their diet?] They put them on a plant-forward, more Mediterranean-type diet, a diet rich in the things that we just looked at, rich in polyphenols, rich in colour. Welcome back to the table, fat, but the good fats. And they encouraged social interaction. These people improved.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT The paper describes a low-fat vegan diet, with 14% to 18% of calories from fat. The published paper described a whole-foods, minimally processed vegan diet, especially low in harmful fats, sweeteners and refined carbohydrates. Only about 14% to 18% of calories came from fat, with 63% to 68% from mostly complex carbohydrates.1 The researchers supplied 21 meals a week, plus snacks and supplements, delivered twice a week, and participants were asked to eat only the food provided.
