The supplements included omega-3 fats, curcumin, vitamins and minerals. 1 No targets for polyphenols were LIFESTYLE CHANGE AND THE KETOGENIC DIET 163 found in the paper, so the claim that the diet was “rich in polyphenols” could not be checked. The programme included support groups.1 Do Ketones Calm Microglia? ““When you’re talking about a ketogenic diet, you’re targeting… the mitochondria. With a ketogenic diet along with exercise, we’re targeting mitochondrial function. And remember that that is the pivot point for determining supportive versus destructive microglia.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Ketones calm inflammatory microglia and protect mitochondria in laboratory and animal studies. But no trial was found testing this in people with Alzheimer’s. A ketogenic diet is very low in carbohydrates and high in fat. On it, the liver makes ketones, which the body and brain can use as fuel in place of glucose. The main ketone is beta-hydroxybutyrate (BHB). In a study in microglia grown in the laboratory, BHB shifted the cells towards a calmer, anti-inflammatory state. 3 A 2023 review describes evidence, mostly from cell and animal studies, that ketones can dampen an important inflammatory switch inside cells, called the NLRP3 inflammasome.4 In mice with strokes, a ketogenic diet increased tolerance to the injury and reduced an inflammatory pathway in the brain, and in nerve cells in the laboratory BHB protected mitochondria from breaking apart. 5 In a mouse model of multiple sclerosis, the diet reduced inflammation in microglia and increased markers of the clearance of damaged mitochondria.6 LIFESTYLE CHANGE AND THE KETOGENIC DIET 164 No study was found showing that a ketogenic diet and exercise work through a single “pivot point” that sets the state of microglia in people with Alzheimer’s. Do Ketogenic Diets Help Memory Loss, Alzheimer’s and Parkinson’s? ““In clinical trials on mild cognitive impairment and early Alzheimer’s, a keto diet or ketone supplementation… rescues roughly 10 to 18% of the brain’s total energy supply, directly closing up to 80% of the fuel gap left by impaired glucose metabolism. This energetic boost translates to up to 15% improvement in standard memory and test scores, alongside up to a 20% gain in daily functioning.” Steven Bartlett on The Diary of a CEO ““And also in Parkinson’s. There is a researcher in New Zealand named Matthew Phillips who has published papers on each of those, and I wrote a book chapter with him on the use of the ketogenic diet in Parkinson’s.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Small trials show that ketone supplements increase the brain’s use of ketones, and a ketogenic diet improved daily function in Alzheimer’s and some symptoms of Parkinson’s. Meta-analyses of trials find gains in thinking, but the percentages Steven Bartlett reads out could not be confirmed. In mild cognitive impairment, a trial of a daily drink of a special fat that the body turns into ketones found that the brain’s use of ketones rose LIFESTYLE CHANGE AND THE KETOGENIC DIET 165 by 230% over six months, while its use of glucose did not change. 7 The same trial reported improvements in memory, language and other thinking skills, and a companion report found benefits over placebo on several tests.7,8 A 2020 systematic review of trials described ketogenic therapy as promising for thinking in people with Alzheimer’s or mild cognitive impairment, but noted that the research is at an early stage and the trials vary a great deal.9 In Alzheimer’s, Dr Phillips ran a trial in which 26 patients spent 12 weeks on a modified ketogenic diet and 12 weeks on their usual diet, with healthy-eating advice, in random order. On the ketogenic diet, they reached steady ketosis, and their daily function and quality of life improved significantly. Their scores on a test of thinking also rose, but not by enough to rule out chance.10 In Parkinson’s, a 2018 trial by Dr Phillips randomised 47 patients to a low-fat or ketogenic diet for eight weeks. Both groups improved, but the ketogenic group improved more on the symptoms of Parkinson’s that do not involve movement, such as urinary problems, pain, tiredness, daytime sleepiness and thinking: by 41%, compared with 11% in the low-fat group. There was no difference in movement symptoms.11 A 2026 meta-analysis of 12 randomised trials with 707 older adults with mild cognitive impairment or Alzheimer’s found improvements in overall thinking, with an effect size of 0.34, and in memory and language, but not in attention or executive function. It also found rises in total and LDL cholesterol. 12 A 2024 meta-analysis of ten trials with 691 people with Alzheimer’s found improvements on two standard tests of thinking, MMSE and ADAS-Cog, and rises in triglycerides and LDL cholesterol. 13 The trials in the 2024 meta-analysis lasted 3 to 15 months, and the trials in both meta-analyses used different diets and supplements.12,13 For ketone supplements alone, a 2026 meta-analysis of 29 trial protocols with 1,117 people found a statistically significant improvement in thinking compared with placebo (effect size 0.29), LIFESTYLE CHANGE AND THE KETOGENIC DIET 166 which the authors described as modest, with no significant difference between healthy people and people with Alzheimer’s.14 The specific figures Steven Bartlett quotes, such as rescuing 10% to 18% of the brain’s energy supply or closing 80% of the fuel gap, do not appear in these trials and could not be confirmed. The 2020 review described the research as still at an early stage and highly varied. 9 No trial was found showing that the diet slows the underlying disease. Is the Brain Resistant to Insulin in Alzheimer’s? ““The brain requires glucose. And when we become insulin resistant, certain parts of the brain suffer. Not all of the brain cells require insulin, but some parts do: the hippocampus… the hypothalamus… the prefrontal cortex… and finally, an area called the olfactory bulb… everything I just mentioned are issues in Alzheimer’s… this is an upstream energy deficiency… You can bypass that with the ketogenic diet.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Insulin receptors are plentiful in memory areas of the brain, and insulin signalling is impaired in Alzheimer’s. But neurons take up glucose mostly independently of insulin. A 2023 review notes a high density of insulin receptors in the hippocampus, which suggests roles for insulin in memory, and that changes in the regulation of insulin pathways have been observed in the brains of people with Alzheimer’s. 15 A study of brain tissue after death found greatly reduced insulin signalling in the hippocampus of people with Alzheimer’s who did not have diabetes. Markers of insulin resistance rose from people with normal thinking to those with mild LIFESTYLE CHANGE AND THE KETOGENIC DIET 167 cognitive impairment to those with Alzheimer’s, and were linked to poorer memory.16 The same review notes that neurons take up glucose mostly independently of insulin, although insulin can influence this uptake, mostly when energy demand is highest. 15 Another review notes that GLUT1 and GLUT3 are the main glucose transporters in the brain, and that the insulin-sensitive transporter GLUT4 has low activity there.17 In the SNIFF trial, 289 people with mild cognitive impairment or early Alzheimer’s were given insulin through the nose, or a placebo, for 12 months. In the main analysis of 240 people, there was no benefit on thinking, daily function or markers in the spinal fluid. The first 49 participants were left out of that analysis because the device used to deliver their treatment proved unreliable. 18 A 2025 meta-analysis of five trials with 540 people with mild cognitive impairment or Alzheimer’s found no significant differences between insulin through the nose and placebo in thinking, daily function or markers in the spinal fluid.19 How Quickly Does the Body Switch to Burning Fat? ““What you want to try to establish is flexibility, such that when you do decide you’re going to fast for a day or two… you’re able to jump into ketosis relatively quickly, as opposed to having to adapt to a ketogenic state, which may take, you know, a couple of weeks.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Steady ketosis is achievable, and studies in athletes show that the body shifts towards burning fat within days to a few weeks. LIFESTYLE CHANGE AND THE KETOGENIC DIET 168 On a ketogenic diet, the level of BHB in the blood exceeds 0.5 to 0.6 mmol/L. In the Alzheimer’s trial described above, participants achieved sustained ketosis, with an average of 0.95 mmol/L over 12 weeks.10 Studies of how quickly the body adapts have been done in athletes. In elite race walkers, five to six days on a ketogenic diet increased how much fat they burned during exercise by more than 200%, reaching levels previously reported only after three to four weeks. 20 A review concluded that large increases in fat burning occur within three to four weeks, and possibly within five to ten days.21 A 2026 meta- analysis of 33 studies in trained athletes on low-carbohydrate or ketogenic diets found that fat burning increased in all 30 studies that measured it. Performance was impaired in studies that measured it within the first week, but maintained or improved in those that measured it after more than a week.22 Dr Perlmutter was not suggesting a strict ketogenic diet at all times. As he put it, “I’m not saying one needs to do that at all times.” These studies were in trained athletes and measured fat burning during exercise, not fuel use in the brain. For the brain, a 2017 scan study of 10 healthy adults found that after four days on a ketogenic diet, the brain’s uptake of ketones rose sixfold, and ketones supplied an estimated third of its energy.23 LIFESTYLE CHANGE AND THE KETOGENIC DIET 169 16 Genes and Supplements The conversation ends on genes and on what Dr Perlmutter takes himself, including methylated B vitamins and fish oil. His figures for the MTHFR gene variant and for the risk carried by APOE4 are higher than most published estimates, and trials of DHA supplements have mostly found no benefit for thinking. One copy of APOE4 raises risk, and a 2024 study proposed that two copies represent a distinct genetic form of Alzheimer’s. Do a Quarter of People Need Methylated B Vitamins? ““I have a little bit of a genetic quirk that goes by the name of MTHFR. And what that means is I don’t handle B vitamins as well as I should… my homocysteine level was elevated. Homocysteine itself is an amino acid that is a significant risk factor for heart disease and Alzheimer’s… I have to take a different type of B vitamin called methylated B vitamins… for me and for probably about… 25% of the global population.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO 170 IN SHORT About a quarter of copies of the MTHFR gene worldwide carry the common variant, but only about 8% of people have two copies. High homocysteine is a risk factor for dementia, but no study was found showing that methylated B vitamins lower homocysteine more than folic acid in people with these variants, and a review found that ordinary folic acid corrects the higher homocysteine seen in people with two copies, although a 2026 study found that they less often reached their homocysteine target on folic acid. MTHFR is an enzyme that helps the body process folate, a B vitamin, and a common variant of its gene makes it work less well. A 2016 meta-analysis estimated that about 24% of copies of the gene worldwide carry the variant, but everyone has two copies, and only about 7.7% of people carry two variant copies. 1 The figures also vary widely by ancestry, from about 10% of gene copies in Africans to 34% in Europeans.1 An international expert statement concluded that raised homocysteine is a risk factor for decline in thinking, dementia and Alzheimer’s that can be changed. 2 A 2021 meta-analysis found that each 5 µmol/L rise in homocysteine was linked to a 9% higher risk of dementia.3 For heart disease, a trial in people with vascular disease found that folic acid with vitamins B6 and B12 did not reduce major cardiovascular events. 4 A 2017 Cochrane review and meta-analysis of 15 trials with 71,422 people found no differences in heart attacks or deaths from any cause, and a reduction in strokes (risk ratio 0.90).5 Trials of B vitamins for the brain have given mixed results. In the VITACOG trial, 271 people over 70 with mild cognitive impairment took high doses of folic acid, vitamin B6 and vitamin B12, or a placebo, for two years. In the 168 who completed the brain scans, the brain shrank by 0.76% a year in the treated group and 1.08% a year on placebo, and shrinkage was 53% lower in people whose homocysteine started above 13 µmol/L. 6 But a 2014 meta-analysis of 11 trials with 22,000 people GENES AND SUPPLEMENTS 171 found that B vitamins lowered homocysteine by about a quarter and had no significant effect on any area of thinking, on overall thinking or on cognitive ageing.7 Methylated B vitamins do lower homocysteine. In a 2024 trial of 54 people with these gene variants and raised homocysteine, a combination of methylated B vitamins lowered it by 30.0% compared with a placebo, and by 48.3% in people with two copies of a variant. 8 That trial did not compare them with ordinary folic acid. In a 2023 trial in 272 adults, the fall in homocysteine with methylfolate was not substantially different from that with another form of folate, folinic acid, and people with one copy of the MTHFR variant had a larger fall with folinic acid. 9 Studies that compared the two directly by genotype found that methylated folate can raise blood folate faster or higher than folic acid, but this was also true in people without the variant. 10,11 In two trials that compared them by genotype, methylated folate did not lower homocysteine more than folic acid. In one, women with two copies of the variant benefited most from either, and in women with one or no copies folic acid was more effective. 12,13 A small study of 20 people found that low doses of methylated folate lowered homocysteine in people with and without two copies of the variant. 14 In a randomised trial of 220 women with recurrent miscarriage, there was no significant difference in miscarriage rate between methylated folate and folic acid, and the authors concluded that the results do not support any benefit of methylated folate in women with any of the MTHFR variants studied.15 A smaller, non-randomised study of 100 women with MTHFR variants and recurrent miscarriage reported more full-term pregnancies with methylated folate than with high- dose folic acid, 60% compared with 22%, but the groups were not randomly assigned and received different doses. 16 The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention states that people with common MTHFR variants can process folic acid normally and should not avoid it because of their genes.17 GENES AND SUPPLEMENTS 172 People with two copies of the variant, about 7.7% of people worldwide,1 have higher homocysteine than people with one or no copies.18 A review found that they also have lower folate in their blood, and that giving ordinary folic acid removed these differences. 19 A 2026 study of 344 patients with raised homocysteine in China, treated with folic acid for a year, found that those with two copies were less likely to reach their homocysteine target than those with none (odds ratio 0.080). The study looked back at existing records and did not compare folic acid with methylated forms. 20 No study was found showing that people with MTHFR variants need methylated B vitamins instead of folic acid. Does DHA Protect the Brain? ““I take DHA from fish oil, omega-3, but we want to look at the DHA content… So the fish oil that I take, I’m getting about 1,000mg a day of DHA.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Eating more omega-3 fats is linked to a lower risk of dementia in observational studies. But trials of omega-3 supplements, including DHA, mostly show small or no benefits for thinking, and little or none in people who already have Alzheimer’s. DHA is an omega-3 fat found in oily fish and is a major building block of the brain. A 2023 meta-analysis of 48 studies following people over time, with more than 100,000 people, found that, on average, a higher intake of omega-3 fats was linked to about a 20% lower risk of dementia or decline in thinking. Each extra 0.1 grams a day of DHA or GENES AND SUPPLEMENTS 173 EPA, another omega-3 fat, was linked to an 8% to 9.9% lower risk of decline in thinking.21 A 2023 review of 78 trials found that 43.6% reported a benefit for thinking, and 66.7% of the trials in adults with mild cognitive impairment did so.22 A 2024 systematic review of 24 trials, each with at least 100 adults, found that omega-3 supplements improved thinking in 5 of them, that all four trials in people with Alzheimer’s were null, and that most trials in people with normal thinking or mild cognitive impairment were null or not powered to detect small effects.23 In people without dementia, a 2025 overview of reviews, with a meta- analysis of 14 trials, found a statistically significant but modest improvement on a standard test of thinking (effect size 0.16).24 In people who already have Alzheimer’s, a 2022 review found no benefit from DHA, 25 and a 2025 meta-analysis of four trials with 702 patients found no significant effect on a standard test of thinking. 26 A 2024 meta-analysis found slower decline on one rating scale, the CDR, but no significant differences on four other measures of thinking and daily function.27 Higher doses of DHA do reach the brain. In a 2020 trial, DHA supplements raised DHA in the spinal fluid by 28% compared with placebo, but cognitive scores did not differ between the groups.28 In a 2026 trial, DHA raised a spinal fluid measure of DHA at six months, but there were no differences in thinking or brain structure over 24 months.29 A 2026 study of people in the Alzheimer’s Disease Neuroimaging Initiative found that those taking omega-3 supplements declined faster on several tests of thinking. It was an observational study, so it cannot show that the supplements caused the decline.30 No trial was found showing that taking around 1,000 mg of DHA a day protects thinking or prevents dementia. GENES AND SUPPLEMENTS 174 How Much Does APOE4 Raise Risk? ““Carrying more of the APOE2s is associated with a pretty dramatic risk reduction. APOE3 is neutral, but carrying the APOE4 allele, like about 25% of people, is associated with an increased risk of Alzheimer’s… if you carry one, by about fivefold. 500%, fivefold… And if you carry two, in other words, you’re APOE4/4, the risk may be as high as a 12-fold increase.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT APOE4 raises Alzheimer’s risk, and two copies raise it more than one, while APOE2 is often protective. But the size of the effect varies by ancestry, and one copy usually raises the odds about two to three and a half times, not five times. APOE is a gene that comes in three common forms, called E2, E3 and E4, and everyone carries two copies. A 2021 pooled analysis of 389,000 people without dementia found that 23.9% carried at least one copy of E4.31 In a 2023 study of 68,756 people from different backgrounds, one copy of E4 was linked to higher odds of Alzheimer’s, with odds ratios ranging from 1.90 in Hispanic people to 4.54 in East Asian people, and 3.46 in white people. 32 Meta-analyses of studies in Italy, Latin America and China found odds ratios of 5.48 to 7.08 for two copies.33–35 E2 was linked to lower odds in white people (odds ratio 0.53) and Black people (0.69), but not in East Asian or Hispanic people. 32 Two meta- analyses found lower odds for E3, 33,35 and a third noted that such results may reflect differences in how cases and controls were selected.34 A 1997 meta-analysis of 5,930 people with Alzheimer’s and 8,607 without found odds ratios of 3.2 for one copy and 14.9 for two in white GENES AND SUPPLEMENTS 175 people from clinic or brain bank studies, with a weaker association in African American and Hispanic people. 36 The fivefold figure for one copy is higher than the odds ratios in these studies, and the twelvefold figure for two lies between the meta-analyses above and the 1997 estimate. These figures describe groups of people, not what will happen to any one person. Does APOE4 Decide Your Fate? ““Early in the day, we as neurologists were told, well, when we know the APOE status of our patients, if they carry APOE4, we shouldn’t tell them… If you carry an APOE4 allele, or two, you are not necessarily destined to get Alzheimer’s… Your lifestyle issues can offset this issue of a genetic predisposition. It’s a genetic predisposition, I won’t deny that. It is not a genetic determinant.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT Guidelines did discourage routine APOE testing. One copy of APOE4 raises risk, and almost everyone with two copies develops the brain changes of Alzheimer’s. A healthy lifestyle is linked to lower risk even in people at high genetic risk, but no study was found showing that it offsets APOE4. Practice guidelines have recommended against routine APOE testing.37 A 2026 paper explains that this was based on concerns by doctors, geneticists, policy makers and patients about the value of knowing the risk when there is no treatment for Alzheimer’s dementia.38 The new anti-amyloid drugs give the gene a further importance. A 2023 review of these drugs reports that in the main lecanemab trial, brain bleeds occurred in 39% and brain swelling in GENES AND SUPPLEMENTS 176 32.6% of people with two copies of APOE4, compared with 17.3% and 12.6% of everyone on the drug.39 APOE4 is the strongest known genetic risk factor for Alzheimer’s. 40 A 2024 study, which examined 3,297 people after death and 10,039 during life, found that almost all of them had the brain changes of Alzheimer’s when examined after death, and by 65 nearly all had abnormal amyloid in their spinal fluid. Their symptoms began at about 65 on average, within a narrow range of ages. The authors proposed that carrying two copies should be seen as a genetic form of Alzheimer’s, similar to the rare inherited forms. 41 In one disclosure study, 46 of 50 carriers (92%) understood that they were at increased risk.37 In the FINGER trial in Finland, a programme combining diet, exercise, brain training and management of heart risk improved thinking in older adults at risk. The estimated benefit was larger in E4 carriers, but the difference between carriers and non-carriers was not statistically significant. 42 A 2024 analysis of brain scans in the same trial suggested that the programme may not directly affect amyloid build-up linked to APOE4, but may partly counteract the effects of other genetic risk. 40 In a UK Biobank study of 196,383 older adults, a healthy lifestyle was linked to a lower risk of dementia in people at high genetic risk, with a hazard ratio of 0.68. 43 A ratio below 1 means a lower risk. But that study used a score combining many genes, not APOE4 alone, and it was observational.43 Learning one’s status has had mixed effects on behaviour. In one trial, people who learned they carried E4 were more likely to report changing their health habits a year later (odds ratio 2.73), but this was self-reported,44 and another study found that disclosure led E4 carriers to change their plans for the future, but not their protective health behaviours.45 GENES AND SUPPLEMENTS 177 How Many Genes Are Linked to Alzheimer’s? ““And to be sure, there are 80 other genetic issues that relate to Alzheimer’s risk. But again, genetics loads the gun, lifestyle pulls the trigger.” Dr David Perlmutter on The Diary of a CEO IN SHORT A 2022 study identified 75 regions of the genome linked to Alzheimer’s. A study published in October 2026 identified 127 regions of the genome linked to Alzheimer’s. Studies that scan the whole genome look for regions, called loci, where small differences are more common in people with a disease. In 2022, a study of more than 111,000 people with Alzheimer’s or a family history of it, and almost 678,000 without, identified 75 such regions. A risk score built from them separated people at higher and lower risk, beyond the effects of age and APOE4.46 The count has since risen. A study of people from many backgrounds, published in Nature in October 2026, identified 118 regions in its main analysis and nine more in analyses of specific ancestries, 127 in total, 48 of them new.47 The 2022 study included people with a family history of Alzheimer’s in place of confirmed cases.46 GENES AND SUPPLEMENTS 178 References The studies, reviews and reports behind each section, listed under the chapter and section they support. 01 The State of Alzheimer’s Can Alzheimer’s Be Prevented? 1.
